#5198. Bitcoin crashes to $59,000 as Anthropic falls 25% from yesterday’s IPO-day high

Bitcoin slid to its lowest price since July 1, while the Nasdaq Composite fell nearly 3% in Friday morning trading.

Bitcoin fell below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since July as US technology stocks slid and Anthropic shares fell about 25% from their IPO-day high.

Bitcoin traded at $59,470, down 5.66% over the previous 24 hours, according to CryptoSlate’s Bitcoin price data.

Anthropic’s Nasdaq debut Thursday drew strong demand for the maker of Claude. Shares opened at $111.50, 34% above the $83 offer price, and climbed to $115.10 before closing at $97.17, still 17% above the offer. Anthropic said Friday the IPO totaled $93.5 billion in gross proceeds after underwriters exercised their option to buy more shares.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The listing gave stock investors a direct stake in a leading AI lab, making Anthropic’s shares a gauge of investor appetite for AI stocks. By Friday afternoon UTC, the stock was down 11% on the day at $86.48, about 25% below its debut high and just 4% above the IPO price.

The Nasdaq Composite was down 2.85% after losing 2.25% Thursday. Nvidia shares were down 6.3%. The S&P 500 fell 2.02% Thursday, its sixth straight decline.

The sell-off followed the Wednesday release of Federal Reserve minutes showing almost all policymakers expected further rate increases would likely be appropriate under their baseline outlooks. The Fed held its benchmark rate at 3.75% to 4% in October.

#5196. Hill, Moreno blame delays for missed Clarity Act window but differ on reach of agency rulemaking

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Moreno says agencies can deliver 80% of a crypto framework, while Hill warns rulemaking cannot replace lasting legislation.

Rep. French Hill and Sen. Bernie Moreno said Congress missed a chance to pass the Clarity Act while momentum from the GENIUS Act was strong, but offered different assessments of how much regulators can accomplish while market structure legislation remains stalled.

Speaking in separate interviews on Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum, Hill argued that agency action cannot provide the permanent legal certainty that legislation would. Moreno said regulators could deliver much of the framework themselves, though he acknowledged legislation is the ideal route.

"I’m a little bit more bullish than French," Moreno said. "I think it can accomplish 80% through a very rigorous rulemaking process that can’t be unwound."

The Ohio senator added that the Securities and Exchange Commission and Commodity Futures Trading Commission should use the next two years to establish rules that would make it difficult for a future administration to undo.

"Is that the right way to do it? No," he added. "I think the right way to do it is Congress should pass the Clarity Act."

Hill, the Arkansas Republican who also chairs the House Financial Services Committee, said since the Clarity Act's failure, regulators have mostly followed in the footsteps and goals of market structure proposals. But he added that their authority is limited.

"My argument is it’s not a permanent fix. It is subject to litigation. It does not have the force of law," Hill said.

Both Hill and Moreno said it was a mistake to advance the GENIUS Act separately from Clarity, which passed the House but stalled out after a failed Senate vote in September.

"I urged the Senate and the administration last summer to have Clarity and GENIUS passed together in the House," Hill said. "And I still stand by that."

Moreno, a former car dealer, gave a sales analogy for the breakdown.

"Time kills all deals," he said, adding that lawmakers failed to capitalize on momentum from the stablecoin legislation.

"The bill started at 300 pages, ended up at 600 pages," Moreno said.

Neither had much hope for passage during the lame-duck session. Asked what was realistically possible to achieve over the coming weeks, Moreno answered: "Zero. Zero."

Hill was slightly more optimistic, saying lawmakers "never give up hope in a lame duck," though "the chances are not high."

Hill said it was a good sign that Rep. Maxine Waters pointed out digital asset market structure as an issue she intended to address if she's elected as committee chair.

Moreno also said bipartisan negotiations were not off the table, while blaming earlier talks for being "tornadoed" in disputes over President Donald Trump and ethics.

He said he would consider restarting negotiations with the House-passed legislation.

"By the time we got to September with the Senate version, it was just very, very, very complicated," Moreno said.

Hill argued that Congress still needs to finish the job: "To have GENIUS but not market structure is a huge mistake."

#5194. Bitcoin faces a new inflation threat as a regional US factory price hits a 4 year high

An October PCE reading above expectations could lift expected Fed rates and raise costs on benchmark-linked dollar loans. A separate November survey found more factories raising prices.

Nearly half of manufacturers surveyed by the Philadelphia Fed said they raised selling prices in November, while about as many reported falling new orders as rising ones. The US inflation report due Nov. 25 could shift expectations for the Fed’s December decision and affect the cost of benchmark-linked dollar loans used to finance Bitcoin positions.

The Philadelphia Fed reported on Nov. 19 that the selling-price index rose to 47 from 40.1 in October, its highest since June 2022. Some 48.1% of respondents said they raised prices for their own goods, 1.1% lowered them and 50.8% reported no change.

The new-orders index fell to −0.4 from 4.3, its fourth straight decline. Roughly 32% of firms reported more orders and a similar share reported fewer. Shipments held up better, with that index rising to 25.7 from 15.3.

These regional diffusion indexes subtract the share of firms reporting a decrease from the share reporting an increase. The readings measure how widely prices and orders changed among the factories surveyed. An index of 47.0 is the gap between the shares reporting higher and lower selling prices, measured in percentage points.

Philadelphia Fed manufacturing survey, October to November 2026

Price increases spread even as reports of rising and falling new orders nearly balanced out.

The survey covers regional manufacturers. Indexes describe the breadth of change across respondents. Respondent shares do not measure changes in dollar prices or total orders.

Dots mark the two reported monthly index readings on a common linear scale. The connecting lines compare endpoints. The strip partitions the reported November price responses in proportion to each share. The index subtracts the share reporting declines from the share reporting increases.

The survey covers regional manufacturers. Indexes describe the breadth of change across respondents. Respondent shares do not measure changes in dollar prices or total orders.

The Bureau of Economic Analysis scheduled its October Personal Income and Outlays report for 8:30 a.m. ET on Nov. 25. It includes the personal consumption expenditures price index, a national measure of consumer prices for the month before the regional factory survey. Revised third-quarter GDP is due at the same time, so interest rates could respond to both releases.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The minutes of the Fed’s October meeting show why the inflation reading matters. The Fed held its target rate at 3.75% to 4%, and almost all participants judged that more increases would likely be appropriate under their baseline outlooks. In a discussion about inflation risks that included the AI investment boom, a majority said higher costs could feed through to consumer and business prices more widely and persistently.

The Fed’s policy rate influences other interest rates and borrowing costs. If October inflation comes in higher than expected and markets price in higher Fed rates, traders whose dollar loans reset against short-term benchmarks could face more expensive Bitcoin positions.

A cooler inflation reading could pull rate expectations lower heading into the Fed’s Dec. 8–9 meeting.

#5193. Tokenized Stocks Shrug Off the Crypto Selloff

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Sign up to get 2 days early access to each edition of Tokenization Briefing

Bitcoin fell as low as $62k late Saturday, its sixth straight weekly decline. Meanwhile, tokenized stocks crossed $4 billion in onchain value for the first time on Nov 11. Tokenized stocks are now roughly five times their value on January 1.

Tokenized stocks track equity prices, not crypto, so a falling BTC doesn’t directly pull their prices down. What stands out, however, is that crypto users appear to keep buying them through the selloff. Demand for 24/7 trading and onchain access to US equities appears to have found strong product-market fit as they are holding up even as the rest of the crypto market weakens.

In September, rising share prices drove most of the growth in value of tokenized stocks. Since mid-October, new minting has driven the growth in value even as share prices dropped. Over the eleven weeks to Nov 13, minting added $1.12 billion while price moves took away $339 million. Binance’s bStocks accounted for about four fifths of that minting, led by NVIDIA, SpaceX and Circle.

Market value has stalled over the past two weeks. We expect minting to pick back up again with the launch of OKXICE on Nov 23 opening large-cap tokenized stocks to US retail.

Securitize reported $11.5 million in tokenization revenue in Q3, its highest on record and 28% more than Q3 2025. Securitize's average tokenized assets reached a record $5 billion as of Q3 as BlackRock, Ark Invest and Neuberger brought new products onchain.

Tokenization revenue is more variable because it rises and falls with issuance, while asset servicing has settled around $7 million a quarter. Securitize's acquisition of River One could help smooth out those revenue swings and diversify its top line. Lending against tokenized assets adds recurring income on top of issuance. We expect credit to show up as Securitize's third revenue line within a year of the deal closing.

Most of the roughly $4 billion of tokenized stock sits in wrappers that US investors can't buy. Only about 10% is open to American investors, and almost all of that is crypto companies including Securitize, Figure, Exodus and Forward Industries. NVIDIA, SpaceX and the other large names are effectively offshore only.

That could change on Nov 23, when OKXICE opens to US customers who have onboarded through its CeDeFi Wallet. It’s the first venue built for American retail at scale, so the onshore share should grow post-launch. We expect issuer-backed listings to drive it, with 67 companies having opted out.

#5191. Movie2K Defendant Could Hand Over 40,000 Bitcoin to End Trial—and Stay a Billionaire

Movie2K Defendant Could Hand Over 40,000 Bitcoin to End Trial—and Stay a Billionaire

The alleged operator of defunct movie piracy site Movie2K could walk away from his trial in Germany and remain a billionaire, under a deal offered by the judge in the case.

The presiding judge at the Leipzig Regional Court has offered to drop tax evasion and money laundering charges against the 43-year-old main defendant if he hands over 40,000 BTC, public broadcaster MDR reported last week. He would also have to pay €38 million ($42 million) in compensation for potential victims, according to MDR.

Saxony's Office of the Public Prosecutor General confirmed the offer to news agency dpa, tech outlet heise reported on Tuesday. The 40,000 BTC would be worth around €2.36 billion ($2.6 billion) at current prices, according to heise.

But prosecutors believe the defendant holds a total of about 56,000 BTC. If he accepts, he could keep around 16,000 BTC, worth roughly €940 million ($1 billion) today and more than €1 billion when the offer was made.

A co-defendant has been offered a deal to hand over 600 BTC and pay €17 million ($19 million), and his lawyer said she could accept it, according to MDR. The main defendant's lawyers are considering the offer. The trial resumes on December 3 and could end before Christmas.

Movie2K offered hundreds of thousands of pirated films before shutting down in 2013. Its operators made money from advertising and subscription traps, and invested much of it in Bitcoin when it traded far below today's prices.

The copyright violations are time-barred, so the trial, which began in February, has focused on money laundering and tax evasion. The judge has reportedly expressed doubts about proving some of the charges, in part because legally and illegally acquired Bitcoin may have been mixed in the defendant's accounts, heise reported.

The main defendant was arrested abroad in 2023, and in early 2024 handed over nearly 50,000 BTC. Authorities in Saxony sold the coins later that year, raising about €2.64 billion ($2.9 billion), which is held in a state judicial treasury account. The money still belongs to the defendant until there is a final ruling, according to heise. Germany's government was left holding less than $300 million in Bitcoin after the sales.

Blockchain analytics firm Arkham said last year that German authorities had failed to seize some 45,000 BTC linked to the site.

The offer comes days after German lawmakers approved new powers allowing customs officials to seize assets, including crypto, of doubtful origin without first opening a criminal case. Under that law, owners of significant assets would have to show they were lawfully obtained.

#5189. CFTC Chair Selig outlines how Hyperliquid and other onchain venues could enter the US

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Onchain venues looking to offer derivatives to U.S. retail traders would need to register as an exchange or partner with a registered one, Selig said.

Commodity Futures Trading Commission Chairman Michael Selig said crypto onchain platforms looking to offer derivatives to U.S. retail traders can register as an exchange or partner with one that is already registered.

Selig was asked how a decentralized platform like Hyperliquid could enter the U.S. during an interview Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum. He pointed to Backpack, which earlier this week received three licenses for its U.S. entry, as an example of the first route. Other platforms are pursuing partnerships with registered firms, Selig said.

"You're going to have to come in and partner with an exchange or obtain your own exchange, which doesn't change the ability and the great features of crypto and blockchain," Selig said.

Backpack's three licenses include Futures Commission Merchant (FCM), Designated Contract Market (DCM), and Derivatives Clearing Organization (DCO). "Together, the three licenses provide the full regulatory stack for Backpack's U.S. entry, the first by a crypto-native exchange offering leveraged perpetual futures with global unified liquidity," Backpack said earlier this week.

Selig said platforms can continue using onchain technology, but they would still have to follow the same rules as other exchanges.

Selig also said the CFTC would work with the Justice Department when states sue federally registered platforms in an effort to challenge the agency's jurisdiction. The CFTC and the U.S. government had already sued Utah together in October, and the agency filed separate lawsuits against Arkansas and Louisiana on Nov. 13.

"If it's a legal and lawful derivative, it should be regulated by the market regulator. It's not something that goes to the state," Selig said.

Selig distinguished derivatives exchanges from casinos and state-licensed gambling venues, saying the CFTC has no business regulating the latter. On event contracts, Selig said the CFTC checks whether they are derivatives and meet its rules, including whether they can be easily manipulated. He said the agency's proposed Rule 40.11 lists the public policy factors it would consider when reviewing these contracts.

Selig said just because a single-stock perpetual contract has no expiry date does not make it a security-based swap. He said the structure of these contracts matters and compared their daily funding payments to variation margin payments on other futures contracts.

"It doesn't have an expiration date. And so there's what's called a funding rate, where payments are exchanged on a daily basis," Selig said. "This is very similar to variation margin with other types of futures contracts. But they're structured as futures contracts; they're not structured as a swap or as a security-based swap."

Bridging Crypto and Traditional Finance | Guardarian CEO

Bridging Crypto and Traditional Finance | Guardarian CEO

#5188. Bitcoin ETF investors have a way to hedge during Thanksgiving’s 40+ hour trading gap

Arca-listed spot Bitcoin ETFs miss Thursday’s core session. Eligible CME futures can still trade, with Thursday trades submitted for clearing due to clear Friday.

Spot Bitcoin ETFs listed on NYSE Arca will miss a full core trading session on Thanksgiving. The exchange’s Friday core session will open 41.5 hours after Wednesday’s core close. During the Thursday closure, traders with access to CME Group’s derivatives market can still use eligible Bitcoin futures to adjust their exposure to Bitcoin’s price.

Arca’s core session closes at 4 p.m. ET Wednesday, Nov. 25, and reopens at 9:30 a.m. ET Friday, Nov. 27, according to the NYSE holiday calendar. On Friday, early trading starts at 4 a.m. ET, the core session closes at 1 p.m. ET and late trading ends at 5 p.m. ET.

CME says standard and Micro Bitcoin futures and eligible options trade around the clock on Globex, with weekday maintenance from 4:00 to 4:02 p.m. CT. Traders need an account with a futures broker that offers access. Spot-Quoted futures follow a separate schedule.

An investor holding the Grayscale Bitcoin Trust ETF, which holds Bitcoin and lists its shares on Arca, could sell cash-settled Bitcoin futures to hedge against a decline in Bitcoin’s price. If both the ETF shares and the futures fall, gains from the futures position could offset some of the loss on the shares. The investor keeps the shares, and their price can move by a different amount from the futures price.

The scheduled NYSE Arca and CME events for Nov. 25–27, 2026.

With settlement banks closed Thursday, CME will defer processing of variation payments, cash margin and collateral transfers.

Arca has separate early and late sessions. CME availability depends on eligible contracts, maintenance and futures broker access. CME Clearing gives a day for Thursday submissions to clear.

The 41.5-hour gap adds 24 hours from 4 p.m. Wednesday to 4 p.m. Thursday and 17.5 hours until the 9:30 a.m. Friday core open, all Eastern time. The diagram shows sequence. CME details follow its product schedule and two holiday notices.

Arca has separate early and late sessions. CME availability depends on eligible contracts, maintenance and futures broker access. CME Clearing gives a day for Thursday submissions to clear.

CME says it will skip new daily settlement prices for crypto contracts on Thursday. It will still publish fixings, the prices used to determine exercise and assignment for crypto options expiring that day.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Under the CME Clearing holiday advisory, Wednesday’s settlement prices will carry forward for Thursday’s end-of-day settlement variation, the daily debits and credits tied to changes in settlement prices. CME will skip its intraday cycle. It will still recalculate initial margin, the collateral required to carry positions, at the end of the day.

Trades submitted to CME Clearing on Thursday will clear Friday. With settlement banks closed for Thanksgiving, CME says cash margin, collateral transfers and settlement variation payments will go unprocessed Thursday. A futures trade can change a trader’s exposure when it executes, while clearing and cash movements follow the holiday schedule.

CME has scheduled Friday’s daily equity and crypto settlement prices for noon Central. At that hour, 1 p.m. Eastern, Arca’s shortened core session will end.

#5186. 'It Speaks in Tongues That Only 6.2 Astra Understands': Why Early Testers Hate OpenAI’s New Model

'It Speaks in Tongues That Only 6.2 Astra Understands': Why Early Testers Hate OpenAI’s New Model

For an AI developer, hearing that your model is nice is always pretty good. But hearing your model is like a "sadistic shinigami that turns your life into a living hell," is a totally different thing.

OpenAI released GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol on Tuesday, promising smarter AI agents, better coding and impressive creative capabilities. Some of the developers who tested the models before launch had a rather different message: They're brilliant, frustrating and, in certain cases, almost impossible to work with.

The reaction quickly spilled across social media. AI engineer Hesam, who is very active in the AI community declared that “everyone agrees it’s the worst model OAI dropped.”

Developer and T3 Code creator Theo Browne published a lengthy letter to OpenAI following early testing. “I do not like 6.2 Astra. I would not choose this over Opus 5.5 for my work as a developer,” he wrote. He said OpenAI had fixed only a handful of the complaints he submitted.

“It speaks in tongues that only 6.2 Astra understands,” Theo wrote, calling its answers “the most unparsable outputs of any llm I've used this year,” referring to large language models. The complaint was as much about communication as capability: Astra could solve complex problems but struggled to explain what it had done or recognize when it was asking users questions they could not possibly answer.

In one experiment, Theo asked Astra to create a new level for Outer Wilds. Two hours into the project, the model declared itself blocked and asked: “does r24's reviewer write it's clips to 24_g521.mp4 or 24_p11.mp4?” The technical question was, from his perspective, incomprehensible and irrelevant to someone who had simply requested a playable level.

Things went worse in a code rewrite. Theo said he gave Astra seven days to improve the project; it landed no improvements and made the compiler 56% slower. Four more days yielded zero changes. “It runs in loops and goes nowhere,” he wrote.

This whole weird language issue is not isolated. For example, pseudonymous account, TokenGremlin, praised the model: “GPT-6.2 Astra is SOTA in Three.js, Blender, and 3D,” but said it “starts speaking an alien language during long workflows.”

Theo also described asking why worktrees were slow on one computer. Astra responded with four paragraphs about package-manager internals and cache behavior. The answer Theo said he received from Claude Opus 5.5 was considerably clearer.

“it's because the ssd is slow,” Claude replied.

The model's supposed creativity was another sore spot. Theo and another tester independently requested original levels for two different games, Outer Wilds and Majora's Mask. Both got water-and-bell-themed musical levels, both named “Bellwether.” “This model regresses to the mean like nothing I've ever seen,” Theo wrote.

Several other accounts also flagged the same slop: Seems like GPT-6.2 Astra has a thing for bells.

— Racist James Franco (@racist_james) November 18, 2026

For a model supposedly capable of extraordinary creativity, repeatedly inventing the same level wasn't an encouraging sign.

AI developer Matt Shumer was also disappointed. “I've never written a bad review of a model. This one is especially bad,” he wrote, arguing it might impress in small demonstrations but “just straight up doesn't work” when pushed on serious projects. In a separate follow-up he called it “utterly unusable.”

I've never written a bad review of a model. This one is especially bad.

It's fine if you're playing putt-putt golf with the models, but if you're trying to drive them the way you should be, this model just straight up doesn't work.

— Matt Shumer (@mattshumer_) November 17, 2026

Max Weinbach described the same gap between intelligence and usability. “I had more time being annoyed by the models than time I was impressed by them,” he wrote. In a follow-up, he praised the models' ability to do “fantastic work” but said they “need a ton of ‘continue’ prompts” and often stop rather than try another approach. “Using them is a struggle and annoying even if they are really good models.”

Elsewhere, an X commentator compared the previous Astra to “a sadistic shinigami” and claimed the new version only made matters worse.

6.0 astra was very like a sadistic shinigami who would make your life a living hell if you asked for any kind of help

an incredibly powerful model that somehow required you to run yourself into the ground just to get it to accomplish anything

Yet some of the same developers also praised Astra. Theo called it a “great reviewer” and “world class at 3d & game stuff,” singled out its computer-control abilities, and described a novel reverse-proxy solution he estimated could save $25,000 monthly. The split was between impressive capabilities and how difficult it was to get dependable work out of them.

Those strengths are consistent with the technical capabilities OpenAI promoted at launch. As earlier coverage detailed, independent testing placed Astra at 62 out of 100 on Artificial Analysis's Intelligence Index, an aggregate of nine evaluations covering capabilities including reasoning, coding and scientific knowledge. Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 scored 65.

Some of the criticism has support in OpenAI's own safety documentation.

In an internal evaluation called Blocker Deception, researchers gave AI agents realistic coding tasks with deliberately introduced obstacles, such as missing files or unavailable tools. They measured the percentage of trials in which a model's final response concealed or misrepresented those obstacles.

GPT-6.2 Astra concealed the obstacle in 22.28% of those trials, versus 11.09% for GPT-6 Astra. The numbers quickly circulated on X, where AI commentator and leaker Chetaslua summarized the finding as “2x worse at telling you when it's stuck.”

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman responded publicly to the backlash. “Although the new models are very smart, we have more work ahead of us on making the models nice to work with,” he wrote, promising to prioritize improvements. “But even if it takes a little extra work, the intelligence and capability are extraordinary.”

Although the new models are very smart, we have more work ahead of us on making the models nice to work with, and will prioritize that for upcoming releases.

But even if it takes a little extra work, the intelligence and capability are extraordinary.

OpenAI's Thibault Sottiaux suggested early reviews may not fully reflect the version users received at launch. “We gave a few folks access a while ago and then made a lot of changes in the last 3-4 days,” he wrote, adding that he was “pretty sure no re-testing was done” during that period before review videos were produced.

Still, he acknowledged the underlying complaints. While calling the new models “a hefty jump” over their predecessors, Sottiaux said OpenAI had more work to do on “taste and niceness of use.”

GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are now available through ChatGPT Work, Codex and OpenAI's API for eligible users. The Codex-specific changes are included at launch, but developers accessing the models directly through the API do not receive that same prompting intervention.

#5184. Ripple’s Alderoty doubts Elizabeth Warren will ‘set crypto policy in this country,’ backs durable agency rules

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Alderoty downplayed Warren’s influence, backing durable agency rules, and urging crypto groups to unify their message to Congress.

Ripple Chief Legal Officer Stuart Alderoty is looking to federal regulators to carry crypto market rules over the finish line, arguing that Elizabeth Warren's expected rise to lead the Senate Banking Committee won't put her in charge of the industry's future.

"I don’t think Elizabeth Warren is going to set crypto policy in this country," Alderoty said at The Hill and The Block’s Capitol Crypto Forum on Thursday. "She'll probably focus on oversight and hearings, which I think will be a short-term distraction."

Alderoty, who also serves as president of the National Cryptocurrency Association, said he expects other lawmakers to take the wheel on crypto policy over Warren, who he described as "overtly anti-crypto."

Despite this, he does not expect a version of the Clarity Act to be reintroduced in the coming Congress, pointing to a packed legislative calendar and the 2028 presidential election as priorities.

Pieces of the proposal could be folded into tax legislation or other bills, he said, but a broader bill would likely struggle to garner the committee support needed to reach the floor.

"I think the emphasis and the focus is going to move to the SEC and the CFTC," Alderoty said, also mentioning collaborations with the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Asked whether action from those agencies would be enough for the industry over the next two years without market structure legislation, Alderoty said: "I think it's enough, and I think it's really important."

He argues that rules created through public comment and data analysis would be difficult for a future administration to reverse because they would need to carry out their own policymaking and justify disrupting the market's reliance on the existing rules, he said.

Alderoty contrasted that with the approach taken by former SEC Chair Gary Gensler, arguing that reliance on enforcement left little to no durable policy when he left.

"The Gensler SEC did not do the hard work of rulemaking," he said. "What they decided to do was fall back to regulation by enforcement."

"This administration is doing the hard work of rulemaking, and that is durable," he added.

Alderoty also pushed crypto companies to improve how they present their priorities to Congress.

He cited a handful of possible reasons for the breakdown in the Clarity Act's passage, including the Senate's decision not to advance the House-passed bill, the schism between stablecoin and market structure legislation and disputes over rewards. Ethics concerns surrounding the Trump family’s crypto ties ultimately "overwhelmed the conversation," he said.

The industry's competing messages also made things murky, he added, with different groups and companies all going after lawmakers with "esoteric issues" on DeFi, stablecoin rewards, and token classifications.

When you consider that lawmakers' understanding of crypto can vary widely, it makes the industry's more technical pitches hard to follow. Alderoty likened the situation to a policymaker telling him about fishermen coming in to discuss fishing laws, tackle and tides that the lawmaker didn't understand.

"We need to do the hard work of seeing what do we agree on, making that clear," Alderoty said.

Despite the setbacks, he urged the industry to continue working across party lines.

"Crypto is a policy issue," Alderoty said. "It's not a political issue."

#5182. SEC Charges Eight Colorado Firms in $37 Million Fake Crypto Platform Scam

The SEC charged eight Colorado-based entities on Thursday with running a fake investment adviser and crypto trading platform scheme that took more than $37 million from U.S. investors. The agency said the defendants are likely operated by people overseas.

According to the SEC’s complaint, filed in the U.S. District Court for the District of Colorado, the defendants defrauded more than 1,500 investors from at least August 2024 through May 2026. The advisers and the trading platforms they pointed victims to were fronts, the agency alleged, and none of the crypto assets investors thought they were buying existed.

Social media ads sent prospective victims to WhatsApp group and private chats run by the scammers. People in those chats posing as investment advisers first gave advice on trading U.S. stocks, then pushed victims to send as much money as possible to a supposed crypto trading platform.

The complaint says the scheme ran in four adviser and platform pairs, each active for roughly six months. Advisor WeChat Capital and Tech Brokerage Asset Alliance operated from about August to December 2024. HB Wealth Advisors and IdeaCrypto Exchange followed from January to July 2025.

Convergent Wealth Advisors and RealSimple Crypto Exchange ran from about July to December 2025. Associated Wealth Advisors and Essential Crypto Exchange closed out the scheme from January to May 2026. Each pair vanished after collecting millions, the SEC said, and a new one took its place.

Victims were told to buy ether, tether or USDC on a U.S. exchange and move it to a self-custody wallet. They then sent it to an address supplied by the adviser, and a realistic app showed balances and gains that were invented.

When investors tried to withdraw their supposed profits, they were told they first had to pay a fee, calculated as a percentage of those profits, in crypto to a separate address. The complaint says investors who balked were threatened and pressured. Eventually the websites went down and the chats went silent.

To look legitimate, the defendants made fake SEC certificates and pointed skeptical investors to real filings with the SEC, FINRA and FinCEN. The complaint says those filings carry no regulatory approval, since registering for access to its EDGAR system is ministerial and Forms ADV are not reviewed before publication.

The complaint says Convergent Wealth and Essential Crypto also filed false Forms ADV listing regulatory contacts who do not appear to be real and offices in Colorado that did not exist. The SEC said a Form D and the two Forms ADV have been removed from its website.

Three unnamed investors illustrate the damage. One lost nearly $500,000, including money borrowed from a 401(k), a son and a neighbor. Another lost about $850,000, partly by liquidating a 401(k), and a third lost close to $1.6 million, including the investor’s house and IRA.

All eight entities face charges under Section 10(b) of the Securities Exchange Act and Rule 10b-5. The four advisers are also charged under Sections 206(1) and 206(2) of the Investment Advisers Act, and Convergent Wealth and Essential Crypto face an additional charge under Section 207.

The SEC is seeking permanent injunctions, disgorgement of ill-gotten gains with prejudgment interest, and civil penalties. It also wants the advisers barred from acting as or being associated with any investment adviser, and Convergent Wealth and Essential Crypto barred from filing as exempt reporting advisers.

The agency thanked regulators in Hong Kong, Singapore, mainland China and Switzerland for their help. It also pointed to its investor alerts on group chats and on false claims of SEC registration, and urged investors to check the background of anyone selling them an investment at Investor.gov.

“The SEC continues to seek out and expose fraudulent investment scams that prey on U.S. investors,” said Laura D’Allaird, chief of the SEC Enforcement Division’s Cyber and Emerging Technologies Unit.

#5181. Kalshi’s Quintenz says insider trading is ‘just easier to see’ in prediction markets than stocks

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Kalshi board member Brian Quintenz said there are far more insider trading cases in the stock market, but the link between a trader and an event's outcome can be easier to see in prediction markets.

Brian Quintenz, Kalshi board member and former commissioner of the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said there are far more insider trading cases in the stock market than in prediction markets, where it can be easier to see when someone has affected the outcome of an event they traded on.

"And there are by far more insider trading cases that happen in the stock market than anything that happens in the derivatives markets or in the prediction market space," Quintenz said Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum. "It's just easier to see because it's very direct."

Quintenz said the possibility of insider trading should not make a market illegal or invalid. He said enforcement after a violation should supplement the work exchanges do to prevent it, and added that there was more work to do.

Dan Berkovitz, vice chair of external and regulatory affairs at Millennium Management and a former CFTC commissioner, said federal and exchange rules already prohibit insider trading. He said exchanges also need rules on who can trade, including employees of organizations involved in a contract and people who can influence its outcome.

"So you need rules against insider trading, you need participation limits, and you need strict anti-manipulation provisions," Berkovitz said.

Berkovitz pointed to mention markets, where the outcome can depend on whether a person says a particular word or phrase. A person who can decide whether to say something can also decide the outcome of the contract, he said. Berkovitz welcomed the CFTC's guidance but said more work is needed on who has insider information or can influence the outcome.

"Who has influence or control of the contract? Who has insider knowledge? Those types of things, I think we can still use further development, but there's initial steps being taken to get there," Berkovitz said.

Christy Goldsmith Romero, senior fellow at the Atlantic Council and a former CFTC commissioner, said exchanges are the first line of defense against insider trading and that the CFTC probably needs to do more. She pointed to the requirement that exchanges certifying their own contracts attest that those contracts are not readily susceptible to manipulation. She added that greater clarity was needed from the CFTC to assist exchanges with that.

Quintenz agreed that more guidance would help. However, he said whether a contract is readily susceptible to manipulation is a judgment call and that the CFTC should explain how it applies the standard rather than leaving exchanges to come up with different standards on their own.

The panel also discussed who should regulate sports event contracts. Berkovitz explained that the post-Dodd-Frank final rule continues to prohibit gaming contracts via the CFTC, subject only to future regulatory amendment. The latest interim rule, however, draws a distinction between gambling conducted at a casino and products listed for trading on a federally regulated exchange, Berkovitz said. He added that a legal question remains over whether essentially the same product can be treated as a swap on one platform but not another.

Quintenz said he had submitted a comment letter in his personal capacity, noting that the Supreme Court's 2018 sports betting ruling did not give states exclusive authority. He said federal regulators can oversee exchanges and states can continue regulating casinos and sportsbooks.

Meanwhile, Goldsmith Romero said the Supreme Court would likely take up the matter given the conflicting decisions across the Circuit Courts. "I think it's likely that the prediction markets may end up losing on the sports contracts," she said.

She also said the Securities and Exchange Commission could become more involved as prediction markets offer more contracts related to public companies and their stocks. She added that it would also help if both the SEC and CFTC worked together to provide more clarity.

#5179. Lucky Trader Banks 66,000% Profit on GTA 6 Meme Coin Ticker Confusion

Lucky Trader Banks 66,000% Profit on GTA 6 Meme Coin Ticker Confusion

One lucky meme coin trader made bank as Grand Theft Auto 6 launched worldwide, turning $300 into over $200,000 after early footage of the game sent degens flocking from one unofficial token to another.

The trader bought $307 worth of the DCTY token shortly after it launched in October and has sold their bag for $202,278, according to DEX Screener, a profit of almost 66,000%.

Early indications had pointed to GTA 6 featuring an in-game meme coin—with, it's worth stressing, no actual blockchain element—called "DacityCoin," which appeared in the official launch trailer on a plane with a wing walker setting off a smoke bomb on top of it.

It was later linked to leaked gameplay of an mission which appeared to show game protagonist Lucia parachuting as a stunt to attract investment to DacityCoin.

Amid this hype, per DEX Screener, a real-world and unofficial DacityCoin launched using the Solana launchpad Pump.fun. It first peaked at a $4.95 million market capitalization, following the launch trailer's release, before tanking 93% to $343,620 as hype faded.

With GTA 6 set to launch within a matter of hours of publication, the token drew renewed attention, propelling it to a new all-time high of $10.8 million Wednesday. Unfortunately for investors, GTA 6's staggered global release means that some gamers are already playing it—and footage started to spread which suggested the Solana token's ticker was wrong.

The result was a rollercoaster of volatility, with the DacityCoin token now sitting at a market cap of around $800,000.

Gameplay footage from Brazilian streamer Combo Infinito suggests that the in-game token is called DacityCoin, with the ticker DCTY.

https://t.co/kgefwOzGCH pic.twitter.com/LEDrcP7gve

That propelled another Solana token with a seemingly accurate name and ticker sprang into action. DCTY's previous all-time high market cap was just $54,000 before the ticker was revealed, and it set a new high of over $12 million after footage of the game appeared to confirm that its ticker matched the in-game token. It is now trading at a market cap of around $10 million.

The game itself appears to be somewhat inconsistent in its naming of the in-game token. During the trailer, it appeared to be spelled "DacityCoin" as one word, however, the subtitles in the leak show it spelled as "Dacity coin," two words. Similarly, the company behind the in-game token is AudaCity, but leaks show three different spellings: Auda City, AudaCity, and Auda-City.

It is worth stressing: Rockstar has not explicitly confirmed that a meme coin will be featured in-game, and any real-world token has no official links to GTA 6 or its creators. Footage is still being released, and absolute confirmation of the in-game token's involvement is still being established.

Those still holding the now-tanking DacityCoin are hoping things will turn around. Mike Lerro, the community takeover lead for DacityCoin, shrugged off any chance that the unofficial token's name and ticker are wrong.

"No, bro. DacityCoin and AudaCity are in the game. Yes or no?" When Decrypt suggested the ticker might be wrong, he replied, "It doesn't matter… Okay and ? The person who creates it will dump on you then it goes to zero. Who's gonna [do the community takeover]? No one."

"DACITYCOIN AND AUDACITY ARE IN THE GAME," Lerro wrote in the meme coin's Telegram. "NOTHING ELSE MATTERS."

"It's so crazy, it'll correct itself," Lerro said in an emergency Telegram group chat call. "I hope so," an investor replied.

#5176. Coinbase backs CFTC proposal to distinguish prediction market contracts from gambling

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Coinbase said event contracts already qualify as swaps under the Commodity Exchange Act and that explicit inclusion would provide greater regulatory certainty.

Crypto exchange Coinbase has filed comments supporting the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission's proposals to explicitly include event contracts in the definition of a "swap" and separate them from casino-style gambling products, as federal regulators and states remain locked in legal disputes over jurisdiction of prediction markets.

In a letter submitted Wednesday, Coinbase said that event contracts — including contracts based on sports, politics, culture, and weather — already qualify as swaps under the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA).

The company argued that event contracts are tradeable financial instruments whose value and payment depend on future events, and that they can help market participants "hedge real economic risks."

"Making that treatment explicit will provide important certainty in an area where state actions and conflicting judicial decisions have created unnecessary confusion," the company said.

In a separate letter, Coinbase backed the CFTC's interim rule to exclude casino-style gambling products from the "swap" definition. The company said such gambling products are neither tradable nor cleared and were "never indicated" by Congress to fall within the definition of swap.

"Casino games and sportsbook wagers are not derivatives, and they have long been regulated by the states and tribes," the company added.

Coinbase argued that exchange-traded event contracts are "fundamentally distinct" from sportsbooks traditionally regulated under state laws.

"An event-contract exchange serves as a neutral market operator," the company said. "[It] does not choose prices or act as a counterparty to its members' trades, and it is indifferent to whether any given customer wins or loses."

Coinbase's comments were in response to the CFTC's proposal and interim final rule released last month, which were open for public comment through Nov. 18.

Federal regulators and state authorities have been embroiled in escalating legal battles over who has the authority to regulate prediction market platforms. The CFTC has maintained that it has exclusive jurisdiction over prediction markets under the CEA, while several states have argued that certain event contracts violate state gambling laws.

During a Thursday interview at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum, CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said the CFTC would work with the Justice Department to defend its jurisdiction when states sue prediction market platforms.

The CFTC and the DOJ sued Utah last month and filed lawsuits against Arkansas and Louisiana last week.

"If it's a legal and lawful derivative, it should be regulated by the market regulator. It's not something that goes to the state," Selig said.

#5174. Fed's Waller Calls for 50-Basis-Point Rate Hike, Says Tokenized Finance Still Needs Traditional Banks

Fed's Waller Calls for 50-Basis-Point Rate Hike, Says Tokenized Finance Still Needs Traditional Banks

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller wants the central bank to get more aggressive with interest rate hikes, arguing that stubborn inflation may require a 50-basis-point increase in December rather than the smaller move investors expect.

Speaking during a CNBC interview Wednesday, Waller said the Fed must either deliver a half-percentage-point increase at its December 8-9 meeting or explicitly signal that another hike will follow in January.

“You might need to, like, shock people,” Waller said, referring to the central bank's efforts to prevent businesses and consumers from accepting inflation near 3% as normal.

“My view is then you have to go 50 in December to put it in place and get it done,” he said, warning that insufficiently aggressive action could weaken expectations that inflation will return to the Fed's target.

Waller's concerns follow three consecutive months in which core consumer prices rose 0.3%, a pace equivalent to roughly 3.5% inflation annually. That's considerably higher than the Fed's 2% target, which is formally measured using a separate inflation indicator known as the Personal Consumption Expenditures price index.

The governor had already established his threshold for a more aggressive move in a November 10 speech. He said another 0.3% core CPI reading would warrant considering a half-point hike, arguing that moving only 25 basis points could damage the Fed's credibility.

October inflation subsequently met that condition. Headline consumer prices rose 0.4% during the month, while core inflation climbed 0.3%, both exceeding economists' forecasts.

Waller's latest comments suggest he is increasingly concerned about the psychology of inflation, particularly the risk that companies begin treating annual price increases of 3% as acceptable and routinely pass higher costs to customers.

The proposed increase would lift the Fed's benchmark rate from its current range of 3.75%-4% to 4.25%-4.50%. Two consecutive quarter-point hikes in December and January would reach the same destination, but Waller argues that timing and communication matter when inflation expectations are at risk.

Markets remain less convinced that the Fed will move that aggressively in December. Futures pricing Wednesday morning showed roughly a 95% probability of a 25-basis-point increase and only about a 5% probability of a half-point move

The prospect of higher borrowing costs has been particularly painful for crypto investors. Higher interest rates make Treasury securities and other interest-bearing investments more competitive with speculative assets, while tighter financial conditions can reduce the money available for riskier bets.

Bitcoin has already suffered a sharp selloff amid the changing rate outlook. As Decrypt previously covered, persistent inflation fears have coincided with withdrawals from spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds and weakening technical indicators, adding pressure to an already fragile market.

Waller's increasingly hawkish monetary-policy stance comes alongside a more accommodating view of blockchain networks, although he sees established financial infrastructure as essential to its adoption.

In a separate November 17 speech at The Clearing House Annual Conference in New York, Waller discussed how tokenization—the representation of financial assets on digital networks—could reshape payments and securities markets. That speech focused on financial technology rather than interest-rate policy.

He pointed to a growing convergence between traditional markets and blockchain platforms, with banks and payment providers experimenting with tokenized deposits, stablecoins, and programmable transactions.

Waller described how tokenized money could allow investors to purchase securities outside conventional trading hours, with payments and assets changing hands simultaneously through smart contracts.

But he rejected the idea that the development of blockchain-based markets necessarily makes existing payment infrastructure obsolete.

“Central bank money need not be used in every transaction or on every platform, but its presence at the foundation of the system is essential,” Waller said.

#5172. Strategy (MSTR) Shares Tumble for Sixth Straight Day to $86 as Bitcoin Slides

Strategy (MSTR) shares fell 4.6% on Thursday to close at $86.13, their sixth straight daily decline, as bitcoin dipped below $62,000 and a broad selloff hit U.S. stocks. In after hours trading, shares were down to $85.50.

The stock opened near $93 and slid through the afternoon, finishing near its low of about $86. The Dow Jones Industrial Average dropped more than 1,000 points, and the Nasdaq Composite lost about 2.1%. Bitcoin touched a low of $61,658 during the session, its weakest price since July. It was back near $62,000 late in the afternoon.

The drop adds to a brutal month for the largest corporate holder of bitcoin. Strategy shares are down about 37% from their $136.02 close on Oct. 19, and down about 29% from the $121.07 close on Nov. 10, which is when the latest leg lower began. Thursday was the stock’s sixth consecutive losing session.

The slide has wiped out most of the year’s value. Strategy closed 2025 at $151.95, which puts the stock down about 43% in 2026. The shares fell below $100 on Tuesday and now trade about 11% below Tuesday’s $96.50 close. That day they also dipped under the company’s STRC preferred for the first time since mid-August.

Thursday’s move tracked bitcoin closely. Institutions have kept buying even so, and a New York state pension fund boosted its stake by more than 10% in a recent filing. The bitcoin price slumped under $62,000 as traders weighed the risk of Federal Reserve rate hikes and a sharp pullback in technology stocks.

Strategy added 485 bitcoin in the week ending Nov. 15, according to its Nov. 16 Form 8-K. It paid $33.4 million, or an average of $68,887 per coin, and its total reached 850,849 BTC.

Those coins cost $64.20 billion, or an average of $75,452 each. With bitcoin near $62,000, the stack is worth roughly $52.8 billion, which leaves Strategy with an unrealized loss of about $11 billion. Bitcoin is trading about 18% below the company’s average purchase price.

The 850,849 coins are more than 4% of bitcoin’s 21 million supply cap, and the company also held about $4.9 billion in U.S. dollar reserves as of Nov. 15, which gives it a cushion for preferred dividends and buybacks. Strategy has not announced a new purchase since that filing. It has reported its buys on Mondays in recent weeks, so the next update would normally come Monday.

Most of Strategy’s recent cash has gone to its preferred stock instead of bitcoin. In the same filing, the company said it repurchased 2,282,510 shares of its STRC preferred for $228.1 million, nearly seven times what it spent on bitcoin.

It funded the purchases with $244.7 million raised from selling common stock. The 8-K shows $332.2 million remaining under the preferred repurchase program and $1.0 billion under its common stock buyback authorization.

Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said on X that Strategy plans to keep going. He wrote that STRC traded more than $600 million on Wednesday, calling it the only one of 456 U.S.-listed income preferreds to do so this year.

He said the buybacks add liquidity, lower dividend obligations and strengthen the credit. “We intend to keep buying below $100,” Saylor wrote.

#5168. Inside Bukele’s Casa Presidencial Dinner: Bitcoiners Discuss Energy, Immigration, and El Salvador’s Future

On November 10, 2026, President Nayib Bukele hosted roughly 30 Bitcoiners at Casa Presidencial during Bitcoin Histórico. Guests included Ricardo Salinas Pliego, Parker Lewis, Mike Peterson, and miners; discussions covered power generation for compute and immigration.

This November 10, in the midst of the Bitcoin Histórico conference taking place in the Historical Center of San Salvador, a group of Bitcoiners representing a wide range of sectors within the industry met with President Bukele.The dinner was organized by The Bitcoin Office, which is part of the Office of the President, and was led by Stacy Herbert and Max Keiser. The dinner went on from about 7:30 pm to 10:30 pm local time and included about thirty Bitcoiners, many regular visitors to El Salvador.

Among the guests was billionaire Ricardo Salinas Pliego, founder of Grupo Salinas, which owns Banco Azteca. Salinas is a known Bitcoiner, who has made public appearances with Keiser and Herbert in the past, and is speculated to be planning a run at the next Mexican Presidential election. Salinas met privately with the President besides the dinner and reportedly gifted him volumes one and two of Manuscripts of the Conquistadors, works curated and edited by Jorge Nikaido, Chief of Staff of Grupo Salinas.

Representing investors and founders were Parker Lewis, founder of Unchained Capital, Mike Peterson of Bitcoin Beach fame, Katie Ananina of Citizen X, and Aleks Svetski, entrepreneur and founder of Satlantis. Bitcoin miners also attended, such as  Beau Turner,  CEO of  Abundant Mines, Nacho Pauls, CCO of OCEAN Mining, and Adam Ortolf of Denver Bitcoin mining. Jon Atack, a Bitcoin core contributor, was also at the dinner alongside engineer Giacomo Zucco of Plan B Network, and AI tech entrepreneurs Ginger Gaetz of the ANIA AI initiative and Marc Seal, Founder of Aevonix.The academic and media side of the industry was also heavily represented, with none other than Michael Goldstein, also known as Bitstein and co-founder of the Nakamoto Institute. Eric Yakes, economist and author of The 7th Property, was there, as well as Efrat Feningson, independent journalist and podcaster. Local Bitcoiner Michael Ruiz also attended the dinner; he is the founder of Build in El Salvador (BIS), an increasingly popular kind of business club and Bitcoin meetup mix.

The guests arrived at the Casa Presidencial earlier in the evening and were chatting and networking while waiting for the president. Max Keiser, always a showman, arrived at the Casa Presidencial with a gift some might speculate carried a magical artefact. Photographed above, the case was said to contain a Stetson El Presidente cowboy hat, though some have speculated it might in fact have carried the Philosopher’s Stone.

Upon arrival, Bukele shook everyone’s hand and quickly invited them to take their assigned seats, dine, and relax- no big speeches or over-the-top formalities, Ananina told Bitcoin Magazine, describing the dinner as a kind of open mic.

“President Bukele’s table was adorned with bitcoin orange florals,” Herbert told Bitcoin Magazine; “we gathered anarchists, billionaire tycoons, Wall Street bankers, circular economy activists, VC investors, bitcoin miners, and content creators” at the country’s equivalent of the White House.

A three-course meal began to be served, with steak — of course — being the main course, followed by a three-layered chocolate dessert. In between were three hours of conversation which left a very strong impression on the individuals interviewed on the night. Bukele had his own microphone, while a couple of other mics were passed around. Six to seven major questions were asked throughout the night.

At some point, energy generation came up, and Salinas in particular engaged the topic with the President.

Baeu Turner, one of the three bitcoin miners at the dinner, told Bitcoin Magazine that Bukele described El Salvador’s electricity generation as “being roughly matched to the country’s current demand.” Bukele described plans to add more than a gigawatt of additional generation across multiple projects, including geothermal and generation using imported liquefied natural gas.

“What stood out to me,” added Turner, “was how directly he connected those development plans with demand for compute. Bukele said they can develop this power knowing there are users who can put it to work, whether that’s Bitcoin mining or other data centers. The demand could come from the Salvadoran government’s own operations or from outside companies bringing equipment and investment into the country.”

Other miners responded positively to that idea, including Salinas, who has deep investments and experience with energy production. His Grupo Dragón is involved in geothermal, wind, solar, and natural-gas generation across Mexico.

Immigration was also discussed. Spurred by Max Keiser’s enthusiasm about the country, where he roughly said that the world should recognize El Salvador’s arc and that people — bitcoiners presumably — should migrate; suddenly the topic was set on the table. Ruiz, whose family is from the region but was born and raised in the U.S., moved to El Salvador three years ago and has become a recognizable figure in the local Bitcoin community. He followed up with a question about immigration, in particular about gentrification and possible resentment from the local population towards Western migrants, pointing to very negative online comments about some Bitcoiners, such as being called “colonizers”.

Bukele asked back, “When you go out, walk around, and interact with Salvadorans, what are the interactions like?”, The question, to anyone that’s spent time in El Salvador in recent years, answers itself. Salvadorians are very welcoming, Ruiz told Bitcoin Magazine; “there’s a lot of love. People receive us with open arms, and they’re glad we’re here, and they’re happy”.

Immigration in El Salvador actually tends to be from regional countries like Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, and Colombia, with smaller numbers reported from the United States. Bukele went on to dismiss concerns about immigration, joking that you could host the whole population of the world in El Salvador by matching the population density of Manhattan. This was not a statement meant to be taken as a goal or ideal, but rather to set the topic in context. He added that the country is obviously looking for the most capable and intelligent people, and even said that “all great empires were built with immigrants’ help”, which says a lot about the President’s mindset on the topic.

In response to concerns about gentrification, the President told the story of a fisherman and house caretaker who got a raise from $300 to $800 a month from an American who bought the property. As a result, the fisherman was able to buy a motor for his boat, which he used to fish and row by hand before that. The scale-up of his fishing business was so productive that the caretaker even got his son a boat, doubling his fishing fleet. The only people who complained about this growth in wages were the neighbours, so the story goes, who must have been feeling the pressure from their own villa’s caretakers, wanting a raise from meager wages.

At some point Efrat asked, “How come you are criticized around the world despite El Salvador’s GDP growth, a strengthening economy and popularity at home, which I saw firsthand talking to locals and visiting some of the areas once worst hit by gang violence?”

“Because I’m not part of their club,” the President allegedly responded, “I’m not the kind of ruler they like”. He added that there are evil people in the world who use mainstream media to criticize leaders like him and “call him names”, central bankers, including the ECB, came up as one of the forces behind the negative media campaign against Bukele.Beyond the clear hit pieces, he noted that many people in governments or the international political establishment are not evil per se, but are often ‘order followers’, who operate on certain narratives and resist new paradigms.

The recent wins for the country in terms of education reform were also discussed. Bukele allegedly said that many private schools are now integrating similar AI tutoring programs as the one led by his administration. The results made international news when the pilot program deployed in public schools over the past couple of years brought PISA test results to par with Germany and Switzerland.

When asked about their reflections on the presidential dinner and the Bitcoin Histórico conference in general, Max Kaiser shared his vision in a manner that merits being printed in full:

“Bitcoin Histórico promised two days of Excellence Only, as the Bitcoin Office likes to say, and it delivered big time. The National Palace was a regal setting to showcase the Renaissance 2.0 taking shape in El Salvador with presentations by the best Bitcoin minds and builders in the world. Audience members felt like they had been transported to a time and place found nowhere else in the world right now, where hope and happiness are in abundance. No doom.”

Stacy Herbert, whose presence and attention to detail were felt throughout the festivities, told Bitcoin Magazine, “We promoted Bitcoin Histórico as an elevated bitcoin conference experience with Philosopher King meets Renaissance 2.0 vibes. In every detail, my team and I achieved that, including with the official Bitcoin Office dinner at Casa Presidencial.” She added that guests were free to ask any question they wanted, and that no topic was off limits.

“They grilled the President for several hours. His wit, his charm, his intelligence – all unscripted – was there for all to see firsthand. And they will have walked away understanding why it is that El Salvador is winning under his leadership.” Herbert added. As far as attendees to the dinner who Bitcoin Magazine spoke with, both claims remain very much corroborated.

#5167. Bitcoin Miners Are Selling Their Stacks to Fund the AI Boom

Bitcoin Miners Are Selling Their Stacks to Fund the AI Boom

Two of the biggest U.S. Bitcoin miners are turning their Bitcoin into AI infrastructure.

MARA sold 13,597 BTC after the end of September at an average price of about $81,039, raising roughly $1.1 billion, according to its third-quarter shareholder letter filed with the SEC.

That’s about 40% of the 34,012 BTC it held at the end of September. Combined with the 26,984 BTC it sold in the first nine months of the year, MARA has sold more than 40,000 BTC in 2026, worth roughly $2.5 billion at today’s prices.

MARA used $750 million of the proceeds to fully repay Bitcoin-backed loans it took out to fund its acquisition of the Long Ridge power plant.

The company said it intends to keep using Bitcoin to raise cash for AI infrastructure, convertible note puts that become exercisable next year and potential share buybacks. Its board has authorized a repurchase of up to $500 million.

MARA insisted the shift is “not a pivot away from bitcoin,” describing mining as its operating foundation while it builds out AI and high-performance computing infrastructure.

Meanwhile, Riot Platforms sold 20,264 BTC in the first nine months of the year for about $1.5 billion, primarily to fund data center development, and ended September with just 2,418 BTC, according to its Nov. 4 results.

The miner also signed a 15-year lease for its entire Corsicana, Texas, campus, covering 910 megawatts of capacity and worth about $28 billion over the initial term. CEO Jason Les called it “the largest lease in Riot’s history.” Riot’s quarterly filing identifies the tenant as Anthropic.

Mining economics help explain the shift. Riot’s Bitcoin mining revenue fell to $116 million in the third quarter from $160.8 million a year earlier, as the average value of each Bitcoin it mined dropped to $70,855 from $114,361.

Including equipment depreciation, Riot said it cost $78,805 to mine each Bitcoin, more than the coin’s average value over the quarter. MARA’s revenue fell 35% to $164.2 million.

The sales add supply to an already fragile market. Onchain analytics firm Glassnode said Tuesday that the two miners drove the recent selling by Bitcoin treasury companies, and that treasury firms outside those two were net buyers in November.

Strategy, the largest corporate holder, kept buying, adding 485 BTC last week to bring its holdings to 850,849 BTC, worth around $54 billion.

#5164. Michael Saylor sees up to $7.5 billion in ‘natural demand’ for Strategy’s STRC preferred stock

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Saylor said the credit-market contraction began around May, and the company is responding by reducing the amount of STRC outstanding until it reaches an equilibrium level.

Strategy Strategy, Inc. (MSTR) Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said "natural, organic" demand for the company's STRC preferred stock at its current 12% dividend rate is likely between $5 billion and $7.5 billion.

"I don't know exactly where the number is, but I’m going to go out on a limb and say that somewhere between $5 billion and $7.5 billion, there is natural organic demand to hold the instrument at 12% dividend," Saylor said during a live investor Q&A on Thursday.

His comments come as Strategy has been actively repurchasing STRC shares at prices below its $100 stated level. President and CEO Phong Le said the company has bought back about $3 billion of the preferred stock at prices between $95 and $99, below the issuance price level.

Saylor described the current environment as a credit-market contraction that began around May. He said the company is responding by reducing the amount of STRC outstanding until it reaches an equilibrium level that the market will support at the existing dividend rate.

"We're going to contract the outstanding STRC because 12% is a fat dividend for an investment-grade instrument,” Saylor said. "As we buy it back, we lengthen the duration, we strip the risk, we improve the credit."

CEO Le also said that the recent buybacks have de-risked the company's balance sheet after credit markets tightened over the past four to five months. Le added that Strategy had previously expanded STRC from an initial $2.5 billion issuance to roughly $8 billion under favorable market conditions.

Saylor framed the support for STRC as important for the long-term credibility of the product. He compared it to an insurance company paying claims, arguing that failing to back the security in a weaker market would damage confidence among credit investors over multiple years.

"When the company is swapping either BTC or MSTR for STRC, we're supporting the credit, we're supporting the brand of the company, we're improving the product quality," Saylor said.

The Strategy Chairman also reiterated that the company has at least three years' worth of cash right now, so it will still be able to operate even if the current bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) downturn continues.

The world's largest digital asset treasury firm owned 850,849 BTC as of Nov. 15, according to its latest disclosure, which amounts to more than 4% of bitcoin's maximum supply cap. Last month, holders of Strategy's STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD preferred shares approved a change allowing dividends to accrue daily, rather than on the previous schedule.

Last week, the company sold approximately $244.7 million in MSTR shares to fund bitcoin purchases and STRC repurchases.

#5161. Bitcoin Price Slips Near $61,500 as Tech Selloff Spreads to Asia

The bitcoin price slipped to near $61,500 on Friday morning, according to Look Into Bitcoin data, as a selloff in technology stocks that followed Anthropic’s Nasdaq debut spread to Asian markets and dragged bitcoin to its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin first broke below $62,000 on Thursday evening, after Thursday afternoon’s report showed it giving back a rebound toward $64,000. The bitcoin price then slid to nearly $61,200 during Asian trading before bouncing back above $61,700. Friday marks a third straight day of losses.

The selling started in Asia. South Korea’s Kospi slid more than 5%, with chipmakers SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics down around 6%. The Korea Exchange halted program selling for five minutes after the drop, while Japan’s Nikkei fell as much as 4.85% to a seven-month low.

U.S. futures pointed lower too. Nasdaq-100 futures fell more than 1.7% and S&P 500 futures dropped 0.8%, Yahoo Finance reported. Gold futures lost 3%. The S&P 500 is pointing to a seventh straight daily decline, its longest losing streak since the start of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020.

Anthropic shares fell 8% in premarket trading after closing their first session at $97.17, up 17% from the $83 IPO price. Nvidia was down about 2.4% and AMD fell nearly 7%, CNBC reported. Hawkish signals from Federal Reserve officials this week also weighed on sentiment, Reuters said.

Bitcoin funds are feeling the pressure. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded $541.1 million in net outflows on Thursday, its largest single-day outflow since launch. That topped the previous record of $528.3 million set on Jan. 30. The fund bled $2 billion over nine straight days of outflows earlier this month.

Leveraged traders took heavy losses as the bitcoin price broke support. Bitcoin liquidations hit $712.9 million over 24 hours, with $614 million coming from long positions. Open interest in bitcoin futures fell to $18 billion from $20 billion a week ago, and funding rates flipped broadly negative as traders unwound their bets.

Traders are now watching whether the bitcoin price can hold the $60,000 area. A large pocket of long positions sits in the $60,500 to $60,900 band, just below the current price, and could come into play on a further drop. Bitcoin is also on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, which would be its second-longest losing streak after nine weeks of declines in 2022.

Crypto stocks pointed lower in premarket trading. Strategy (MSTR) traded near $83.90, down about 2.6% from Thursday’s close of $86.13, after the stock fell for a sixth straight day on Thursday. Coinbase (COIN) slipped about 2.7% to near $106.

Bitcoin miners fell harder. IREN (IREN) dropped about 4.4%, CleanSpark (CLSK) lost about 3.6% and MARA Holdings (MARA) declined about 3.4%. Riot Platforms (RIOT) was down about 3.3%. IREN has also been under pressure as investors reassess AI infrastructure stocks. Delays in bringing some of its capacity online led Microsoft to spend less with the miner than planned last quarter.

Longer-term buyers have not left entirely. Institutional investors added 49,229 BTC through U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs in the third quarter, lifting their holdings to a record 356,491 BTC. Swiss bank Sygnum called the current downturn the shallowest bear market for bitcoin to date, putting the drawdown at around 54% compared with declines of more than 70% in previous cycles.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $61,800.

#5159. Senate passes ‘Death Bets Act’ to ban prediction market contracts tied to death and war

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Death Bets Act, which now heads to the House, would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination, and war.

The U.S. Senate has passed the "Death Bets Act," which would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination, and war.

The S.4035 bill, titled "Discouraging Exploitative Assassination, Tragedy, and Harm Betting in Event Trading Systems Act," cleared the Senate on Thursday and now heads to the House of Representatives.

Sen. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) introduced the bill in March 2026 as primary sponsor, with Sen. John Curtis (R-Utah) as a co-sponsor. The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA) to prohibit listing contracts related to war, death, and similar activities.

"[These contracts] create rampant opportunities for insider trading, and perhaps worst of all, they're offered with no regard for national security and, in far too many cases, even basic morality," Schiff said Thursday.

Schiff added that prediction markets are operating in a "Wild West" and are "desperately in need of regulation and guardrails."

Under the CEA, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission has the authority to block contracts tied to war, terrorism, or assassination. While the final decision is at the agency's discretion, the lawmakers backing the Death Bets Act argued that such flexibility is no longer sufficient.

Prediction market platforms have also taken steps to remove controversial contracts. Earlier this year, Polymarket took down a market that let users bet on the likelihood of a nuclear weapon detonation due to criticism on social media.

The congressional push adds to the mounting legal pressure prediction market platforms are already facing. More than a dozen states have taken legal or enforcement actions against Kalshi for allegedly violating state gambling laws.

Meanwhile, federal regulators and state authorities remained locked in escalating legal battles over who has the authority to regulate prediction market platforms. The CFTC has maintained that it has jurisdiction over prediction markets under the CEA, while several states have argued that certain event contracts violate state gambling laws.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said during a Thursday interview at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum that the agency would work with the Justice Department to defend its jurisdiction in legal disputes.

In recent weeks, the CFTC and the DOJ have filed lawsuits against several states, including Utah, Arkansas, and Louisiana, seeking to block state enforcement against prediction market platforms.

"If it's a legal and lawful derivative, it should be regulated by the market regulator. It's not something that goes to the state," Selig said.
