#5158. Bitcoin Tanks Ahead of Anthropic’s IPO. Should We Really Blame AI?

Bitcoin Tanks Ahead of Anthropic’s IPO. Should We Really Blame AI?

Bitcoin fell toward $63,000 Wednesday, extending a six-week retreat just as AI developer Anthropic prepared to price one of Wall Street’s biggest initial public offerings.

The timing has revived an attractive theory: investors are selling crypto to buy AI shares.

Right now Bitcoin is about to touch $62,000, down 3.8% on today’s session. Anthropic expects to price shares Wednesday at an indicated $70 to $78, ahead of Thursday’s Nasdaq debut under ANT, potentially valuing the company at $1.4 trillion, according to its IPO prospectus.

Kraken chief economist Thomas Perfumo told MarketWatch the IPO “could also be drawing capital out of risk assets” as investors position for the sale.

That’s a theory Binance founder Changpeng Zhao shared, flagging the listing as one possible factor in Bitcoin’s decline in an X post Sunday.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

There is a tempting precedent. Bitcoin fell 19% in the 50 days before SpaceX listed in June and 23% in the equivalent period before Anthropic. But the comparison misses an important difference: AI stocks rallied before SpaceX; this time they have been falling alongside crypto.

“This time, the sell-off has been led by the AI trade itself,” Block Scholes research analyst Thahbib Rahman wrote in comments shared with Decrypt. Over the prior 50 days, Nvidia fell 32% and the Nasdaq dropped 11%, unlike their gains ahead of SpaceX’s IPO.

Bitcoin’s 30-day correlation with Nvidia rose from 0.20 to 0.56, while its correlation with gold fell from 0.63 to 0.39. “Liquidity is ebbing and flowing out of crypto and equities together once more,” Rahman wrote.

Bitcoin’s daily chart looks weak without any IPO theory. The relative strength index reads 21.8, well below the 30 threshold commonly associated with oversold markets, while an average directional index of 38.7 indicates a strong trend—in this case, downward.

The 50-day exponential moving average is also below its 200-day counterpart, a bearish configuration associated with the so-called death cross. The pattern historically signals deteriorating momentum and the possibility of further losses, although it is a lagging indicator and does not necessarily predict where prices will go next.

Also, oversold does not necessarily mean the selling has ended.

The immediate floor is around $62,000, close to Sunday’s low. Glassnode data show buyers absorbed 66,000 BTC between $62,000 and $66,000 during heavy selling, but stablecoin supply has shrunk 4.2% over 90 days. Below $62,000, the next psychological level is $60,000; resistance sits near $65,500, $69,300 and $73,700.

More tangible selling pressure has already been recorded. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs lost $239.9 million Tuesday, including $209.5 million from BlackRock’s IBIT. Nearly $2 billion in leveraged crypto bets were liquidated during the weekend rout, amplifying losses as exchanges forcibly closed positions.

Hilbert Group portfolio manager Jesse Marre called the selloff “simply a stop loss run,” arguing that derivatives traders had already capitulated. Rising Treasury yields, a stronger dollar, and renewed expectations of Federal Reserve rate hikes also make speculative assets less attractive.

In other words, the dip is more natural market moves than money switching markets.

Nvidia forecast $130 billion in fourth-quarter sales, beating expectations after Jensen Huang’s abrupt departure. Yet markets remained split: global shares slipped 0.1%, while the Nasdaq gained 0.35% early and CoreWeave dropped 5.4%.

BitMEX co-founder Arthur Hayes sees the IPO less as a drain than a barometer. “Bitcoin’s going to go up too” if Anthropic soars, he said, while a weak debut could deepen pressure across risk assets.

Anthropic is scheduled to price Wednesday and start trading Thursday. Before that debut, Bitcoin must hold the same $62,000 area buyers defended during Sunday’s liquidation wave.

#5155. Bitcoin Hovers Just Above Average Holder’s Cost Basis as Price Keeps Dipping

Bitcoin is trading just above its realized price, the average cost of every coin on the network, and a break below that level would put the average holder at a loss for the first time since early 2023, Glassnode, an onchain analytics firm posted Friday.

The price of bitcoin sat near $61,700 on Friday after sliding below $62,000. The realized price is a measure of what holders paid, not where the market trades. When bitcoin trades above it, the typical coin is sitting on a gain. Below it, the typical coin is underwater.

The margin is thin. The firm said the MVRV ratio, which compares bitcoin’s market value with its realized value, sits “just above 1.” A reading of 1 means the market price has fallen all the way to the realized price.

Realized price values each coin at the price it last moved on the blockchain, then averages across the supply. Look Into Bitcoin describes it as bitcoin’s average cost basis. Because it tracks the price when coins last changed hands, it moves far more slowly than the market.

The MVRV ratio expresses the same idea as a multiple. Above 1, holders are in profit on average. Below 1, they are not. A reading just above 1 means the market price is only a sliver higher than what the average coin cost.

The firm did not give the exact realized price in its post, which included a chart. That leaves the exact cushion between today’s price and the average cost basis unstated. Bitcoin Magazine Pro’s full realized price chart sits behind a login, so the current level could not be confirmed independently for this report.

“The average Bitcoin holder is still in profit,” the firm wrote. “The margin is thin.”

The slide has come with heavy selling from funds. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) lost about $541 million on Thursday, its largest one-day outflow since it launched in January 2024, Bloomberg reported. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $582 million in total that day.

The selling stands out against the buying earlier in the fall. Institutions piled into bitcoin ETFs through the third quarter, pushing their combined holdings to a record, Bitcoin Magazine reported this week.

Leveraged traders took losses too. More than $700 million in leveraged crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours, and about 86% of them were bets on higher prices. Bitcoin accounted for roughly $200 million of the total.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said the drop is part of a longer downturn rather than the start of a new one. “I see this as a continuation of the existing bear market rather than the start of another,” he said in a note dated Nov. 18.

The note frames Friday’s price action as a late stage of a decline that began well before November. Butterfill pointed to whale selling that weighed on prices from late 2025, expectations of higher interest rates and weakness in technology stocks, all of which have kept buyers cautious.

“Our base case remains range trading around USD 60k to 90k through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives monetary policy room to ease,” he said. “Geopolitics will have a considerable say in that.”

A move through the realized price would put bitcoin near the bottom edge of that range. The firm said holders as a group have not been in loss since early 2023.

A sustained breakout, Butterfill said, would likely require “a change in the policy outlook, renewed whale participation or both.”

#5153. MoonPay applies for national trust bank charter to expand stablecoin services

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

MoonPay plans to offer custody and stablecoin services through the proposed bank, subject to OCC approval.

Crypto payments firm MoonPay announced Thursday it had filed an application with the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to launch MoonPay National Trust Bank.

The application, dated Nov. 17 and received by the OCC on Nov. 18, said MoonPay's trust bank would provide fiduciary custody services; stablecoin services, including issuance, redemption, and reserve management; and conversion services as an agent.

The filing is to organize MoonPay National Trust Bank as a non-depository national bank, limited to trust company operations and related activities. It would not seek federal deposit insurance and would operate as a member bank of the Federal Reserve.

If approved, the national charter would provide a single federal framework for MoonPay to offer custody and stablecoin-related services. MoonPay’s application comes ahead of the GENIUS Act taking effect on Jan. 18, 2027, when payment stablecoin issuers will generally need approval under the new framework.

Several other major crypto firms have received full or conditional approval for the same type of OCC national trust bank charter.

Last December, the OCC granted conditional approvals to Circle (CRCL), Ripple, BitGo, Fidelity Digital Assets, and Paxos. Circle received final approval in July 2026, while Ripple received its full approval in October. Coinbase's (COIN) became effective earlier this month. Additional firms, including Stripe's Bridge, Crypto.com, and World Liberty Financial, hold conditional approvals.

Meanwhile, MoonPay has expanded its regulated and enterprise footprint over the past year. It received its New York BitLicense in June 2025 and its New York trust charter in November 2025.

Earlier this year, MoonPay agreed to acquire private markets platform North Capital in an all-stock deal. It launched enterprise stablecoin services in November 2025 and partnered with WisdomTree to incorporate a tokenized Treasury fund into stablecoin reserves. It has also announced a strategic collaboration with Dunamu, the parent company of South Korean crypto exchange Upbit, to explore stablecoin-based global payments and fund movement.

Former Commodity Futures Trading Commission Acting Chair Caroline Pham also joined MoonPay as chief legal officer and chief administrative officer last December.

#5151. Why CoinShares Just Sold All Its Ethereum and Half Its Solana

Why CoinShares Just Sold All Its Ethereum and Half Its Solana

CoinShares, one of Europe’s largest digital-asset managers, has sold its entire corporate treasury holding of Ethereum and half of its Solana.

It’s a move that might look like a loss of conviction but is really about something more prosaic: funding a stock buyback.

The decision, disclosed Tuesday in the firm’s third-quarter results, freed up $19.3 million, comprising $16.6 million from the Ethereum sale and $2.7 million from offloading half its Solana.

That covers nearly half of the $40 million share-repurchase program the Jersey-based firm’s board approved in October, and the company was explicit that the proceeds are being “reallocating that capital towards share repurchases.”

CEO Jean-Marie Mognetti framed it as capital discipline rather than a bearish call. “We took the same approach to preparing for the share repurchase program,” he said, noting the sales give the company “the flexibility to act when conditions allow.”

In other words, CoinShares is converting idle treasury crypto into dry powder to buy back its own stock, which it can do under an authorization allowing repurchases of up to 25% of shares outstanding over five years.

The timing is notable. The sale lands during a brutal stretch for crypto markets. Bitcoin was trading around $63,200 on Wednesday, down from six-figure levels earlier in the cycle, while Ethereum sat near $1,770 and Solana around $74, both well off their recent highs.

Spot Ethereum ETFs, meanwhile, have bled cash for days, shedding $29.1 million on Nov. 17 to extend a multi-day outflow streak that has turned Decrypt’s ETF sentiment gauge “bearish.”

Those are precisely the risk-off conditions that have battered Bitcoin treasury firms and pressured the broader sector. For a company whose stock is poised to join the Russell indices in December, deploying capital into buybacks at depressed prices is a conventional play, even if the optics of a crypto manager selling crypto invite a second look.

In terms of scale, these were small corporate treasury positions, not client assets. CoinShares’ total assets under management recovered to $7.4 billion by quarter-end, up from $5.5 billion three months earlier, and its core business of digital-asset investment products is untouched by the treasury move.

The firm actually swung to a strong quarter, posting $39.6 million in net income as net inflows accelerated to $125 million and its lending and trading desks rebounded.

So the sale isn’t CoinShares souring on Ethereum or Solana, assets it still manages billions in on behalf of clients. It’s a treasury-management decision by a newly profitable, soon-to-be-index-listed company choosing to return capital to shareholders rather than let a modest crypto position sit on its balance sheet.

#5148. Block Says Most Bitcoin Payments on Square Are for Coffee and Food

Most bitcoin payments at Square sellers are small, everyday purchases at coffee shops and restaurants, Block said in a new report that it hopes will inform a debate in Congress over a tax break for small crypto purchases.

The report, “The State of Bitcoin Payments,” covers completed bitcoin payments at U.S. Square sellers from Nov. 1, 2025, to Oct. 15, 2026. The median payment was about $13, and the average was about $29.

Food and drink businesses took 72% of those payments, Block found. That is a bigger share than the category’s 67.5% slice of regular card payments on Square over the same period.

“Most bitcoin payments are small and routine,” the company wrote on X Thursday, calling the report one of the first datasets on how people spend bitcoin in the local economy.

Coffee shops and fast food businesses accounted for 62.6% of the bitcoin payments in the food and drink category, with a median payment of $9.10. Grocery stores made up a smaller share but had the highest average payment in the group, at $60.73.

Small tickets dominated. About 42% of bitcoin payments on Square were under $10, and 65.1% were under $20. Those sub-$20 payments made up just 19.1% of total payment value, though, with a few larger purchases accounting for much of the dollars spent.

Customers also came back. Among Cash App users who paid with bitcoin at least twice at the same merchant, the median was three purchases per customer-merchant pair. About 44% of bitcoin payments from Cash App were repeat payments, and 86% of those came within 30 days.

The activity was not limited to Block’s own app. External Lightning wallets such as Strike, Wallet of Satoshi, Phoenix and Coinbase handled 56.6% of Square bitcoin payments and about 66% of payment value, while Cash App wallets made up the rest.

Square sellers can choose how much of each sale they keep in bitcoin, and Square converts the rest to dollars automatically. Block said zero-fee processing for bitcoin payments on Square has been extended through Dec. 31, 2027. Local governments are testing similar setups, with Miami-Dade County now taking bitcoin for property taxes and converting it to dollars.

Block tied the data to tax policy. Under current U.S. federal rules, paying with bitcoin generally means calculating a capital gain or loss on every purchase, even a $10 coffee, the company said.

A Morning Consult survey that Block commissioned found that 23.6% of businesses that don’t accept bitcoin cite tax and accounting complexity as a reason. That is about the same share that cite regulation, refunds or price swings. Among businesses that have considered taking bitcoin but haven’t, the figure was 27%.

Consumers pointed to friction, too. Morning Consult surveyed 8,669 U.S. adults who are aware of bitcoin in August. Among those who don’t own it, 29.6% said they would be more likely to pay with bitcoin if it were easier, and 15.7% of that group said they were unsure how it is taxed or regulated.

Block said Congress is weighing a crypto tax package and pointed to a de minimis exemption, which would exempt small gains on qualifying purchases, as one option. Industry groups have pressed lawmakers on crypto tax rules for months, with little movement so far.

The report doesn’t give totals. Block said the number of Square bitcoin payments, the number of sellers receiving them and total bitcoin payment volume are proprietary and not disclosed. Merchant lists like the 425 Los Angeles businesses that accept bitcoin offer a rough sense of reach.

“We think tax policy should account for how people spend bitcoin as well as how they hold it,” Block said.

#5144. Google's Genie 4 Is Here, Letting Ultra Subscribers Build AI Worlds in Real Time

The company announced Wednesday that its latest world model is rolling out through Project Genie, a browser-based experiment for generating interactive environments. Genie 4 was unveiled in October, but access until now had been limited to trusted testers.

Instead of rendering a finished video from a prompt, Genie generates the scene as someone explores it. With the fourth model, users can type instructions midway through a session to change the weather, introduce a character, or steer what's happening without starting over.

Genie 4 also creates sound alongside the visuals, from wind and footsteps to dialogue that reacts to events in the scene. Google says the model improves the way people move and objects respond to physical forces, though those claims come from its own demonstrations rather than independent testing.

Genie has come a long way from the side-scrolling experiments Google showed in 2024. The first version learned from more than 200,000 hours of gameplay footage and could turn a single image into a simple playable 2D environment, as Decrypt reported at the time.

Those early sessions lasted just 60 seconds, and character controls or physical behavior could be unreliable. Google is now keeping Genie 3 available alongside the new model, with sessions extended to three minutes, support for 360-degree panorama images, and the ability to connect multiple generated worlds into longer journeys.

That three-minute limit applies to the updated Genie 3 experience; Google did not specify a comparable session length for Genie 4 in Wednesday's announcement. Users can choose between the models depending on whether they want Genie 4's stronger visuals and audio or Genie 3's longer explorations.

The technology is not the same as a traditional game engine that builds a stable level with fully defined rules. Genie predicts what a world should look and sound like as it changes.

Google acknowledges that objects can drift or change between views, complex physics remain difficult, and generated speech or text may be inaccurate. It says Project Genie's audio and video carry SynthID watermarks and that inputs and outputs undergo safety screening.

#5143. Bitcoin Price Crashes to $59,000 as Market Selloff Deepens

The bitcoin price crashed below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since early July, falling about $1,800 in roughly an hour as a deepening U.S. tech stock selloff dragged risk assets lower.

Bitcoin traded near $61,400 at 9:40 a.m. ET and slid to about $59,600 by 10:45 a.m., a drop of nearly 3%, according to Look Into Bitcoin data. The bitcoin price is down about $3,400, or more than 5%, from the same time Thursday, when it traded near $63,000.

The break of $60,000 is a key psychological level, and it came with little warning. Bitcoin price had held above $61,000 for most of the morning, even after a third straight daily loss.

Wall Street opened sharply lower. The Nasdaq Composite fell more than 2% in morning trading as shares of Anthropic extended losses in its second day since a record Nasdaq debut. Nvidia and AMD also traded lower.

Bitcoin has been moving with tech stocks. The market has questioned whether heavy spending on artificial intelligence will pay off, and Friday’s open did nothing to ease that worry.

Rate expectations are adding pressure. Traders see a roughly 94% chance the Federal Reserve raises rates in December, CME Group’s FedWatch tool showed. Higher rates tend to hurt bitcoin and other assets that pay no yield.

Earlier Friday, South Korea’s Kospi index fell more than 5% and chipmakers sank across Asia. That selling carried into U.S. trading and then into bitcoin.

Some analysts had flagged $60,000 as a floor. Katie Stockton of Fairlead Strategies told CNBC the bottom of bitcoin’s trading range sits “just below 60,000,” and that it “appears to be trying to find a footing.” Friday’s drop tests that view.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said this week the drop is a “continuation of the existing bear market.” His base case is range trading around $60,000 to $90,000 through much of 2027.

JUST IN: Bitcoin falls below $60,000 for the first time since June.BTC is now less than 4% away from a new cycle low 👀 pic.twitter.com/GSZkbGdzZz

Funds have been sellers. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) lost $541.1 million on Thursday, its biggest one-day outflow on record. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $582 million in total, extending a streak of heavy withdrawals this week.

Leveraged traders were hit as well. More than $700 million in crypto positions were liquidated in the 24 hours before the latest drop, and about 86% were bets on higher prices. The slide below $60,000 likely forced out more of them.

The move extends a month-long slump. Bitcoin has dropped roughly $20,000 in the past month, a slide tracked as the price fell under $62,000 on Thursday.

Crypto stocks fell even faster. Strategy (MSTR) was down about 7% near $79.85, a day after it tumbled for a sixth straight session. Coinbase (COIN) fell about 3% to $105.36. Shares of Strive (ASST) fell to $15.31 Friday morning, down 21.47%, or $4.19, over the past five days. The stock has slid steadily from near $19.50 as bitcoin price’s sell-off deepened, with its sharpest drop in Friday’s trading.

Bitcoin miners took the worst of it. MARA Holdings (MARA) dropped about 11% to $5.55. Riot Platforms (RIOT) lost about 11% to $15.85, and CleanSpark (CLSK) fell about 12% to $5.13.

Data center operators that also mine bitcoin were hit hard. IREN fell about 12% to $12.62, and Cipher Digital (CIFR) dropped 14% to $5.58. Both have traded with AI stocks, which are also under pressure.

The bitcoin price is now at its lowest since the first days of July. Bitcoin set its record above $126,000 in October 2025, so it has lost more than half its value from that peak.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $59,600.

#5138. Palmer Luckey Says He’s Building an Offshore Haven for Industry—And 'Illegal' Medicine

Palmer Luckey Says He’s Building an Offshore Haven for Industry—And 'Illegal' Medicine

Palmer Luckey, the billionaire founder of Oculus and defense technology company Anduril, is spending over a billion dollars to have a private island where he can do things without worrying about U.S. laws.

He wants to build a massive industrial platform in the ocean where American businesses can operate without some of the regulations they face on land. And he has apparently been working on the idea for nearly a decade.

In an X post published Wednesday, Luckey revealed a project called SLAB, a proposed seastead with a 1,000-year design life that he says could support industries ranging from metal processing and oil refining to medical research and treatment.

Selling Oculus to Facebook in 2014 gave me money for helicopter flight lessons and developing a seastead called SLAB with a 1,000 year design life.

The goal was to be cheaper than land and absent state/local laws that cripple critical American industries like metal processing,… pic.twitter.com/aJkd0eOgLF

— Palmer Luckey (@PalmerLuckey) November 18, 2026

His goal, he wrote, is to create an industrial base cheaper than land and free from state and local laws that he believes are crippling critical American industries. Luckey described the project as a way to strengthen the United States rather than escape it, calling it a "relief valve" for outdated regulations.

“Me and a few close friends cashed it up at a $1B valuation back in 2017, and while it still has a long way to go, there has been significant progress towards my goal of reviving manifest destiny and westward expansion,” he wrote.

Luckey said he used proceeds from Facebook's 2014 acquisition of Oculus to finance the project.

The announcement included an AI-generated illustration of a gigantic offshore platform, rather than photographs of an operating facility so it’s hard to know which type of endeavor he’s dedicating his time and money to.

Luckey said he decided to disclose SLAB after learning that journalists were preparing to describe it as a floating AI data center. He rejected that characterization as incomplete, saying the project was considerably older and "way cooler than that."

Luckey provided a more specific indication of his ambitions in a heated exchange with journalist Joshua Topolsky, who questioned whether creating an offshore community was a sensible way to modernize medical research.

Topolsky argued that regulatory reform could happen without constructing an island in the ocean and challenged Luckey to identify which existing rules had prevented him from pursuing medical advances.

Luckey responded that he would rather build a place where people could save their families with "safe but currently illegal medicine ASAP" than wait for lawmakers to reform the system.

He accused political interests and large pharmaceutical companies of obstructing change, arguing that conventional reform would require legislation capable of securing support from 60 senators.

Luckey's plan also faces a significant legal complication: moving an operation into the ocean does not automatically place it outside American jurisdiction.

When another X user asked how close SLAB could operate to San Francisco without falling under its jurisdiction, Luckey answered, "Three miles."

That generally corresponds to the boundary of California's state-controlled coastal waters, according to the U.S. Bureau of Ocean Energy Management.

That said, federal jurisdiction extends considerably farther. The United States has a 12-nautical-mile territorial sea and a 200-nautical-mile exclusive economic zone, where it retains authority over certain economic activities and the construction and use of artificial islands, according to NOAA guidance.

The applicable rules would depend on SLAB's location, whether it floats or is attached to the seabed, and how it is registered. Even vessels operating on the high seas remain subject to applicable maritime laws and the jurisdiction of their flag states, so it’s not as if he’d be living in an anarchist fairy tale.

Medical research presents its own complications, if that’s what he’s actually doing. The FDA requires foreign clinical studies submitted in support of U.S. drug approvals to meet good clinical practice standards, including independent ethical review and informed consent. Conducting treatment offshore would not, by itself, make an unapproved drug legal for sale in the United States.

Previous seasteading experiments have encountered similar jurisdictional disputes. A floating community off Thailand triggered a confrontation with authorities in 2019, while a subsequent project in Panama adopted boat registration to operate within an existing legal framework.

This is all, of course, speculation. Luckey has not publicly identified SLAB's proposed location, the specific regulations it would avoid, or the medical treatments he hopes to make available. He has also not announced a construction deadline or provided independently verifiable details of the project's current physical development.

#5135. U.S. Treasury Secretary Pushes Back on Bigger Fed Hike

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent pushed back Friday on calls for a bigger Federal Reserve rate hike, even as bitcoin broke below $60,000 for the first time since July and stocks sank on bets that the Fed will keep tightening into a selloff.

Bessent told CNBC he would encourage the Fed not to embrace a half-point increase at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. He cited the energy supply shock and a drop in crude prices as reasons for the central bank to hold back.

He argued that one governor now backing a 50 basis point hike had wanted a similar cut within the last year. Bessent said it “seems somewhat peripatetic” to want the bigger increase only 11 or 12 months later. He did not name the governor.

“I would encourage the Fed to look forward, not backwards,” Bessent said.

The comments came two days after Fed Governor Christopher Waller told CNBC a half-point hike is probably necessary in December and that the central bank may need to “shock people.” Waller said inflation is “headed in the wrong direction, and it’s spreading out.”

Waller is not alone. Minutes of the October meeting showed nearly all officials expect a quarter-point hike in December, and two floated a larger one. Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee also declined to rule out a bigger move on Thursday.

“We’ve now had multiple months in a row of borderline disturbingly high inflation rates with little progress in the sectors where we would expect to be seeing some progress,” Goolsbee told reporters. “So everything’s always on the table.”

Not everyone wants to go bigger. New York Fed President John Williams said Tuesday that “I don’t think the situation calls for dramatic action.” St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem has suggested as many as four hikes by the spring of 2027.

The Fed raised rates in September for the first time in more than three years and held them steady in October. Inflation has stayed hot since the Iran strikes pushed up energy prices earlier this year.

Citi still expects a quarter-point increase in December. Global chief economist Nathan Sheets said Thursday he does not see a need for 50 basis points despite persistent inflation. In a note Friday, the bank wrote that “Fed officials will consequently have little appetite to hike into an equity sell-off.”

Citi expects the Fed to hold after December and begin cutting later in 2027. Traders agree on the next step: the CME Group’s FedWatch tool showed a more than 98% chance of a quarter-point hike, and the 10-year Treasury yield fell more than 10 basis points to 4.95% as investors bought bonds.

Stocks fell for another day. The Nasdaq Composite dropped more than 2% in morning trading, and the VIX volatility index topped 30, its highest level since March.

Bitcoin traded near $59,700 shortly before 11 a.m. ET, down more than 5% on the day, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro. It broke below $60,000 for the first time since early July and is on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, the longest streak since 2022.

Higher rates are a headwind for assets that pay no yield, and the selloff has reached companies that hold bitcoin. Strategy’s stock fell for a sixth straight session on Thursday, and the bitcoin price has dropped roughly 25% in a month, a slide Bitcoin Magazine tracked earlier this week.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill tied the outlook to inflation in a note dated Nov. 18. “Our base case remains range trading around USD 60k to 90k through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives monetary policy room to ease,” he said.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $59,300.

#5133. Wintermute policy chief calls for crypto lobby to ‘clean house’ in D.C.

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The call comes as banks and lawmakers push back against the SEC's tokenization exemption.

Wintermute's head of policy and advocacy, Ron Hammond, urged the crypto industry to "clean" its house in Washington following what he called a series of lobbying setbacks amid policymaking.

Speaking at the Capital Crypto Forum on Thursday, Hammond said the industry has struggled to counter larger financial interests as tokenization brings crypto and traditional finance into direct competition.

"I would say we probably need to clean house a little bit on the policy side here," Hammond said. "There's been fractures in the industry that show that either A, we're not united enough, or B, when we go up against a larger force, we just don't win”

His comments come a month after the Securities and Exchange Commission in September introduced a five-year innovation exemption that opened a route for trading tokenized U.S. stocks.

However, the policy has drawn resistance from both traditional finance companies and lawmakers, with Hammud also having some reservations.

"We see it being really limited and tailored in its final form," he said. " But effectively, even for companies like us, it doesn't really do much good for us. I mean, the limits are pretty strong. So that's why we're saying it's a great first step, but we have a lot more ways to go here."

Dozens of major companies, including JPMorgan, Goldman Sachs, Wells Fargo, and McDonald's, have objected to third-party tokenized versions of their shares under the SEC's exemption process.

"It's weird to see in New York, we are partnering with a lot of these firms that I'm lobbying against here in D.C. [...]," Hammond said. "We partner with the banks all the time, but in D.C. we are against them on a lot of fronts."

Hammond also criticized crypto's choice of political allies, saying some lawmakers who campaigned as pro-crypto later opposed tokenization.

Earlier this week, four Democratic senators, including Angela Alsobrooks, Ruben Gallego, Mark Warner and Catherine Cortez Masto, warned SEC Chair Paul Atkins that the exemption left gaps around know-your-customer and anti-money-laundering requirements.

The SEC's tokenization exemption is also facing questions from within its incoming leadership. On Thursday, Kristin Johnson, Trump's Democratic pick for the commission, said at a Senate Banking Committee hearing that she would welcome a formal rulemaking process with public comment, given concerns raised by those opposing the rule.

Meanwhile, the industry also faces competition from other jurisdictions, Hammond said, as officials seek to attract digital-asset businesses.

"When I go over to the UK, they love to hear any moment that America has a gap. Like when Clarity failed, they were all asking me, 'What can we do to get these firms back to the UK?'"

#5131. ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Grok Helped Plan Terror Attacks in Government Tests

ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Grok Helped Plan Terror Attacks in Government Tests

Four of the most widely used AI chatbots helped government investigators develop potentially deadly attack plans in less than 12 hours, despite safeguards intended to stop exactly that kind of request.

OpenAI's ChatGPT, Anthropic's Claude, Google's Gemini and Grok all supplied usable information during covert testing by the Government Accountability Office, according to a report released Wednesday. Investigators posed as attackers, then checked the responses against real locations, available materials, and expert assessments.

Two systems assisted with plans involving improvised explosives in crowded U.S. cities. Two others supplied information for attacks on electrical infrastructure, according to the watchdog, which withheld the models' assignments and operational details to prevent copycat attempts.

Senator Maggie Hassan, the New Hampshire Democrat who requested the investigation, warned that a terrorist could obtain "a concrete playbook for how to carry out a deadly attack" in less than 12 hours, according to her statement.

The chatbots initially activated protections, including refusing requests, removing harmful information or suspending accounts. Investigators bypassed those barriers with common jailbreak techniques, which manipulate an AI system into ignoring its safety rules.

The GAO checked suggested locations, researched whether materials were available and consulted specialists about the plans' feasibility. Some answers contained errors, including misplaced roads on a map, and investigators did not construct explosives or execute an attack.

In the report, "viable" means somebody would likely be able to follow the information supplied, not that an attack was demonstrated to succeed. Each model was tested on one assigned scenario, and the agency said the results were not statistically representative or suitable for ranking the companies.

OpenAI and Google said the tests involved older models, while Anthropic acknowledged that determined users can sometimes extract harmful material. The companies described continuing improvements to safety systems, though the GAO concluded those protections failed in its tests.

This is not really new. The findings follow a previous research showing eight of 10 popular chatbots assisted simulated teenage users planning violent attacks. The separate study tested 720 responses and found the bots provided actionable help roughly three-quarters of the time.

The GAO findings arrive amid outages affecting banks, Microsoft services and the National Security Agency. Officials say the disruptions were separate incidents, not evidence of a coordinated AI-driven cyberattack, even as the rush to repair software flaws has created its own problems.

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton connected the NSA's outage to patching work. Officials described the recent breakdowns as maintenance-related, although individual organizations offered different explanations for their failures.

AI tools now find vulnerabilities faster than human teams can test and deploy fixes. Last month, the AI-assisted discovery of critical PHP flaws pushed website operators into an urgent patching campaign; the software powers a large share of the web.

The sheer volume is becoming another problem. VulnCheck data reveals there have been more than 100,000 software vulnerabilities logged this year by November 13, more than twice the total for 2025. Engineers still have to evaluate and deploy fixes without breaking the systems they are meant to protect.

That acceleration cuts both ways. Defenders gain powerful tools for finding weaknesses, but hasty repairs can disrupt essential systems, and malicious users may gain faster access to attack planning.

Hassan said she will make AI safety legislation an early priority when she takes over the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committee in January. She plans hearings examining risks to public safety and critical infrastructure; the GAO itself made no formal policy recommendations.

The Digital Asset Tax Certainty Act, introduced by Rep. Jason Smith, passed the House Ways and Means Committee in September and includes most provisions from the PARITY Act, Horsford said.

Rep. Steven Horsford, D-Nev., said he wants the House to take up a bipartisan crypto tax bill before this Congress ends, after speaking with Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith, R-Mo., about its prospects.

"I talked to Chairman Smith earlier this week. He agreed that we have the opportunity to get our bipartisan bill through the House," Horsford said Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum.

Horsford said the bill should be brought up in the House before this Congress ends, but did not give a date for a vote.

Under Smith's committee-approved bill, a person using crypto to pay a qualifying network or transaction fee of no more than $10 would not recognize a gain or loss on that payment. The bill also extends wash-sale rules to traded digital assets.

Horsford said after the committee vote in September that the bill would tax mining and staking rewards as ordinary income but still did not settle when that income should be recognized.

#5128. Researchers Publish Security Analysis of Bitcoin’s Post-Quantum SHRINCS Proposal

Several researchers have published a 131-page security analysis of SHRINCS, a proposed post-quantum signature scheme for Bitcoin, and are asking the wider community to look for flaws in it. Researcher Mikhail Kudinov announced the first version Thursday in a thread on X, saying the paper reduces SHRINCS’ security to the properties of its underlying hash function.

The analysis, dated Nov. 18, estimates classical security of 121.4 to 122.7 bits for SHRINCS, though those figures rest on assumptions that include three unproven conjectures. Kudinov said the work is not finished. The team is still working on a new analysis of a property called SM-DSPR, whose bound has so far been conjectured, and on tightening a reprogramming step and improving a bound against quantum attackers.

SHRINCS is a hash-based scheme, meaning its security rests on hash functions rather than the elliptic curve math behind today’s Bitcoin signatures. It places two designs under one key: a stateful part built on FXMSS with WOTS+C, and a stateless fallback built on SLH-DSA. Because both use the same hash function, Kudinov said, the proof has to cover both.

Signature size is the main cost. Under the draft proposal, stateful SHRINCS signatures run from 548 to 4,619 bytes, while the stateless fallback is 5,777 bytes. Schnorr signatures on Bitcoin today are 64 bytes, and ECDSA signatures typically come in around 71. Signatures take up scarce block space, so larger ones mean fewer transactions per block.

The compact stateful path carries a danger. It relies on one-time keys, and if a wallet accidentally reuses one, an attacker can forge a signature for that address and steal the funds. Wallet software therefore has to track the state of every key. The stateless path avoids the problem, but at a much larger size.

Jonas Nick, Blockstream’s director of research, told Isabel Foxen Duke, head of the MARA Foundation, on MARA Foundation TV that wallets must automatically fall back to the stateless path whenever a state counter could be compromised. “It needs to be impossible for the user to behave in a way that this catastrophic event happens,” he said. “Wallet developers need to deal with this stateful component correctly.”

Nick also warned that heavy use of the larger signatures would weigh on the network. “We don’t know what the fee market will look like once we have these very big signatures,” he said. “And it’s not just the fee market, it’s also just fewer transactions fit into a block, meaning fewer people can use Bitcoin. And I think this changes the value proposition of Bitcoin.”

The authors are clear that the analysis needs scrutiny. Kudinov said it has not been sufficiently peer reviewed, and the project’s GitHub repository warns that the documents may contain errors and should not be the sole basis for deploying SHRINCS. “We tried to be careful, but a proof of this size can have mistakes,” he wrote, asking readers to open an issue if they find one.

The team also produced two results that are not specific to SHRINCS: a classical bound for a property called ITSR without reprogramming, and a quadratic quantum bound for undetectability. Kudinov said they also apply to SPHINCS+ and SLH-DSA, the hash-based schemes SHRINCS borrows from, and that they were published as separate papers.

The SHRINCS work lands as quantum risk climbs the agenda. A global securities regulators’ group this week warned that moving blockchains to quantum-resistant cryptography may be especially hard, and researchers recently built a post-quantum defense for the Lightning Network after Bitcoin’s first quantum-safe transaction was shown without a fork.

The draft Bitcoin Improvement Proposal for SHRINCS is also open for comment. “We need all the possible feedback — engineers, cryptographers, protocol developers,” Kudinov, a post-quantum cryptography researcher at Blockstream, said of it. “There are a lot of things to check, test, complain about, or praise.”

#5126. Ledger Confirms Tampered Bitcoin Wallets in Berlin as Spy Implant Probe Spreads Beyond Asia

Ledger Confirms Tampered Bitcoin Wallets in Berlin as Spy Implant Probe Spreads Beyond Asia

The tampered Ledger wallets aren’t just a Southeast Asia problem anymore.

Ledger said Wednesday it has confirmed tampered hardware wallets in Berlin and is investigating reports of two more in Dubai, expanding a probe it had previously described as a localized incident in Southeast Asia.

“Our findings since then show tampered devices outside Southeast Asia. It is the same incident,” the company said in an update on its support site.

The investigation began last month after customers who bought devices from reseller CryptoBilis reported losses. Onchain investigators traced a suspected drain of about $87 million from hundreds of victim wallets across Ethereum, Tron, and Bitcoin.

Ledger said at the time that all confirmed cases were tied to CryptoBilis, and it later asked resellers in seven Asian markets to pull devices from sale.

Every device Ledger has confirmed shows the same kind of physical modification, designed to capture a user’s recovery phrase during setup. A recovery phrase, typically a string of 24 words, is the master key to a crypto wallet: anyone who has it can drain the funds.

Our investigation into the hardware implant incident we disclosed Oct 15th is ongoing, and we published an update: https://t.co/wx6mfQPr99

There is no flaw in Ledger's secure chip or operating system, and no confirmed case involving a device bought on https://t.co/vQ4QWa1D0f

Ledger maintains there’s no flaw in its secure chip or operating system and no confirmed case involving a device bought on ledger.com.

In Germany, Ledger said a few tampered units were localized to Berlin, and it urged anyone who bought a Ledger device there since January 2026 to contact support.

In Dubai, it’s working with distribution partners to learn how two suspected units entered circulation. Those devices were bought at a Virgin Megastore location, according to multiple reports. Ledger also found one affected Nano Gen5 among the Southeast Asian resellers it previously paused.

The company expects more tampered devices to surface, though it says the number remains small overall. Its Donjon security team is examining suspected devices, and Ledger has asked resellers to pause sales wherever inspections are ongoing.

Customers worried about a device should stop using it, Ledger said. It advises moving funds to a new wallet created on a device bought directly from ledger.com, without reusing the old recovery phrase or passphrase, since a tampered device can capture both. It also recommends filing a report with local authorities.

For a limited time, Ledger is offering physical inspections, including X-rays, at its factory in Vierzon, France.

“We will never ask for your recovery phrase,” the company said on X.

The last few months have been a rough stretch for hardware wallet markers overall. Rival manufacturer Trezor said this week that hackers who breached a third-party shipping partner likely took information tied to about 27,000 orders. That’s the company’s third such breach in as many months.

#5124. Singapore settles $77.5M tokenized bill sale with wholesale CBDC

MAS issued the bills directly on its test network, which it plans to rename SG Digital Ledger and use for settlement beyond the trials.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) issued 100 million Singapore dollars ($77.5 million) in tokenized bills on Nov. 6, with banks using wholesale central bank digital currency (CBDC) to pay for them.

Five banks submitted bids totaling more than twice the amount on offer, MAS said Thursday. The four-week bills mature on Dec. 4.

MAS issued the bills directly on the Singapore Dollar Test Network. That gives borrowers an asset they could pledge as collateral for loans on the network, the central bank said.

Singapore began experimenting with blockchain-based bank payments in 2016. In 2025, banks used the test network and MAS-issued digital currency to settle overnight loans.

MAS now plans to turn the Singapore Dollar Test Network, where the bills were issued, into a settlement system for financial institutions, Managing Director Chia Der Jiun said Thursday at the Singapore FinTech Festival. The network will be renamed SG Digital Ledger.

“As the use of tokenized assets and digital money grows, trusted settlement on CBDC will be needed,” Chia said. “It underpins settlement finality and confidence, especially for large value settlement.”

In 2027, MAS plans to issue more tokenized bills, bring more financial institutions into the trials and expand the bills’ use as collateral.

#5122. SEC Freezes Assets in $560 Million Crypto Trading Ponzi Scheme

The Securities and Exchange Commission has frozen the assets of Indianapolis-based Backward Hat Capital and its two co-founders, saying the firm ran a $560 million Ponzi scheme that told investors their money would be turned into crypto and used for AI-driven currency trading.

The agency said Backward Hat, co-founders Phillip Williams and Devin Johnson, Johnson’s firm Traders Point Capital and Joseph Anderson raised the money from more than 2,000 investors starting in July 2022. A federal court granted a temporary restraining order, an asset freeze and expedited discovery to halt what the SEC called an ongoing scheme.

Federal prosecutors in New York and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission brought parallel cases. The SEC and CFTC suits were filed in the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of Indiana, and the CFTC said the court has appointed Kevin B. Duff as temporary receiver to take control of the companies and the individual defendants’ assets.

According to the SEC’s complaint, Williams and Johnson sold limited partnership interests in private funds through a network of promoters, including to Williams’s former investment advisory clients. They promised a proprietary, “AI-driven” algorithm that supposedly returned at least 6% every month by trading foreign exchange.

Investor money, the pitch went, would first be converted into a crypto asset and then used for that arbitrage. The complaint does not name bitcoin as the asset. It says about $109 million of the roughly $210 million raised from July 2022 to September 2025 was sent to the fund’s Coinbase account and converted.

Of that, more than $56 million went to personal crypto accounts held by Williams, Johnson and a relief defendant, according to the complaint. About $40 million ended up on three platforms where some of it was used to trade other crypto assets, but little to no money was used to trade FX. After October 2025, the scheme raised about $350 million, and none of it was sent to Coinbase.

The SEC said the defendants made more than $250 million in Ponzi payments to earlier investors and moved more than $220 million to themselves. The money paid for exotic cars, expensive homes, private air travel and other businesses they owned, and helped feed Johnson’s gambling. Promoters took more than $50 million.

Anderson is accused of producing fake financial reports, including at least two “audit reports” and a “quality of earnings” report from a supposedly independent firm. The SEC said the documents falsely claimed the returns had been verified against bank and brokerage records. It said Anderson did no audit work, was not independent and had a financial stake in the scheme.

Prosecutors in Manhattan charged Williams and Johnson with securities fraud, conspiracy to commit wire fraud and conspiracy to commit securities fraud. The FBI arrested Williams in Kokomo, Indiana, and Johnson in Gilbert, Arizona, on Wednesday. Prosecutors put the total raised at more than $600 million.

“As alleged, Phillip Patrick Williams and Devin Allen Johnson promised to use investor funds for profitable foreign exchange currency trading,” said U.S. Attorney Jamie McDonald. “Williams and Johnson instead stole hundreds of millions of dollars from their investors for personal expenses and to pay off earlier investors in a Ponzi-like fashion.”

The CFTC put the Ponzi at more than $550 million from at least 2,100 participants and said defendants kept soliciting money through at least October 2026. Its complaint seeks restitution, disgorgement, civil penalties, trading bans and a permanent injunction. The SEC is seeking injunctions, disgorgement and civil penalties.

The case follows other recent enforcement actions tied to crypto-branded fraud. The SEC charged two companies in a $130 million crypto trading scheme last month, and New York sought a $1.37 billion judgment against the operators of the NovaTech Ponzi scheme. In August, the CFTC charged Goliath Ventures with a $400 million bitcoin fraud.

Anne McKinley, acting associate director of the SEC’s Chicago Regional Office, said the promise of “a low-risk, high rate of return on a purportedly complex investment should make all potential investors stop and think before investing their money.”

#5120. IOSCO warns quantum upgrades could slow blockchains, leave security gaps

A Solana test network ran about 90% slower with quantum-resistant signatures, and wallets that keep using older keys could remain vulnerable.

The International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) warned that upgrading blockchains to withstand quantum attacks could slow transactions and raise fees.

In a report released Thursday, IOSCO said some quantum-resistant digital signatures take up far more space than those used to authorize transactions today. Larger signatures mean fewer transactions can fit in each block, which could increase congestion, push up fees and require more storage.

IOSCO cited an experiment in which a test version of Solana ran about 90% slower with quantum-resistant signatures.

No known quantum computer can break the cryptography that secures these networks today. IOSCO urged firms to start preparing now because the transition could take years.

IOSCO said layer-1 networks use cryptography both to authorize transactions and to reach agreement on which blocks to add. Even after an upgrade, funds in dormant wallets and applications could remain vulnerable if they still rely on older keys.

Layer-2 networks can use quantum-resistant proofs to verify transactions and still depend on vulnerable systems to settle those transactions, move assets between chains or approve upgrades.

In April, Bitcoin advocate Samson Mow warned that larger post-quantum signatures could reduce Bitcoin’s throughput and that rushed fixes might introduce new vulnerabilities.

In October, Starknet’s community backed research into becoming an independent, quantum-resistant layer-1 network, following concerns over its reliance on Ethereum’s security upgrade timeline.

Ethereum researcher Justin Drake has since outlined a 2028 post-quantum transition target, ahead of the network’s existing 2029 roadmap.

#5118. Bitcoin Struggles to Hold $64,000 as ETF Outflows Hit $2.9B in November

Bitcoin Struggles to Hold $64,000 as ETF Outflows Hit $2.9B in November

Bitcoin is struggling to hold the $64,000 level as investors continue to pull money from spot Bitcoin ETFs and brace for another interest rate hike from the Federal Reserve.

The leading cryptocurrency was trading at around $63,800 on Thursday, down 1% on the day and 13% over the past week, according to Decrypt data. It has hovered between roughly $63,000 and $65,000 since the weekend, briefly dipping below $63,000 and touching a 24-hour low of $62,068, per CoinGecko data.

"Bitcoin remains under selling pressure," Daniela Hathorn, senior market analyst at Capital.com, told Decrypt, noting that it has fallen roughly 26% from its late-September peak near $87,000. "Elevated Treasury yields, dollar strength and expectations of further Fed tightening are weighing on risk appetite, while the broader technology selloff has added to the pressure."

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $306.4 million in net outflows on Wednesday, after $239.9 million on Tuesday, according to SoSoValue data. The funds have seen outflows on nine of the past 10 trading days, with Monday's small $8 million inflow the only exception, and have lost about $2.9 billion so far in November. Their total net assets have fallen to $77.7 billion. Bitcoin miners have added to the selling, with major miners including Riot and MARA selling some of their holdings to fund a shift into AI infrastructure.

Monday's brief inflow "does not signal a market inflection point," Tim Sun, senior researcher at HashKey, told Decrypt. Net short positions in Bitcoin futures held by leveraged funds on CME have fallen to their lowest level since early 2023, and futures basis yields have narrowed, he said, indicating that hedge funds have largely unwound the basis trade that paired spot ETF purchases with short futures. That means the current outflows are mainly driven by investors actively cutting their Bitcoin exposure, Sun said.

"If macro expectations do not improve, investors may shift from buying the dip to selling into rallies, creating additional overhead resistance on subsequent rebounds," he added.

"Recent Bitcoin ETF outflows and leveraged liquidations" have amplified the decline, Hathorn said. "Despite stronger Nvidia earnings, the market has struggled to stage a significant rebound, suggesting confidence remains fragile. A sustained recovery may require renewed ETF inflows and a more supportive interest-rate outlook."

Some traders have also pointed the finger at Anthropic's upcoming IPO for drawing money away from crypto, though analysts have disputed that the listing is draining crypto liquidity, attributing it instead to ETF outflows, forced selling and rising interest rates.

Traders see a 90.7% chance that the Fed will raise rates by a quarter point at its December 9 meeting, and a 9.3% chance of a half-point hike, according to CME FedWatch. The odds of a half-point move have risen from 6.5% a day earlier. Fed Governor Christopher Waller has called for an even bigger, half-point increase, unless policymakers signal another rise in January.

Hathorn said $64,000 is the immediate level to watch, followed by support around $62,000 and the psychological $60,000 mark. A sustained recovery above $66,000 to $68,000 could ease selling pressure, "but the broader trend remains bearish while lower highs persist," she said.

Onchain analytics firm Glassnode said in its weekly report on Tuesday that the crash had pushed investors’ realized losses to levels seen only near past market lows in 2018, 2020, 2022 and early 2023, a sign of capitulation. Bitcoin is now holding a dense block of recent buyers between $62,000 and $66,000, which grew by about 66,000 BTC during the two days of heaviest selling, Glassnode said. But a daily close below that block could set off a fast drop toward the June low of $58,500, and the Realized Price, the average price at which all Bitcoin last moved, at $53,700.

Traders on Decrypt Predict see a 43% chance that Bitcoin will fall to $60,000 at some point before the end of November, and a 12% chance that it will drop as low as $55,000. They give just a 2% chance that it will climb back to $82,500 this month.

There are some signs of support. About 1.1 million BTC, roughly 5.5% of supply, last changed hands at prices between $60,000 and $64,000, and that band has grown by more than 25,000 BTC in recent days, according to Neil Steinhardt, head of Nexo U.S., in a note shared with Decrypt Wednesday. A dense base of holders with costs near current prices "can act as a support zone, though it is not a floor," he wrote.

Bitcoin has also held up better than gold. The amount of gold one Bitcoin buys rose above its 200-day average this week, to about 17.1 ounces, around 40% above its March low, Steinhardt said.

#5116. JPMorgan says bitcoin price correction widens gap to estimated $86,000 production cost

JPMorgan says the widening gap likely put more pressure on higher-cost miners to sell bitcoin reserves.

Bitcoin's recent price drop has widened the gap between its market price and JPMorgan's estimated production cost of $86,000, likely putting more pressure on higher-cost miners to sell bitcoin from their reserves.

Bitcoin was trading near $65,000 when JPMorgan analysts led by Nikolaos Panigirtzoglou published the report on Wednesday, about $21,000 below their central cost estimate. Bitcoin fell below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since July, widening that gap to more than $26,000.

The analysts said miners likely added to the selling as the gap widened. Higher-cost miners may have sold their bitcoin reserves to raise cash, continue operating or fund a move into AI, they said.

"That said, we believe the gap could narrow as the hashrate declines and mining difficulty adjusts as unprofitable miners exit the network," the analysts wrote.

The analysts said crypto prices fell sharply over the past two weeks as tech stocks slid further and investors revised their expectations for Fed rates.

Spot bitcoin and ether ETFs, along with leveraged ETFs linked to Michael Saylor's Strategy, saw large outflows in both October and November, similar to May and June, the analysts said. Weekly data showed that the outflows were heavier in the latest two weeks than in the previous four weeks. Equity ETFs, on the other hand, saw inflows.

The analysts said retail investors likely drove most of the crypto ETF outflows because institutional positioning in futures markets largely held up. "Our CME futures positioning proxies suggest that bitcoin positioning has declined modestly, while ethereum positioning remained relatively elevated, holding up even better than bitcoin," the analysts said.

As for retail investors, they may have sold crypto ETFs to fund stock ETF purchases, seeing better opportunities to buy the dip in equities, according to the analysts. They also pointed to a decline in stablecoin market capitalization as a sign that "some capital has also left the crypto market altogether."

The deal added 2,455 BTC to H100’s holdings. BitcoinTreasuries ranks H100 third among Europe’s publicly traded Bitcoin holders, behind Germany’s Bitcoin Group SE and France’s Capital B.

#5113. GameStop CEO Says Digital-Only Games Have ‘Hurt Consumers’ as GTA 6 Skips Discs

GameStop CEO Says Digital-Only Games Have ‘Hurt Consumers’ as GTA 6 Skips Discs

GameStop CEO Ryan Cohen has criticized video game publishers for abandoning physical discs, as Grand Theft Auto 6, one of the most anticipated games ever made, launches on Thursday as a digital download only.

"When you buy a game, you should be able to sell it, trade it or lend it to a friend," Cohen told Bloomberg. "Unquestionably, the decision to move to all digital has hurt consumers, and it's also hurt GameStop."

Cohen declined to name specific companies, but Take-Two Interactive is releasing the $80 game only as a download, with retailers selling boxes containing download codes. Asked about presales, Cohen said: "It's a code in a box. I mean, we're selling a lot of it."

Take-Two declined to comment to Bloomberg.

The shift hits a core part of GameStop's business. Pre-owned and refurbished products made up 22% of the retailer's sales, about $171 million, in the quarter that ended August 1, down 32% from a year earlier, according to Bloomberg.

Cohen said he has spoken with publishers, who he argued benefit from limiting trade-ins because used copies compete with new sales. "They're taking away competition," he said.

Sony announced in July that it will stop producing physical discs for new PlayStation games in January 2028, a move experts have said will hit low-income gamers hardest. PlayStation founder Ken Kutaragi has dismissed the backlash as a "mentality issue," while an Infinity Ward veteran said publishers have pushed to cut discs since 2007.

Cohen said he has met with Rep. Ritchie Torres (D-NY), who is backing the Power to the Players Act, which would require gaming companies to give consumers ongoing access to the titles they buy. "A game that can be taken away the moment a company pulls the plug is not a purchase. It is a rental disguised as a sale," Torres said in October.

In Europe, the Stop Killing Games campaign gathered 1.3 million signatures for a European Citizens' Initiative calling for publishers to keep games playable after support ends.

The question of whether buyers own digital games has also reached the courts. Sony has argued in court that digital games can't be owned, prompting some in the crypto industry to pitch blockchain-based ownership as a fix.

Cohen's GameStop has also made bets beyond gaming. The company bought 4,710 BTC for more than $500 million in 2025, and in March moved all but one of them into a covered call strategy on Coinbase Prime to generate income. In May, after eBay rejected its takeover offer, GameStop sought shareholder approval to raise its authorized share count to 2.5 billion as it built a stake in the online marketplace.

#5112. Binance hack laundering ramps up as $149 million in bitcoin mixed through coinjoins: Elliptic

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Attackers moved $860 million in ether from major wallets, swapping some into bitcoin before mixing it within hours.

Launderers allegedly linked to North Korea are now mixing tens of millions of dollars in bitcoin a day in a bid to make tracing the funds harder.

At least 2,310 BTC, worth around $149 million at the time, has been mixed through coinjoin transactions, with roughly 90% of that occurring since Tuesday, Elliptic said in a Friday update.

Coinjoins are a method of obfuscating the source of a bitcoin transaction by combining different users' transactions. This makes it harder to match senders' and recipients' coins.

This follows transfers of around 488,000 ETH, valued at $860 million, from five wallets. Most of this ether is now parked in new addresses, minus around $70 million that was swapped to bitcoin through THORChain. That BTC was subsequently mixed within hours.

The report's author, Tom Robinson, noted the coinjoins were organized by Kruw, a Wasabi Wallet coordinator. Coordinators arrange the transactions to be mixed but don't take custody of users' funds.

The 18,720 BTC stolen directly from Binance are still untouched. The bitcoins being mixed stem from swaps of other stolen assets like ETH, XRP and SOL.

Despite this, Elliptic said very little has likely been cashed out. The firm's latest tally puts about $2.35 billion in wallets linked to the theft, while $2.85 billion is frozen.

Separately, Binance said Wednesday that it obtained a $6.5 billion worldwide freeze order from the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts against the attackers. Additional court orders give issuers and exchanges the green light to return stolen assets that they've already frozen.

Binance previously described the October breach as a monthslong campaign involving malicious software, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that gave attackers access to the exchange's wallet-signing systems.

#5109. Rep. Casten seeks to curb prediction market insider trading with DOJ whistleblower program

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Reps. Casten and Lieu introduced a bill that would create a new program at the Justice Department where whistleblowers can report information anonymously.

Rep. Sean Casten, who could have a top spot in Congress next year, is clamping down on insider trading in prediction markets by incentivizing people to report information anonymously to the Justice Department, amid high-profile incidents involving governmental officials and military personnel.

On Friday, Casten and Rep. Ted Lieu introduced the Policing Insider Trading in Prediction Markets Act, which would create a new program at the Justice Department where whistleblowers can report information anonymously about fraud in prediction markets and could potentially be compensated if the information results in a successful enforcement action.

"Prediction markets have a serious insider trading problem, and the current Administration is engaged in corruption of epic proportions," Casten said in a statement. "By encouraging whistleblowers to come forward and report wrongdoing, we can take an important step in cracking down on insider trading to restore accountability and public confidence in the U.S. rule of law."

The news was earlier reported by Politico.

Over the past year, certain bets on prediction markets have drawn scrutiny from both Republicans and Democrats. On Thursday, the Senate passed the "Death Bets Act," which would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination and war.

Insider trading on these platforms has also emerged as an ongoing concern, particularly following reports that an active-duty U.S. Army soldier used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of the capture of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro. Subsequent cases have raised further concerns about government officials exploiting access to nonpublic information for financial gain through prediction markets, including allegations involving the White House's former teleprompter operator.

Political candidates have likewise faced allegations of insider trading on these platforms.

With Democrats now taking control of Congress in January, prediction markets are likely to garner more scrutiny. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., who is slated to chair the Senate Banking Committee, has said she would hone in on prediction markets. On the House side, it is not yet clear who will lead the House Financial Services Committee. Rep. Maxine Waters had been expected to take on that role, but has since been challenged by Casten.

#5107. Binance Wins $6.5B Freezing Order Over Hack That Can Be Served via the Blockchain

Binance Wins $6.5B Freezing Order Over Hack That Can Be Served via the Blockchain

Binance has secured a $6.5 billion worldwide freezing order against the hackers behind last month's attack on the exchange, which the FBI has blamed on North Korea.

The injunction was issued by the courts of the Abu Dhabi Global Market (ADGM) against the unknown perpetrators, Binance's lawyers at Gibson Dunn confirmed, according to City AM.

The order breaks new legal ground. It can be served directly via the blockchain, and market participants will be notified of a "living list" of 26,000 wallet addresses identified as holding stolen assets, Gibson Dunn partner Kendall Day said. That will enable "those assets to be frozen rapidly even as they are laundered at pace," Day said.

Binance has also secured proprietary injunctions and delivery-up orders, including against parties holding frozen assets who are not accused of wrongdoing, according to City AM. Decrypt has reached out to Gibson Dunn for comment.

"We will continue using every lawful tool available to trace, freeze and recover stolen assets, support our partners in returning funds where possible, and keep those assets out of the hands of the attackers," Dugan Bliss, Binance's deputy general counsel, said in a statement reported by Bloomberg.

The FBI has attributed the October 29 hack to TraderTraitor, a group linked to North Korea, which has denied involvement. The attackers gained access after an engineer was phished through a fake job offer, according to Bloomberg. The Secret Service has joined the FBI's investigation.

About $2.85 billion in funds connected to the hack has been frozen, including by Ripple and World Liberty Financial, according to Bloomberg. Stablecoin issuers Tether and Circle froze $2.7 billion shortly after the attack, and the Justice Department said last week it had obtained court authorization to seize or restrain $1.2 billion of that.

The hackers have used thousands of wallets and multiple blockchains to launder the rest, and wallets linked to the hack began moving Ethereum again this week after two weeks of inactivity.

Binance has said it fully covered the $6.3 billion in customer assets affected and absorbed the losses itself.

#5105. GTA VI publisher racks up nearly 3x Nvidia’s tokenized stock volume

Tokenized Take-Two shares drew $249.5 million in DEX volume Wednesday, nearly three times the $88.1 million for tokenized Nvidia shares.

Tokenized shares of Grand Theft Auto VI publisher Take-Two Interactive recorded nearly three times the trading volume of tokenized Nvidia shares on decentralized exchanges (DEXs) as the blockbuster began its global rollout.

Trading volume for tokenized Take-Two (TTWO) shares surged 578% to $249.5 million on Wednesday, up from $36.8 million a day earlier, according to Token Terminal data. Tokenized Nvidia (NVDA) recorded $88.1 million in DEX volume despite ranking first by market cap at $399.4 million.

The surge made TTWO the most-traded tokenized stock tracked by Token Terminal that day, accounting for roughly 42% of the $590.7 million in DEX volume.

DEX trading volume by reference stock on Wednesday. Source: Token Terminal

Tokenized stocks are blockchain-based assets that track the value of publicly traded shares, allowing investors to gain exposure through crypto trading platforms. Their onchain trading volumes do not represent transactions in the underlying shares on traditional stock exchanges.

Take-Two shares on the Nasdaq rose 6.2% to close Wednesday at $216.61, according to Yahoo Finance. Shares gave back some of those gains Thursday, falling 4% to $208 in premarket trading as of 7:02 a.m. ET.

GTA VI, which Citi analyst Jason Bazinet estimated cost about $1.4 billion to make, ends a 13-year wait for a sequel to GTA V. PlayStation and Microsoft’s Xbox both said it was the most preordered game in the history of their respective stores.

Most of the tokenized TTWO trading was in a Solana-based asset offered by Backpack Securities, a platform operated by crypto exchange Backpack. The token accounted for about $239 million, or 96%, of the day’s trading volume.

Over the past 30 days, tokenized NVDA shares recorded about $2 billion in DEX trading volume, compared with $808.1 million for tokenized TTWO shares.

The GTA VI launch has also attracted crypto scammers. Blockchain security company Blockaid said in a Wednesday X post that it had flagged more than 8,800 GTA-themed tokens on Solana as malicious, suspicious or spam, alongside hundreds of social media posts promoting fake airdrops and preorders.

Separately, four US senators questioned the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) on Monday about potential gaps in investor protections and anti-money laundering safeguards under its new tokenized securities exemption.

#5103. Hong Kong adds Hyperliquid to list of ‘suspicious’ crypto platforms

Hong Kong’s securities regulator said Hyperliquid has no local license and may have promoted its trading services to investors in the city.

Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission (SFC) added Hyperliquid, a decentralized exchange for perpetual futures, to its Alert List on Wednesday.

The SFC said Hyperliquid lacks a license to operate in the city.

The entry names both Hyperliquid and Hyperliquid Hong Kong, while identifying the exchange’s trading site. The SFC said it suspects Hyperliquid of running a trading platform in Hong Kong, claiming to run one there or actively marketing its services to the Hong Kong public.

The SFC’s list of suspicious virtual asset trading platforms now has 69 entries.

Cointelegraph contacted Hyperliquid for comment but did not receive an immediate response.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore added Hyperliquid to its Investor Alert List on June 26. The list includes firms that investors may mistakenly believe the central bank regulates. Hyperliquid said at the time that it never claimed to hold a Singapore license.

#5100. Morning Minute: Google’s Newest AI Model Tops Benchmarks Ahead of Anthropic IPO

Morning Minute: Google’s Newest AI Model Tops Benchmarks Ahead of Anthropic IPO

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

Google shipped Gemini 4 Neon on Wednesday, its fourth frontier model release in three weeks, and on the eve of the heavily anticipated Anthropic IPO.

This isn’t just a normal model release. For one, it’s incredibly good. Neon posts 61 on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index against 59 for Claude Opus 5.5 and 57 for GPT-6.2 Sol. It takes the Vals Index at 70.9%, AutomationBench at 56.3% against Opus 5.5’s 42.5%, and GraphWalks long-context retrieval at 95.2% against Opus 5.5’s 66.8%.

Beyond just the outperformance, Google has been releasing at a pace nobody else is matching. It dropped Argon in mid-October. Carbon on October 28. Hydrogen last week. Google owns its silicon, its data centers, and its distribution, and it is using all three to iterate faster than labs that rent compute. And it has some speculating that Google has already achieved RSI (recursive self-improvement), the final step ahead of super intelligence.

So Google has caught up at the top of the AI race. Just in time for the ANT IPO.

Anthropic will be priced at $83, 6.4% above the top of its $70 to $78 range, valuing the company around $1.5 trillion fully diluted. On the 825 million shares in the original terms, that’s roughly $68.5 billion raised, and about $94.5 billion counting the separate $26 billion private placement from its compute suppliers. It trades Thursday on Nasdaq as ANT. Notably, ANT has been trading on Hyperliquid in the $90-$100 range for the past week but popped to $105 this morning.

The valuation math is a 55% step-up from the $965 billion May round and about 25% below the $2 trillion Anthropic was reportedly chasing in September. Revenue ran $33.2 billion through nine months against $2.5 billion a year earlier, a 13x increase. Annualize that and $1.5 trillion is roughly 34 times sales.

That’s a steep raise given the macro backdrop and the Fed calling for more hikes for longer. AI stocks have been shaky over the past month and even moreso since Jensen Huang left Nvidia. Not the most confident market headed into the 2nd $1T+ IPO of the year.

So what does it mean for today’s price action? Personally, I expect a pop to the $95-$105 level that Hyperliquid has been pricing out for weeks, and it likely sees a spike higher. But there will be massive volatility, and it’s very likely to trade under that $83 level over the coming days and weeks. Especially given the new (surprise) headwinds from Google with its outperforming model.

We will find out soon enough. Good luck to everyone trading it today.
