#5097. Ethereum’s new DeFi security tool raises questions over censorship

Phylax’s system lets decentralized protocols block potentially harmful transactions, but raises questions about potential censorship and Ethereum’s concentrated builder market.

Phylax’s new security system could help decentralized finance (DeFi) protocols stop malicious transactions before they settle.

That may bolster crypto’s defenses against North Korean hackers and United States-sanctioned actors, but it may also introduce a way to freeze smart contract functions without explicitly giving anyone the power to do so.

And that’s the kind of control that raises concerns the system could end up as a centralized tool for law enforcement rather than decentralized finance.

“The question is not just whether there is risk, but if it’s acceptable risk,” says Stefanie Boss, co-author of research on censorship resistance and compliance behavior in Ethereum’s consensus mechanism.

“In this case, I’d lean toward that it is acceptable, as the goal is to make the network safer and more aligned with the network and application values,” she says.

Announced on Nov. 10, Phylax’s integration with block builder Titan Builder, allows Ethereum applications to define rules that exclude transactions that may produce harmful outcomes during block construction.

Titan, which Phylax says produces one in every two Ethereum blocks, checks transactions against those rules before including them in its blocks.

The idea is to prevent exploits from settling rather than relying on monitoring systems or emergency interventions after an attack has begun. “Our mission: stop users from losing money to hacks and errors in DeFi,” the announcement said.

Related: Fears of AI-driven DeFi hack epidemic overstated for now — but not for long

In a blog Phylax uses the scenario of a vulnerable vault that could mistakenly release $1 million to a user whose shares are only worth $1,000. With the system in operation, a circuit breaker could cap the amount leaving the vault in a single transaction, preventing the funds from being drained.

Similar safeguards could also potentially limit losses in major hacks like the $6.3 billion Binance hack or the $380 million Bitget exploit, where malicious actors drained exchange wallets and were able to move large amounts of crypto through decentralized protocols.

Phylax says hacks should not settle. Source: Phylax Systems.

While preventing malicious transactions would be a win for the crypto space, digital assets lawyer Gabriel Shapiro warned that applications using the system could become effectively freezable if builders stopped providing the required attestations.

He explained that smart contracts can be configured to reject transactions unless they are included in blocks from builders that have performed the required security checks. If those builders stopped providing attestations, protected functions could keep rejecting transactions until they resumed.

Related: DeFi TVL drops 39% in 2026 amid market downturn and record hack activity

Phylax founder and chief executive Odysseas Lamtzidis says protocols can avoid that risk by allowing transactions to go through even when the system’s security checks are unavailable.

“The sane default we provide and suggest to protocols is a fail-open behavior,” he tells Magazine.

But that safeguard is optional, leaving individual protocols to decide how much priority to give security over availability. “Some protocols might choose to prioritize safety over liveness,” Lamtzidis says.

He also says protocols can decide whether their smart contracts are immutable or can be changed through governance.

Shapiro says fail-open protections should be mandatory and changes to the safeguards should be subject to time delays to prevent protocols from removing them immediately.

There is also a broader concern about whether the system could reinforce Ethereum’s already heavily concentrated block-building market.

Fan Zhang, an Ethereum researcher, says Phylax’s technology is only becoming practical now because of that concentration. “We finally have a way to implement this effectively,” he says, “but sadly only because of how centralized Ethereum’s builder market has become.”

Zhang says Phylax’s approach has “appealing security benefits, especially in the age of AI bots being able to find bugs faster than ever.”

But he warns that introducing additional code at such a critical point in transaction processing creates new failure modes, including implementation errors and builder signing-key theft, loss or impersonation.

“I think the community is very concerned about centralized builders and is making efforts to address them. So I’m concerned that these efforts may undermine each other,” he adds.

Related: FBI blames North Korea for record $6.3B Binance hack

Ethereum researcher Sen Yang tells Magazine, “If major protocols rely on a small number of builders for security enforcement, this could give those builders a competitive advantage and further exacerbate market concentration.”

Phylax announces its circuit breaker system. Source: Phylax Systems

He says Ethereum’s proposed enshrined proposer-builder separation (ePBS) upgrade, slated for inclusion in the upcoming Glamsterdam upgrade, may reduce reliance on third-party relays, “but it does not directly address builder market concentration.’’

Yang says transactions excluded by dominant builders could still be included by non-censoring builders, potentially causing delays rather than permanent exclusion.

Phylax argues that its system does not introduce a new authority over Ethereum transactions because applications themselves define the rules. The technology is “explicitly opt-in,” Lamtzidis says.

He compares the checks to conditions already written into smart contracts. “It’s no different than a ‘require()’ statement,” he says. “Censorship implies arbitrary decision-making or coercion.”

Zhang makes a similar distinction between applications voluntarily filtering transactions and external parties imposing censorship. “Applications willingly signing up for filtering is fine, as a user can walk away from such applications,” he says. “It’s externally forced censorship that’s the problem. That’s where I’ll draw the line.”

Boss says rules targeting a transaction’s outcome are less concerning than those targeting who is making it. “In this case, it is also result-driven, which is often considered less risky,” she says.

She considers the security objective legitimate, arguing that “the harm of those hacks is much higher than the harm of this type of exclusion.”

But she warns that rules could also be used to enforce sanctions or politically motivated exclusions.

“It is important to ensure that there are proper safeguards in place to make sure that these rules will remain fair and designed for security,” she says.

Magazine: Banks are banning AI agents over security... and competition concerns

#5096. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley Uses Banded Chin-Ups to Build Upper-Body Strength

The supermodel’s go-to move targets your back and arms.

Rosie Huntington-Whiteley returned to the Victoria's Secret runway this year after a 16-year hiatus, but the supermodel has continued to make fitness a priority throughout her career.

“It’s really about consistency because I’m a gym girl—I’m always training and taking care of myself,” Rosie told Vogue.

The former Burberry Body face has previously cited strength training as her main form of exercise.

“I feel really strong and it's improved my posture, muscle tone, and definition,” she said to Vogue on a separate occasion.

She recently shared a workout clip of herself doing an impressive upper-body exercise—the banded chin-up—just days before the show, writing: “No days off.”

Chin-ups are a compound exercise targeting your back and arm muscles, including the latissimus dorsi (lats), biceps, and trapezius (traps).

A chin-up uses an underhand grip, with your palms facing you, while a pull-up uses an overhand grip, with your palms facing away.

Using a thicker band gives you more assistance when performing reps, while a thinner band gives you less.

Remember: while models' and celebrities' training routines can be inspiring, genetics also play a significant role in body shape, height and how easily someone builds muscle or stays lean. Take inspiration from the healthy habits – not the expectation of looking identical.

While the chin-up is a challenging movement, you can build up to your first bodyweight rep—without a band—using a few progression exercises.

Holding yourself above the bar keeps your muscles under constant tension, which helps build strength.

This exercise requires you to lower yourself as slowly as possible from the top of the movement. People are generally much stronger in the lowering phase of an exercise than in the lifting portion, making it easier than trying to pull yourself up. However, the movement still works your muscles throughout the entire range of motion, helping you build the strength needed to perform a chin-up.

Rosie has highlighted the importance of focusing on skill and feeling confident over aesthetics.

“For me, it’s really about feeling strong and fit, as opposed to thinking about a number on the scales or on a measuring tape. Competence builds confidence,” she said.

Getting stronger brings her a sense of accomplishment.

“There's nothing more satisfactory to me than when I go up in weight,” she added to Vogue. “It makes me happy when I start to see the results of [consistently] lifting.” In fact, regularly working out is “when [she] feels her strongest and fittest.”

She added that motherhood had given her a new perspective on her body.

“I’m about to turn 40 next year; I look at my body and I have so much appreciation for having had two children,” Rosie continued.

Kate puts together fitness content that covers functional and strength training, cardio, workout challenges, interviews and news. She's often doing gym laundry or listening to music.

#5095. Quantum Upgrade Could Be 'Particularly Challenging' for Blockchains: IOSCO

Quantum Upgrade Could Be 'Particularly Challenging' for Blockchains: IOSCO

Financial markets, including crypto, need to start preparing now for quantum computers capable of breaking the cryptography that secures them, according to the world's main forum for securities regulators.

A "sufficiently capable" quantum computer could undermine widely used public-key cryptography that supports critical functions across financial markets, the International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) said in a report published on Thursday. The cyber readiness challenge posed by quantum computing "is immediate and requires action," it said.

"Practical quantum computers capable of compromising widely used cryptography do not yet exist, but the scale and duration of the transition mean the financial sector should start preparing today," IOSCO Board Chair Jean-Paul Servais said in a statement.

The 63-page report cited conservative estimates that such machines could emerge between 2030 and 2035. Several industry participants consulted by IOSCO went further, arguing that firms should prepare for the possibility that they arrive within five years, potentially accelerated by AI.

IOSCO said the migration may be particularly challenging for blockchains, because cryptography is embedded in their core architecture and any change requires coordinated action across decentralized networks. Public blockchains such as Ethereum, Solana and Tron rely heavily on elliptic curve cryptography to verify identities and validate transactions, and a powerful enough quantum computer could derive private keys from exposed public keys, a scenario known as "Q-Day."

Swapping in quantum-resistant signatures comes at a cost. Signatures under ML-DSA-44, a post-quantum standard from the U.S. National Institute of Standards and Technology, are about 2,420 bytes, roughly 35 to 40 times larger than today's 64-byte elliptic curve signatures, the report said. It cited evidence from a quantum-resistant Solana testnet that saw throughput fall by about 90% when post-quantum signatures replaced existing ones.

Because the cryptography sits in transaction formats and consensus rules, replacing it requires hard forks, and decentralized networks have no way to force quick adoption, making migration "slow and fragmented," IOSCO said. Accounts and smart contracts still controlled by vulnerable keys could stay exposed after a network adds quantum-resistant options, and the path to quantum resistance for Bitcoin wallets remains uphill.

Wallet makers face their own trade-offs. SHRINCS, the leading candidate for a post-quantum Bitcoin signature scheme, offers "stateful" signatures that are about 10 times smaller but can expose funds to theft if keys are reused beyond a set signing budget, Trezor CTO Tomáš Sušánka has said. Trezor is considering making the larger, safer stateless option the default, and hardware wallets will also need post-quantum cryptography for firmware and device authentication, he added.

The report distinguished "on-spend" attacks, in which an attacker races to exploit a public key revealed by a pending transaction, from "at-rest" attacks on keys left exposed for longer. Dormant or abandoned coins pose a particular problem where their holders can't move them. Crypto firms have formed a quantum alliance as some 6 million BTC sit in exposed addresses, and Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has proposed "quantum landmines" to make attacks on such coins costly.

Layer-2 networks are not insulated either. Optimistic rollups could face forged signatures during their dispute windows, typically seven days on Ethereum, and many zero-knowledge rollups use SNARK proofs that rely on elliptic curves. Hash-based STARK proofs are more resistant, but a rollup is only as safe as the layer-1 contracts, bridges and governance keys it depends on, the report said.

Developers are already working on fixes. Researchers have proposed several ways to make Bitcoin quantum-safe, StarkWare has shown a quantum-safe Bitcoin transaction, and Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has said the network is betting its future on quantum resistance.

IOSCO said firms should migrate to post-quantum cryptography and build "crypto-agility," the ability to update cryptographic systems without major redesign. Quantum key distribution could complement those measures on selected high-security channels, it added.

Most preparations across financial markets are still at an early stage, focused on awareness, governance, training and identifying vulnerable systems, according to the report, which drew on a March 2026 survey of members of IOSCO's Fintech Task Force and discussions with experts. Some respondents described "quantum-procrastination," in which firms put off action while waiting for clearer regulatory signals or wider adoption.

No surveyed jurisdiction reported operational deployment of quantum computing in regulated financial services. The watchdog also urged regulators to act against "quantum washing," in which firms overstate the role, maturity or performance of quantum technology. IOSCO said it will treat quantum as a priority in 2027, focusing on raising awareness across the financial ecosystem.

Quantum hardware is advancing quickly. IBM has claimed a trusted quantum advantage, and Google has said it has cracked some of the field's biggest bottlenecks.

#5093. I Analyzed 30 AI ‘Doctors’ on Social Media and Couldn’t Believe What I Found

The accounts are real. The medical advice is concerning.

I HAVE YEARS’ worth of poop in my intestines.

At least, that’s what a doctor on social media told me. He was wearing a white coat. His diplomas hung on the wall behind him. Ominous music played in the background while he performed a graphic demonstration about how one product can clean my gut of all that fecal matter, leaving me feeling healthier, happier, and less “toxic.”

There’s just one problem: The doctor doesn’t exist. He was created by generative artificial intelligence to sell a product.

You’re probably thinking that you’d never fall for such an obvious scam, but AI-generated content is growing increasingly sophisticated. And unless a social post comes labelled as “A.I. content,” you might never know these virtual characters dispensing health advice aren’t human.

To get a sense of how widespread this issue is, I searched TikTok and Instagram for AI-generated health content featuring fake doctors and other medical professionals. I wanted to see what sorts of information they were giving, if they were selling products, and if there was a transparent disclosure of the use of AI to create that content.

I quickly identified 30 accounts that featured AI-generated doctors and health influencers. Some of these accounts were not marked as AI content. Others had the blue Meta verification check that’s supposed to mark an account as authentic and credible. But how can someone be authentic and credible if they don’t exist?

What I found was shocking—but there are still a few critical tells you can use to sniff out a scam.

TAKE, FOR EXAMPLE, the curious case of James Anderson.

In September 2026, an Australian fact-checking company discovered hundreds of Instagram ads featuring an AI-generated surgeon by that name. Anderson was supposedly from New Zealand, Australia, and even Toronto, depending on the ad. In Canada, the content identified him as Dr. James Anderson, MD, FRCSC (Orth). FRCSC is Fellow of the Royal College of Surgeons (Canada), a regulated credential that was clearly being hijacked.

His pitch was simple and hopeful: Instead of surgery for your sore shoulder, you should consider a device called Mendable Shoulder. The device, allegedly developed by Anderson and a “team of biomedical engineers,” could heal your pain in just 12 minutes a day, according to the pitch. It cost $119, but was cheaper if you bought three. Some of these ads claim that Anderson works at North Toronto Shoulder Clinic, which, as someone who actually lives in North Toronto, I’ve never heard of—because it doesn’t exist.

Whoever created Anderson dreamed up an entire persona for him, complete with a convincing appearance, fabricated credentials, made-up experience, and fake testimonials.

Even real doctors aren’t safe from having their names and likeness scraped by these companies to sell products. A recent investigation by CBC News found hundreds of online videos using deepfakes of real medical professionals whose faces and voices had been stolen and manipulated to make them appear to endorse products and services that they’d never recommended.

NOT ALL THE accounts I studied relied on fake credentials or white coats and fabricated diplomas. Some featured elderly, AI-generated characters who were allegedly sharing “ancient remedies” and old “secrets” from other cultures.

A large proportion of these supposed “remedies” were absurd or even sexist, calling breasts “hooters” or showing a model of a vagina with “worms” coming out of it. All of them told viewers that modern medicine had forgotten about—or hadn’t yet discovered—this special cure. One simple remedy can solve all of your health problems, was a common refrain. Some of these accounts linked to Amazon pages selling supplements or books. Others were clearly going for clicks and views. Which brings up the next red flag...

THE MOST SURPRISING detail I noticed was how popular these accounts appeared to be. Several had follower counts in the millions, which only serves to increase their perceived value and credibility.

Although some of the engagement on the posts probably came from bots (or at least, I want to believe that they are), there had to be some real people in there, too. And real people are certainly encountering this content, and potentially using it to help make decisions about their health. But don’t mistake popularity for authority.

MY INFORMAL SEARCH wasn’t a scientific study. But larger investigations suggest that AI-generated experts are an increasingly sophisticated vector for health misinformation.

In 2026, Spanish fact-checking organization Maldita found 250 AI-generated health accounts reached more than 53 million followers across seven languages.

They also identified 25 Instagram accounts impersonating medical professionals. Collectively, these accounts had more than 3.6 million followers and had accumulated more than 397 million video views.

So remain skeptical, especially when a so-called “expert” or “professional” talks about “ancient remedies,” supplements, or treatments that involve basic foods that are found in your kitchen.

And if they offer a link to buy supplements from their Amazon store, back away. If it sounds too good to be true, or even just seems “off,” ask your doctor or another IRL health professional.

Abby Langer, RD is the owner of Abby Langer Nutrition, a Toronto-based nutrition consulting and communications company

#5091. The 6 Best Pillows of 2026, Tested by Editors

The $190 Purple Harmony Pillow might be divisive, but it earned our best overall badge.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As someone with frequent neck pain, I would give up my cozy sheets, white noise machine, and sunrise alarm clock if it meant I wouldn’t wake up with a stiff neck. (And that’s coming from someone who invests a lot in their sleep.)

"I think that a pillow can make a big difference in people that have a history of neck issues," says Leia Rispoli, MD, an interventional pain management specialist and physiatrist at DISC Sport & Spine Center. This is the very reason why I tend to lug my favorite pillow around on weekend trips.

There isn’t one pillow that works for everyone. Your best option depends on your sleep position, preferred firmness, body type, and whether you tend to overheat at night. To find the best pillows, our editors and testers slept on popular models from Purple, Casper, Coop, Saatva, and other leading sleep brands. We evaluated each one for comfort, support, temperature regulation, adjustability, ease of care, and value.

I love the Purple Harmony Pillow so much that I try to convert nearly everyone who mentions sleep within earshot of me. I even pack it for overnight trips. But it does feel unusual at first. Inside, a springy, ventilated latex core is wrapped in Purple’s GelFlex Grid, a flexible, honeycomb-shaped layer that bends under the weight of your head and springs back when you move. Together, these materials make the pillow feel buoyant rather than sink-in like memory foam.

I tested the medium, 6.5-inch loft, meaning the pillow’s height before it is compressed. It provided enough height to support my head and neck while I slept on my side without feeling rigid. The materials give slightly beneath heavier areas, which helped reduce uncomfortable pressure around my head and neck. As someone who frequently experiences neck and back pain, I consistently felt comfortable and supported while sleeping on it.

Purple recommends the low, 5.5-inch version for most stomach and back sleepers. The medium and tall versions are generally better suited to side and combination sleepers, who often need more height to fill the space between the head and mattress. Body size and personal preference will also affect which height feels most comfortable.

The Harmony was also one of the coolest pillows I tested. Air circulates through the ventilated latex and open honeycomb layer instead of becoming trapped inside a solid block of foam. Its stretchy outer cover is also designed to draw sweat away from the skin so it can evaporate. Even on warmer nights, I rarely felt the need to flip the pillow over in search of a cooler surface.

After nearly three years of regular use, my pillow has not developed noticeable lumps or gone flat. Its durable build helps justify the high price, but there are compromises. The pillow is unusually heavy, and some sleepers may notice the grid’s texture through the cover. Purple’s 30-night trial is also shorter than the trial periods offered by many competing brands.

Still, no other pillow I tested delivered the same combination of responsive support, cooling, and long-term shape retention. I would purchase it again without hesitation.

Women’s Health art director Christine Giordano typically sleeps with two or three pillows, but the Casper Original gave her enough support to use fewer. “This pillow gave me the support I needed while still feeling light and airy,” she says. “It was breathable and didn’t clump, keeping me cool and comfortable at night.”

The Casper uses a pillow-within-a-pillow design: A firmer inner cushion provides structure, while a softer outer layer adds cushioning. Both layers are filled with polyester microfiber, a synthetic material made to feel light and fluffy like down. The construction prevented Giordano’s head from sinking too deeply while still giving the pillow a plush feel.

Giordano tries to sleep on her back but usually shifts onto her left side during the night. The pillow remained comfortable and supportive in both positions, making it a strong choice for combination sleepers who alternate between their backs and sides. However, stomach sleepers or people who prefer a flatter pillow may find its approximately nine-inch uncompressed height too lofty.

The breathable cotton cover and airy synthetic fill also prevented the pillow from feeling stuffy during testing. It’s not as breathable as a dedicated cooling pillow, but Giordano did not notice heat buildup or need to flip it over for a cooler surface.

The entire pillow is machine-washable, which makes it easier to clean than models with foam or latex cores. Its soft filling may need occasional fluffing to maintain its shape, but Giordano did not notice clumping during testing.

At under $100, it is one of the more affordable pillows we tested. Giordano liked it enough that she replaced some of the other pillows in her home and purchased additional Casper ones for her family.

Women’s Health executive health and fitness director Jacqueline Andriakos, CPT, calls the Coop Original Adjustable the most comfortable pillow she has tried. “I’ve never been a pillow snob or had major issues with basic pillows from Amazon, but I noticed that this was far more comfortable than other, cheaper pillows I’ve used,” she says.

The pillow is filled with a blend of shredded memory foam and microfiber. Memory foam molds around the head and neck, while the soft synthetic fibers keep the pillow from feeling like a dense, solid block of foam. Unlike a pillow with a fixed height, the Coop can be unzipped so you can remove filling for a flatter feel or add more for extra support. The process can get messy, but that adjustability makes it easier to tailor the pillow to your body and sleep position.

Coop offers the pillow in classic, crescent, and cut-out shapes. Andriakos tested the classic and cut-out versions while switching between side and stomach sleeping. She preferred the cut-out, which has a curved indentation that creates room for the shoulder and allows the pillow to sit closer to the neck.

“I liked that it could wrap around my neck and shoulders, and I could almost cuddle it like a body pillow with an arm threaded underneath,” she says. Although the shape can look slightly awkward inside a standard pillowcase, it was super comfortable when she slept on her side.

The pillow felt plush, but Andriakos’ head did not sink so deeply that her neck felt unsupported. She also found the outer cover breathable, although sleepers who regularly overheat may prefer a pillow specifically designed for cooling.

Because its height can be adjusted, the Coop can work for side, back, stomach, and combination sleepers. Side sleepers will generally need more filling to keep the head level with the spine, while stomach sleepers may prefer to remove enough filling to create a flatter surface. Finding the right amount may require a few nights of experimentation, but Coop’s 100-night trial gives you time to make those adjustments.

It would take an exceptional pillow for me to consider replacing my Purple Harmony, but the Saatva Latex Pillow gave it serious competition. Although both pillows contain latex, they feel noticeably different. Purple uses one solid, springy latex core, while Saatva uses shredded pieces of latex surrounded by a soft layer of fine synthetic fibers. This gives the Saatva a plusher, more traditional feel, but it has enough structure to support the head and neck.

The pillow feels dense when you first pick it up, but my head settled gently into the soft outer layer without sinking too deeply. The shredded latex inside quickly returned to its original shape when I moved, so the pillow stayed supportive as I shifted positions. As a side sleeper who frequently experiences neck pain, I did not wake up with additional stiffness.

Saatva offers two heights. The standard version measures approximately four to five inches and is designed for back and stomach sleepers who generally need a lower pillow. The high-loft version measures approximately six to seven inches and is better suited to side and combination sleepers who need more height between their heads and mattresses.

I also appreciated that the pillow remained breathable throughout the night. The small spaces between the pieces of shredded latex allow air to circulate, while the cotton cover feels cool. It does not create the immediately cold sensation of a cooling pillow, but I did not notice uncomfortable heat buildup.

The outer layer is filled with microdenier fibers, which are very fine synthetic strands designed to imitate the light, fluffy feel of down. You can machine-wash this outer layer and its cotton cover, but the inner latex core must be removed and kept out of the washing machine.

At $165 for a standard/queen size, this is one of the more expensive pillows we tested. Saatva also provides only 45 days for returns, which is shorter than the trial periods offered by several competitors. Still, its combination of plush cushioning, responsive support, and breathable materials makes it an excellent choice for sleepers who want a luxury-hotel feel that’s still supportive.

For hot sleepers, a breathable pillow can be the difference between a good night’s sleep and a night spent tossing and turning. The Slumber Cloud UltraCool stood out because its cooling effect was immediately noticeable during testing.

Women’s Health managing editor Laura McLaughlin described the sensation as “a little sci-fi” because the surface felt immediately cool when she rested her head on it. The cover contains Outlast, a material designed to absorb excess body heat when you become warm and release it as your skin cools. This helps prevent heat from collecting around your head and face, although the effect may be less noticeable beneath a thick pillowcase.

McLaughlin also found the pillow easy to combine with others. “It’s great for layering with other pillows to get just the right height,” she says. “It’s neither too dense nor too high to use in combination with others already in place.” That makes it especially useful for sleepers who want additional height without placing a rigid pillow beneath their heads.

The interior is filled with polyester down alternative, a synthetic material designed to imitate the airy feel of down. It is available in soft/medium and medium/firm versions, allowing sleepers to choose how plush or structured they want the pillow to feel. The softer option will generally suit stomach sleepers or anyone who prefers more sink while the firmer version may provide better support for back and side sleepers.

The entire pillow can be machine-washed, a convenient advantage over pillows with foam or latex cores. However, synthetic filling can begin to clump after repeated use and washing, so it may require regular fluffing to redistribute the material.

Although the UltraCool does not provide the customized support of an adjustable pillow, it delivered the most noticeable cooling sensation of any model we tested.

The Brooklinen Down Alternative Pillow is built for sleepers who want the fluffy feel of down without actual feathers. It’s filled with recycled poly microgel, a cluster of fine synthetic fibers that creates a light cushioning similar to down.

Unlike many pillows that come in only one firmness, this one is available in soft, medium, and firm versions. Brooklinen recommends the soft pillow for stomach sleepers who generally need less height beneath the head; the medium for back sleepers; and the firm for side sleepers who typically need more support to fill the space between the head and mattress.

Its cotton sateen shell feels smooth and has a subtle sheen. The pillow is also certified by OEKO-TEX Standard 100, which means its materials have been tested for a list of regulated substances. That certification does not evaluate the pillow’s comfort, durability, or environmental impact.

Brooklinen allows most products to be returned within 365 days, although it deducts a $9.95 return fee from the refund. That’s one of the most generous return windows we’ve seen.

The pillow can be machine-washed in cold water and tumble-dried on low with wool dryer balls until completely dry. As with most pillows filled with loose synthetic fibers, it may require regular fluffing to redistribute the fill and preserve its shape.

A pillow’s height, or loft, should keep your head and neck aligned with your spine. Side sleepers generally need a taller pillow, back sleepers a medium-height pillow, and stomach sleepers a low pillow. Your mattress matters, too: The more deeply your shoulders sink into it, the less pillow height you may need. Most of the pillows we tested fell in the five- to nine-inch range, offering different levels of support depending on their material and sleeping position.

Side sleepers often benefit from a medium-firm or firm pillow that maintains its height. Back sleepers generally need medium support while stomach sleepers may prefer something softer that compresses easily. Because firmness labels vary by brand, consider the pillow’s material and height as well.

“There isn't one brand that's the magical brand for everyone,” says Rispoli. Look for a pillow that supports your head without tilting your neck sharply upward or downward. Adjustable pillows can help you fine-tune the height. Consult a health care professional if your pain is persistent, severe, or accompanied by numbness or weakness.

Memory foam molds around the head but may retain heat. Latex feels springy, breathable, and durable. Down is soft and airy but may lack structure while synthetic down alternative is usually easier to wash but may flatten over time. Hybrid pillows combine materials to balance cushioning and support.

Look for ventilated latex, shredded filling, or another construction that allows air to circulate. Some covers also absorb excess body heat or feel cool, although a thick pillowcase may reduce the effect. People who sleep cold may prefer denser foam or plush down filling that holds slightly more warmth.

As a sleep writer, I’ve tested all kinds of sleep accessories, from mattresses to eye masks, but pillows are probably my most-tested product category. (Unfortunately, I once tested so many pillows at the same time that I injured my neck, but I’ve learned my lesson.) To find the best pillows available online, I selected popular picks from leading sleep brands, like Purple, Casper, Saatva, Pluto, and more, for testing. Some helpful testers and I have slept on these pillows for dozens of nights to determine whether they’re worth the money, and our top pick has been my favorite pillow to sleep on for nearly three years. From flat pillow lovers to hot sleepers, our diverse crew of sleepers tried out eight pillows of various styles, materials, and price points.

#5090. Anthropic shares open at $111.50, 34.3% above $83 IPO price

The Claude chatbot maker began trading under the ticker ANT, with the company’s valuation at around $2 trillion at the opening price.

Anthropic shares opened trading under the ticker ANT on the Nasdaq at $111.50 Thursday, about 34.3% above their initial public offering (IPO) price.

Anthropic shares opened trading under the ticker ANT on the Nasdaq at $111.50 Thursday, about 34.3% above their initial public offering (IPO) price.

The Wall Street Journal had reported an expected opening of $110 minutes before trading began as fresh headlines poured in by the minute with ever-rising opening price predictions. The opening price values Anthropic at around $2 trillion on a fully diluted basis.

Anthropic had priced 980 million shares at $83 on Wednesday, above its initial $70-$78 range. The $81.3 billion IPO includes stock from existing shareholders. A separate $26 billion private placement brings the combined deal to $107.3 billion.

Anthropic’s IPO has blasted past SpaceX’s record $75 billion IPO in June, showing that the scale of investor demand for AI companies appears undeterred. SpaceX shares opened at $150, above their $135 offer price.

Crypto exchange OKX said in a note shared with Cointelegraph that its Anthropic pre-IPO perpetual contract traded at about 103 USDC (USDC) earlier Thursday, roughly 24% above the offer price. The contract provides derivative exposure without ownership of Anthropic shares. Binance Wallet and Kraken also offered eligible users tokenized IPO exposure through xStocks. However, both warned that actual allocations could be partial or even zero, with unused funds returned.

Crypto platforms also offered exposure ahead of SpaceX’s listing, although Binance, Bybit, Bitget Wallet and MEXC later canceled their tokenized IPO campaigns after failing to secure share allocations.

Anthropic faces stiff competition from OpenAI, DeepSeek and Google while committing billions to computing infrastructure, including a $28 billion, 15-year data center lease with Bitcoin miner Riot Platforms. The company has committed around $901 billion in total to purchases and leases, mostly for computing power and infrastructure, according to its IPO filing, with spending stretching out as far as 2045.

#5087. Gen Z Embraces Crypto While Most Americans Call It a 'Side Quest'

Gen Z Embraces Crypto While Most Americans Call It a 'Side Quest'

For many young Americans, crypto is coming before the 401(k).

Some 21% of Gen Z adults own cryptocurrency, compared with 16% who hold an employer-sponsored retirement plan like a 401(k), according to a survey released Thursday by SoFi Technologies.

That’s seven times the rate of baby boomers, just 3% of whom own crypto. Crypto was Gen Z’s most widely held investment in the survey, ahead of stocks, bonds ,and real estate.

The online survey of 2,064 U.S. adults was conducted by SoFi and YouGov from Oct. 15 to 28. SoFi, a nationally chartered bank that sells crypto alongside checking and investment accounts, developed the report with Culture Co-op.

Gen Z respondents described crypto less as a gamble than a strategy. They were nearly three times as likely as the general population to call it one of the best ways to build future wealth, 21% vs. 8%, and 61% said they see it as a financial advantage unique to their generation.

Only 20% of Gen Z crypto owners said they bought in because it “sounded interesting,” compared with 34% of crypto investors overall. Diversification (32%) and easier international payments (29%) ranked higher.

Part of the gap reflects age. Many respondents in their late teens and 20s don’t yet have access to an employer retirement plan.

Across the broader population, the picture is far cooler. Only 13% of Americans own crypto, and 62% are firm skeptics. Three in four say crypto feels intimidating to get into, and 77% agreed with the statement, “Cryptocurrency feels like a financial side quest that I don’t have the time or energy for.”

Even owners feel out of their depth: 82% of current crypto investors said they should know more about it than they do, and 11% admitted knowing little to nothing.

“Nobody feels the gaps in their financial education more sharply than someone who has just bought Bitcoin,” said Natalie Brunell, host of the Coin Stories podcast and a contributor to SoFi’s crypto newsletter.

The findings echo a recent survey conducted by Ipsos for the DeFi Education Fund, which found that 22% of Americans have ever owned or used crypto but only 13% used it in the past year.

SoFi argues that putting crypto in the same app as banking is the key to wider adoption. Half of respondents open to crypto said having their primary bank offer it would be a tipping point, and 63% of current investors said they’d trade more if they could do it at their bank.

SoFi has been building out that model, launching a tokenized deposit that pays 4.4% and moves like a stablecoin, as well as a bank-issued stablecoin running on Polygon’s payments infrastructure.

The survey also challenges the crypto “tech bro” stereotype. Black and Hispanic respondents own crypto at more than twice the rate of white respondents, 22% to 9%. Miami led the 10 cities surveyed at 20% ownership, while San Francisco matched the national average at 13%.

#5085. Bitcoin price ignores Anthropic debut as ANT share price dips below $100

Bitcoin dropped toward a third retest of $62,000 as Anthropic debuted on the Nasdaq, hitting highs of $115 per share before dropping below $100.

Bitcoin shrugged off US tech-stock strength at Thursday’s Wall Street open as markets reacted to Anthropic’s $111.50 debut.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD falling to $62,250 as US stock markets headed higher.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#5083. Anthropic Tops SpaceX's Initial IPO With $81 Billion Deal: Where the Money Went

Anthropic Tops SpaceX's Initial IPO With $81 Billion Deal: Where the Money Went

Anthropic has priced the largest initial IPO share sale in history at $81.34 billion, topping the $75 billion SpaceX first sold in June. But SpaceX still has the larger completed IPO after an additional share sale.

The Claude maker sold 980 million shares at $83 on Wednesday and is set to begin trading Thursday on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT.

The price beat the original $70-to-$78 range, while the company increased the offering from 825 million shares. Demand was about six times the number available before pricing.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perps on Decrypt.

At $83, Anthropic is valued at roughly $1.5 trillion including stock options and other potential shares, though its value based on outstanding shares is closer to $1.1 trillion.

The headline $81.34 billion isn't all money for Anthropic. About 956.35 million new shares bring $79.38 billion into the company before fees. Another 23.65 million shares, worth $1.96 billion, are being sold by its seven co-founders—roughly $280 million each. Anthropic receives none of that founders' cash, according to its final prospectus.

On top of the public sale, seven technology companies are buying $26 billion of nonvoting stock in a separate private placement. Google and Nvidia are putting in $10 billion each, AMD $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft $1 billion each, and TSMC $500 million.

The public offering and private placement total $107.34 billion in gross share sales, including the $1.96 billion going to founders rather than Anthropic.

The $104.8 billion net figure is for both transactions, not for the IPO alone. Anthropic gets $79.38 billion from its newly issued public shares, plus $26 billion from the private placement: $105.38 billion before costs. Subtract roughly $580 million in company-paid underwriting fees and estimated offering expenses, and the prospectus estimates $104.8 billion net.

That said, it could rise to about $117 billion if underwriters buy all the extra shares allowed. Anthropic plans to spend the money on operations, hiring and, above all, computing infrastructure.

Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley led a 22-bank underwriting group, with roughly $550 million in underwriting commissions. The company also offered access through brokers including Fidelity, Schwab, Robinhood, and SoFi, but retail investors received less than 20% of the offering.

Amazon remains the largest disclosed shareholder after the transaction with about 14.6% of the company, followed by Google at roughly 13.4%.

Early financial backers also hold enormous paper fortunes. Bloomberg estimates put Lightspeed's stake near $28 billion, Iconiq's above $26 billion and Menlo Ventures' around $22 billion. Ashton Kutcher's Sound Ventures reportedly holds shares worth almost $11 billion. Those figures are estimates of stock value, not cash they received at the IPO.

Investors, however, aren't buying much control. A special share class gives the founders' holding company 50.1% of shareholder voting power, while the Long-Term Benefit Trust can appoint a majority of the board.

So, Anthropic can raise historic sums while keeping key decisions largely away from ordinary shareholders.

Anthropic generated $33.24 billion in revenue during the first nine months of 2026, including $16.96 billion in the third quarter alone—the latest reported quarter. Its $81 billion annualized revenue run rate at the end of October projects recent customer spending over a year; it isn't money already earned.

Roughly 250 organizations now spend at least $10 million annually with Anthropic, and more than 1,900 spend at least $1 million, using trailing 12-month figures. Its net revenue retention reached about 900%.

In plain English, the same eligible customer group spent nine times as much in the latest three-month period as it did a year earlier. That measure excludes fixed-price Claude subscriptions.

But the business still depends on a few major accounts and distribution partners. Its five biggest customers generated about 25% of revenue over the first nine months of 2026, while cloud marketplaces accounted for 44%. In other words, companies like Amazon, Google and Microsoft can invest in Anthropic, provide the machines it runs on and help sell access to Claude.

Even the product changes at extraordinary speed: Claude models released during the six months through September made up more than 80% of third-quarter revenue. At the end of September 2026, Anthropic had about 5,300 employees and was generating more than $6.6 million in trailing annual revenue per employee.

Rapid growth doesn't mean easy profits. Anthropic posted a $567 million operating loss in the third quarter of 2026, after a $974 million operating profit in the second quarter. Over the first nine months, its operating loss totaled $1.26 billion. Those numbers tell a very different story from its much bigger headline net loss.

In the first nine months of 2026, Anthropic posted a $104.04 billion net loss, including $94.3 billion in noncash revaluations of convertible financing and related instruments. Yet it generated $6.57 billion in cash from operating activities during the same period. That is not free cash flow, which also accounts for capital spending, but the distinction matters: a $104 billion accounting loss does not mean $104 billion of cash burned.

The latest balance sheet, dated September 30, 2026, shows $95.6 billion in cash and equivalents plus $36.3 billion in short-term investments—$131.85 billion combined. Convertible notes payable stood at $130.50 billion at their accounting value, with some converting into shares around the IPO.

The final prospectus estimates $232.35 billion in cash and short-term investments on a pro forma, post-transaction basis. That last figure is a modeled balance after the offering and related adjustments, not cash the company held on September 30.

The more consequential number may be what it has promised to spend next. Anthropic disclosed about $900.9 billion in commitments, overwhelmingly for computing and infrastructure, stretching as far as 2045.

It separately estimates another $150 billion to $175 billion in payments under arrangements without fixed dollar amounts. Together, that points to potentially more than $1 trillion—but it is not a bill due today, and some contracts are conditional or can be terminated.

Some $70.7 billion of the stated commitments fall due in the 12 months after September 2026. That explains why even an IPO this large is not necessarily excessive capital for a frontier AI company. Every breakthrough attracts customers, but serving them requires more chips, power, and data centers.

There is a technical catch to the record. SpaceX's June IPO initially sold $75 billion in shares, but the final deal reached $86.25 billion gross after underwriters bought more shares—about $85.675 billion after fees, according to SpaceX's SEC filing.

Anthropic's $81.34 billion beats SpaceX's initial deal, not its final gross total. The extra $26 billion from Anthropic's private placement cannot be counted as public IPO proceeds. Its own option covers another 147 million shares worth about $12.2 billion; if exercised in full, its public IPO would reach $93.54 billion, comfortably above SpaceX's final figure.

Now attention turns to OpenAI. The ChatGPT maker recently raised $11 billion at a reported $1.25 trillion valuation and has indicated it will not list before 2027. Anthropic's debut offers a public-market test of what investors are willing to pay for AI revenue, while exposing the cost of generating it.

The interesting part isn't just that a five-year-old AI lab topped SpaceX's initial IPO. It's that it assembled more than $100 billion in combined public and private financing, started generating cash from operations—and still faces a potential trillion-dollar computing bill.

#5080. Why higher rates could slow Bitcoin buying at treasury firms

A treasury company can have years of dividends covered and still struggle to sell new shares on terms that make another Bitcoin purchase worthwhile.

Rising US interest rates could slow buying by Bitcoin treasury companies long before they have trouble paying their bills.

Strategy raised $244.7 million by selling common stock in the week ended Nov. 15. It spent $206.3 million of the proceeds buying back preferred shares and $33.4 million on 485 BTC.

At the time, its US dollar assets covered more than three years of preferred dividends and debt interest at current payouts. When Treasury bills pay more, investors can demand a higher yield to buy newly issued preferred shares.

For common stock, StoneX analyst Mark Palmer watches how much investors will pay above the value of the Bitcoin each share represents. At a premium, a company can issue stock and buy enough Bitcoin (BTC) to leave shareholders with more BTC per share. However, as that premium narrows, so does the gain.

#5076. Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade could slip to May 2027 over compatibility concerns

Ethereum developers are weighing changes to gas pricing proposals that could disrupt existing smart contracts, after contacting at least 50 affected companies.

Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade could be pushed back as far as May 2027 if developers decide to modify its specifications to address smart contract compatibility issues, according to discussions at Thursday’s All Core Developers meeting.

Glamsterdam is a planned Ethereum hard fork aimed at increasing the network’s capacity and efficiency. However, blockchain security firm ChainSecurity warned in October that proposed changes to Ethereum’s gas pricing could disrupt existing smart contracts and potentially expose them to exploitation, prompting developers to postpone the Hoodi testnet fork, originally scheduled for Oct. 26.

The concerns center on gas repricing proposals EIP-8037 and EIP-8038. Researchers identified 13 real-world examples of potentially affected contracts in October, while a November investigation examined eight smart contract invariants that could be disrupted by the upgrade.

During Thursday’s meeting, developers said the Ethereum Foundation, ChainSecurity and security firm Dedaub had collectively contacted at least 50 affected companies. Three projects were classified as high impact, with fixes completed or expected by mid-December, while medium-impact projects reported timelines extending to Jan. 10.

Developers debated whether to retain Glamsterdam’s existing specifications or modify them to reduce compatibility risks, as changes intended to protect some contracts could disrupt others.

“Every spec change could, again, break new invariants that we are not aware of breaking yet,” one developer warned during the meeting.

Under the current specifications, mainnet activation could come as early as mid-January 2027. However, waiting until January to decide could push activation to late February or early March, or April or May if modifications are adopted, and no decision has been reached yet.

Related: Vitalik ‘pivots’ ETH to store of value, crypto suffers after US midterms: Hodler’s Digest

Separately, Ethereum’s next planned hard fork, Hegotá, could see its scope reduced as the network seeks to accelerate its transition to post-quantum cryptography.

During the meeting, developers considered postponing some Ethereum Improvement Proposals to prioritize post-quantum features, although several argued that reducing the upgrade’s scope would offer little benefit unless it also shortened the rollout timeline.

Developers also raised compatibility concerns over EIP-8151, a proposal designed to protect accounts migrating to post-quantum security. A preliminary review identified a potentially large number of existing smart contracts that could be affected by the changes, complicating the proposal’s inclusion in Hegotá.

No decision was reached on Hegotá’s scope, with client teams expected to submit their positions ahead of further discussions in two weeks.

#5074. What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

Perpetual futures are the engine room of crypto trading. Centralized exchanges processed $85.3 trillion in perps trading volume in 2025 alone, according to CoinGecko's State of Crypto Perpetuals Report 2026, and the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) describes perps as the dominant form of crypto derivatives trading.

They aren't only for crypto, either. Exchanges now offer perps on stocks, commodities and currencies, and Decrypt has become the first news publisher to offer perps trading, powered by Solana exchange Phoenix.

This guide explains what futures are, what makes perpetual futures different, how funding rates and liquidations work, and what to know before trading them.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

To understand perpetual futures, it helps to start with ordinary futures.

A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell an asset at a set price on a set date in the future. Futures have been used for centuries: farmers used them to lock in prices for crops before harvest, and today they are traded on everything from oil and wheat to stock indexes and Bitcoin, on regulated exchanges such as CME.

Traders use futures for two main reasons. Hedgers use them to protect against price swings; an airline might buy oil futures to lock in fuel costs, for example. Speculators use them to bet on price movements without owning the underlying asset.

Traditional futures have an expiry date. When a contract expires, it settles, either by delivering the asset or, more often, by paying out the difference in cash. Traders who want to keep their position open must "roll" it into a new contract, which can add costs and complexity.

Perpetual futures, often shortened to "perps" or "perpetual swaps," work like futures, but with one key difference: they never expire. Traders can hold a position for as long as they like, provided they have enough collateral to keep it open.

The idea was proposed by Yale economist Robert Shiller in a 1993 paper as a way to create markets for assets that are hard to trade directly. Perps took off in crypto. Crypto exchange BitMEX, widely credited with bringing perps to crypto, launched a perpetual contract on Bitcoin in 2016. BitMEX itself closed in September 2026, but the product it pioneered has become the most traded instrument in crypto.

A perpetual contract never actually involves owning the asset. Traders deposit collateral, or "margin," and their profit or loss depends entirely on how the price moves.

When opening a perp position, a trader chooses a direction. Going long means betting the price will rise; going short means betting it will fall. Being able to short as easily as going long is one reason perps are popular: it lets traders profit from falling prices, or hedge assets they already own.

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets traders control a larger position than their collateral alone would allow. With 10x leverage, for example, $100 of margin controls a $1,000 position. If the asset's price rises 5%, the trader makes $50, a 50% return on their margin. But if it falls 5%, they lose $50, half of their margin.

Exchanges track two prices to manage this. The index price is the asset's price on spot markets, usually averaged across several exchanges. The mark price, based on the index and the perp market itself, is used to calculate profits and losses and to decide when positions must be closed. Using an averaged price makes it harder for a sudden spike on one exchange to trigger liquidations.

Because perps never expire, nothing forces their price to converge with the underlying asset, as traditional futures do at expiry. Instead, perps use a funding rate.

At regular intervals—most commonly every eight hours, though some platforms use shorter or longer schedules—traders on one side of the market pay traders on the other:

Decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, for example, pays funding every hour, at one-eighth of the rate calculated for an eight-hour period, to stay consistent with centralized exchanges.

Funding payments go directly between traders, not to the exchange. For traders holding positions over long periods, these payments can add up, and they can either boost or erode returns.

Funding rates also offer a window into market sentiment. Strongly positive funding suggests traders are crowded into bullish bets, while negative funding shows that bears are paying to stay short.

Rates for the same asset can also differ between exchanges. On November 19, 2026, Bitcoin's funding rate was 0.01% per eight hours on Hyperliquid, compared with 0.0037% on Binance, according to Hyperliquid's funding comparison tool.

Some traders aim to earn funding payments rather than bet on prices. In a "cash and carry" or basis trade, they buy an asset on the spot market and short the same amount through perps, collecting funding while their positions offset each other's price moves.

Leverage is a double-edged sword. Every perp position has a maintenance margin, the minimum collateral needed to keep it open. If losses push a trader's margin below that level, the exchange automatically closes the position, a process called liquidation. The trader typically loses most or all of the margin they put up.

The higher the leverage, the smaller the price move needed to trigger a liquidation. At 50x leverage, a 2% move against a position can wipe it out.

Liquidations can also feed on each other. When prices fall sharply, liquidated longs are forced to sell, pushing prices lower and triggering further liquidations, a so-called liquidation cascade. On October 10, 2025, the crypto market saw its largest liquidation event on record, with more than $19 billion in leveraged positions wiped out in a single day.

To cover losses when positions can't be closed in time, exchanges typically keep an insurance fund. In extreme cases, some use auto-deleveraging, closing profitable positions on the other side of the market to keep the system solvent.

Crypto perps trade on two main types of venue.

Centralized exchanges, such as Binance, Bybit and OKX, run their own order books and hold users' funds. They account for the vast majority of perps trading: Binance alone held about a third of centralized perps volume in early 2026, according to CoinGecko. Many are unavailable to U.S. customers.

Decentralized exchanges let traders use perps through smart contracts, while keeping control of their funds. Some, such as Hyperliquid and Solana-based Phoenix, run order books onchain, matching buyers and sellers much like a traditional exchange. Others, such as GMX and Jupiter, let traders trade against a shared pool of liquidity provided by other users.

Decentralized perps are growing fast. Their trading volume jumped from $1.5 trillion in 2024 to $6.38 trillion in 2025, led by Hyperliquid, CoinGecko found. Decentralized exchanges' share of perps trading volume peaked at 13% in late 2025 before slipping to 10% in April 2026, while their share of open interest, the total value of open positions, reached 13.5%.

In the U.S., perps developed largely on offshore exchanges, outside the reach of the CFTC, but that has started to change. In July 2025, Coinbase introduced CFTC-regulated "perpetual-style" futures for U.S. customers, with five-year expiries and up to 10x leverage. Two months later, CFTC-regulated exchange Bitnomial launched what it called the first U.S. perpetual futures, with 25-year terms and eight-hour funding. In May 2026, the CFTC approved a Bitcoin perpetual futures contract from prediction market Kalshi and set out a framework for listing more, and in June, Kraken rolled out perps to eligible U.S. customers through Bitnomial, which its parent company had acquired.

Some of these contracts, including Kalshi's and Bitnomial's original design, have long but fixed terms of 25 years, so are not strictly perpetual. The regulator says perps can be offered on CFTC-registered exchanges under federal oversight, and has advised U.S. traders to avoid offshore venues.

The shift has been contested. CME Group, the largest U.S. futures exchange, has sued the CFTC, arguing that perps are swaps rather than futures under U.S. law. The CFTC has nonetheless continued to approve more perps platforms, starting with Backpack.

Perps are now spreading beyond crypto. Exchanges have begun listing perps tracking stocks, stock indexes, commodities such as gold and oil, and currencies, giving traders around-the-clock exposure to markets that normally close in the evening and on weekends.

Those products carry the same mechanics, and the same risks, as crypto perps. They track an asset's price without giving traders ownership of the asset itself, so a perp on a company's stock doesn't come with voting rights or dividends.

Perps can be useful tools for hedging and speculation, but they are among the riskiest products in crypto:

Decrypt users can now trade perps directly alongside the news, through the Decrypt Money Account. The service is powered by Phoenix, a decentralized exchange on Solana, and lets users go long or short on crypto, stocks and commodities.

Availability varies by region, and as with any leveraged product, traders should only risk money they can afford to lose.

Decrypt offers perpetual futures trading through the Decrypt Money Account, powered by Phoenix.

#5073. Trump’s SEC nominee Johnson backs formal rulemaking for tokenized stock trading

Former CFTC Commissioner and Democrat Kristin Johnson and ex-SEC aide Steven Levine appeared before lawmakers Thursday.

President Donald Trump’s nominees to fill two vacancies at the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) faced questions about investor protections, regulatory oversight and financial innovation during a Senate confirmation hearing Thursday, including scrutiny of the agency’s approach to tokenized securities.

Kristin Johnson, Trump’s Democratic nominee, backed formal rulemaking to address concerns about the agency’s exemption for tokenized stock trading.

“I feel strongly that regulation is made better through the notice and comment period,” Johnson told the Senate Banking Committee in response to Democratic Senator Angela Alsobrooks. She added that she would welcome the opportunity to address issues covered by the exemption through that process.

The SEC’s Sept. 17 exemption lets qualifying platforms trade blockchain-based versions of US-listed shares without registering as securities exchanges under certain conditions. The order took effect immediately and invites comments on possible changes.

Johnson, a former Commodity Futures Trading Commission commissioner, appeared alongside Republican Steven Levine, a former aide to SEC Commissioner Mark Uyeda. If confirmed, they would bring the five-member commission to four members, with three Republicans and one Democrat.

The hearing comes as the SEC has scaled back enforcement activity and pursued regulatory changes intended to accommodate digital assets, including efforts to facilitate tokenized securities that have drawn scrutiny from lawmakers and market participants.

The measure has drawn objections from publicly traded companies, with 67 opposing the tokenization of their shares on the OKXICE platform, according to a Nov. 5 public notice.

Johnson also cited concerns about Anti-Money Laundering and Know Your Customer compliance, noting that objections to the exemption extended beyond investor protection.

Levine, meanwhile, said he had not closely studied the innovation exemption but understood it to be time-limited and include investor protections. He emphasized that Congress had authorized the SEC to grant exemptions from federal securities laws when doing so serves the public interest and remains consistent with investor protection requirements.

He pointed to exchange-traded funds as an example of financial innovation that initially developed through regulatory exemptions before becoming subject to broader agency rules.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

If confirmed, Johnson would become the SEC’s only Democratic commissioner, a development some in the crypto industry believe could strengthen the agency’s approach to regulation.

“Crypto isn’t a partisan issue, and the rules that come out of a bipartisan commission will stand on firmer footing,” Jessica Martinez, US policy director at digital asset infrastructure company Fireblocks, told Cointelegraph.

Martinez added that even minority commissioners can influence policy by challenging proposals and pushing for unresolved issues to be addressed before rules are finalized.

Senators have until noon Friday to submit additional written questions to the nominees, with responses due by 5:00 pm ET on Nov. 24, according to the committee’s closing remarks.

The nominations could then advance to a committee vote before being considered by the full Senate. Both nominees would require Senate confirmation before taking their seats at the SEC.

Protesters Arrested Outside Nasdaq as Activists Oppose Anthropic IPO Over AI Bubble Risks

He argued that workers have little control over how large institutions invest the savings they depend on for retirement.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The concern centers on faster rules for adding new companies to major stock indexes. Under Nasdaq’s fast-entry policy, an eligible IPO large enough to rank among the Nasdaq-100’s top 40 companies can enter the index after 15 trading days, allowing index-tracking funds to buy the shares sooner than under older rules.

That does not mean all pension funds or 401(k) plans must purchase Anthropic shares, let alone buy them during the IPO. Funds tracking a benchmark that admits Anthropic would normally adjust their holdings, while other managers can make different choices.

Protesters also raised concerns about AI-driven job displacement, the water and energy used by data centers, and AI applications in surveillance and military targeting. Their claims about specific military and law-enforcement harms were allegations made by the campaign, not findings established by the release.

Kawas also criticized AI’s military uses and the prospect of companies replacing workers with automated systems.

One protester identified by organizers as among those arrested called for a freeze on what they described as reckless AI development. “Don’t bail out Big Tech when their bubble inevitably pops,” the protester said.

Just before trading began, Anthropic futures on Hyperliquid’s trade.xyz market were priced at $110.48, according to Senpi at around 4:44 pm UTC. That was just $1.02, or 0.91%, below the stock’s opening price.

The massive debut once again puts crypto’s growing pre-IPO futures market in the spotlight, especially as exchanges compete to attract traders who want to bet on major companies before their shares begin trading on Wall Street. SpaceX’s debut earlier this year also drew interest from crypto traders betting on its stock price through crypto futures.

Perpetual futures allow traders to bet on whether prices will rise or fall without owning the underlying shares, and unlike traditional futures they have no expiration date and can trade around the clock — including before a company goes public.

Trading on Hyperliquid was especially strong just before Anthropic shares opened, with the contract on trade.xyz recording $545.7 million in trading volume over 24 hours and $303.3 million in open interest (OI), according to Senpi data.

The contract was also trading at a 2.58% premium to its oracle price, the same data showed.

This interest had been growing for weeks, according to a Thursday report from crypto research firm Block Scholes, which found that daily trading volume for Anthropic futures on Hyperliquid averaged $75 million since Nov. 5, up from $7 million in September.

Traders also appeared to be betting on Anthropic’s stock rising, according to Token Terminal data, which showed that 5,567 traders held long positions in Anthropic futures on Hyperliquid on Wednesday, compared with 1,996 holding short positions.

Open interest in Anthropic perpetual futures on Hyperliquid reached a record $186 million on Nov. 18. Source: Token Terminal

OI across Anthropic futures markets on Hyperliquid also reached a record $186 million on Wednesday. Of that, $86 million came from trade.xyz contracts, while Entropy.io accounted for about $100 million.

Still, Anthropic futures made up just 1% of Hyperliquid’s total trading volume and accounted for only 3% of the increase in non-crypto trading since September, the Block Scholes report found.

Related: SpaceX tokenized IPO campaign draws $557M on Binance ahead of debut

Binance recorded about $741 million in Anthropic perpetual futures trading over 24 hours, compared with $195 million on OKX, according to DefiLlama. Entropy’s Anthropic market, which also runs on Hyperliquid, recorded about $80 million.

Anthropic shares were trading at $98.49 as of 5:43 pm UTC on Thursday, down about 12% from their $111.50 opening price but still nearly 19% above the $83 IPO price.

Anthropic joins Saudi Aramco and SpaceX as one of just three companies to debut on public markets with a trillion-dollar valuation.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

By 12:18 p.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was down 1.55%, the S&P 500 had lost 1.59%, and the Dow was off 2.03%. Investors remain concerned about expensive AI infrastructure and whether the spending will deliver adequate returns. The weak reaction beyond Anthropic undercut hopes that a blockbuster IPO could revive appetite for beaten-down technology stocks.

Crypto's weakness predates Thursday's listing. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs have shed roughly $2.9 billion this month, including $306.4 million Wednesday, amid rising Treasury yields and expectations of further Federal Reserve rate hikes.

Some traders argue the IPO has diverted capital from crypto, but that remains unproven. As Decrypt previously noted, Bitcoin and AI stocks have declined together, suggesting a wider retreat from risk rather than a simple rotation into Anthropic shares.

Bitcoin's daily chart remains decisively bearish. At around $62,640, BTC trades below both its 50-day and 200-day exponential moving averages (EMAs), with the shorter average beneath the longer one.

This is a bearish alignment commonly associated with an EMA-based death cross. Traditionally, a death cross occurs when the 50-day simple moving average crosses below the 200-day average, signaling deteriorating shorter-term momentum. The EMA variation responds more quickly to recent prices. The crossover was confirmed today.

Momentum reinforces the picture. The relative strength index, or RSI, reads 21.3, well below the 30 threshold traders associate with oversold conditions. That can precede a relief rally, but oversold assets can keep falling.

The average directional index, or ADX, stands at 41.2. ADX measures the strength of a trend, not its direction, with readings above 25 generally indicating a strong trend. Here, the negative directional indicator exceeds its positive counterpart, confirming sellers have the advantage. A high ADX reading alone does not mean a reversal is imminent, even with RSI already deep in oversold territory.

Bitcoin has also been making lower highs and lower lows since September, with November's sharp drop slicing through several support levels. A bullish recovery would first need to reclaim $66,512, followed by resistance around $69,292 and $71,539. Above those levels, $73,785 and then $76,984 would present tougher tests of the prevailing bearish structure.

On the downside, the chart marks immediate support near $62,019, almost directly beneath the current price. A decisive daily close below that level could expose the psychological $60,000 threshold, followed by the chart's next Fibonacci extension around $56,841.

The RSI leaves room for a bounce, but the EMAs and ADX show how much work buyers still face. A move through $66,512 would open a test of $69,292; a close under $62,019 would instead put $60,000 back in focus.

Hackers Leak Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Customer Data

Three of the biggest names in payments, Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Chase, disclosed that some of their customers’ data was exposed after hackers breached third-party vendors, the latest jolt in a week of tech failures that has left Americans uneasy about the systems underpinning daily life.

The data was leaked Thursday by a ransomware group, according to a report from The Wall Street Journal. Crucially, all three firms said their own networks were not breached and that no payment systems were compromised.

A Visa spokesperson said no payment data such as card numbers was affected and that the incident involved a third-party service provider in the Asia-Pacific region.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Mastercard said a “limited amount of information from a subset of cardholders enrolled in a specific benefit program” was exposed, with no impact on its payment network.

JPMorgan said attackers targeted outside service providers and likely accessed marketing data, potentially including customer names and email addresses, with no disruption to customer services.

A ransomware group calling itself n0n claimed responsibility, according to cybersecurity firm SOCRadar. None of the three companies named the breached vendor.

Earlier in the week, JPMorgan customers hit problems accessing accounts and sending payments, which the bank attributed to a software update, and the issue spread into broader complaints about other banks.

Paired with the FAA temporarily grounding flights and an earlier Microsoft outage that locked people out of email, the week stoked unusual public anxiety about the fragility of critical tech. JPMorgan was explicit that the data leak had “no connection” to the earlier service disruptions.

For crypto users, the breach lands squarely within a growing danger that customer data, not just funds, is the soft underbelly of finance.

AI has supercharged both the scale and sophistication of cyberattacks, and 2026 has brought a steady drumbeat of data exposures. The record $6.3 billion Binance hack, tied to North Korea, laid bare how aggressively state and criminal actors are targeting financial infrastructure, while a wave of breaches has hit crypto firms through third-party vendors, echoing the vendor weakness at the center of this latest incident.

The payments giants have all leaned deeper into crypto and stablecoins this year, from Visa and Mastercard’s settlement programs to JPMorgan’s growing digital-asset footprint, making their security posture increasingly relevant to the onchain economy they’re racing to join.

For now, the firms say the damage is contained, but the episode adds to a mounting sense that no institution, however large, is immune.

#5067. MoonPay seeks US bank charter for stablecoin, custody services

MoonPay’s proposed national trust bank would offer stablecoin issuance and redemption, reserve management and digital asset custody under federal oversight.

Crypto payments firm MoonPay has applied for a US national trust bank charter to offer stablecoin issuance and redemption, reserve management and digital asset custody services.

According to an OCC filing, MoonPay submitted its application on Tuesday to establish MoonPay National Trust Bank, a non-depository institution headquartered in Miami Beach, Florida. The proposed bank would not accept traditional deposits or issue loans.

MoonPay already provides stablecoin issuance infrastructure to businesses, including support for branded digital dollars. If approved, the charter would allow its proposed banking subsidiary to provide stablecoin and custody services under federal supervision.

The application comes as US regulators prepare to implement the GENIUS Act, which takes effect on Jan. 18, 2027, and establishes federal standards for stablecoin issuers, including reserve backing and redemption requirements.

With the filing, MoonPay joins several crypto and payments companies seeking federal trust bank charters, including Rain, Modern Treasury and Block. Coinbase’s charter took effect earlier this month, while Circle received final OCC approval in July.

The rise in crypto firms applying for federal trust bank charters has drawn opposition from community banks. In October, the Independent Community Bankers of America sued the OCC, arguing that the regulator exceeded its authority by allowing non-depository trust banks to conduct activities traditionally associated with commercial banks.
