#5065. Meet SHRINCS: Bitcoin's Emerging Frontrunner for a Post-Quantum Signature

Meet SHRINCS: Bitcoin's Emerging Frontrunner for a Post-Quantum Signature

Blockstream Research Director Jonas Nick, with Mikhail Kudinov and collaborators, published a security analysis on Thursday for SHRINCS, advancing an emerging frontrunner for a post-quantum signature scheme in Bitcoin.

“We need all the possible feedback—engineers, cryptographers, protocol developers,” Kudinov, a post-quantum cryptography researcher at Blockstream, said in reference to a draft of SHRINCS’ Bitcoin Improvement Proposal (BIP). “There are a lot of things to check, test, complain about, or praise."

Notably, SHRINCS is a hash-based signature scheme that leverages SHA-256 to anchor its security in battle-tested primitives that are already used by Bitcoin, representing a conservative effort to minimize the proposal’s trust assumptions.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That said, the scheme comes with trade-offs, most notably by introducing potential security vulnerabilities at the implementation level if misused. The scheme combines two spending paths: a small, more efficient stateful signature option and a larger, more costly stateless path with fewer security risks on the implementation side.

Although stateful signatures are compact, preserving Bitcoin’s ability to process transactions efficiently, a user can trigger a critical failure—where an attacker can forge a signature for that address and steal funds if a one-time state is accidentally reused. Because SHRINCS’ stateful path relies on one-time keys that can only safely sign a single transaction, wallet developers must carefully track each key’s state.

“It needs to be impossible for the user to behave in a way that this catastrophic event happens,” Nick told Head of MARA Foundation Isabel Foxen Duke during a recent appearance on MARA Foundation TV. “Wallet developers need to deal with this stateful component correctly.”

According to Nick, for SHRINCS to work safely in the real world, its specification and corresponding wallet implementations must automatically enforce the stateless path fallback in any situation where a state counter could possibly be compromised.

While some users might gravitate toward stateless signatures out of caution, Nick warns that widespread adoption could bloat the blockchain and drive up fees across the network.

Bitcoin’s current ECDSA signatures are typically 71 bytes long (and Taproot Schnorr signatures are 64 bytes). Under SHRINCS, stateful signatures are anywhere from 548 to 4,619 bytes, while stateless ones are 5,777 bytes. That said, optimizations of both signature paths have been accelerating with the support of AI-based research, Nick noted.

“We don’t know what the fee market will look like once we have these very big signatures,” he said. “And it’s not just the fee market, it’s also just fewer transactions fit into a block, meaning fewer people can use Bitcoin. And I think this changes the value proposition of Bitcoin.”

As the SHRINCS working group—which includes researchers backed by nonprofits including Brink and OpenSats—continues to refine the proposal’s specification, Blockstream researchers stress that getting the cryptographic foundation right is only half the battle.

Whether the broader Bitcoin ecosystem is ready to embrace SHRINCS’ inherent trade-offs will depend heavily on feedback from the network’s stakeholders in the months ahead. Meanwhile, Nick and Kudinov are requesting review of the proposal and the security analysis.

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

Anthropic's blockbuster debut demonstrates demand for its shares, but Thursday's broader decline shows that Wall Street is not ready to extend that confidence to every company financing or supplying the AI boom.

Guillemet has been a central figure in Ledger’s security operations, first as chief security officer and later as chief technology officer. “The threat landscape changed too. AI is making attacks cheaper, faster and easier to scale,” he added.

“The question we spent a decade answering for crypto (how do you protect what matters when any device can be compromised?) is now a question for every company, every AI agent and every government.”

Guillemet did not disclose details of his next venture but said he was “building something new” and would “dedicate my time to solving this security mess.”

Guillemet’s announcement comes weeks after Ledger disclosed that attackers had modified genuine hardware wallets with hidden components designed to capture users’ recovery phrases, and slipped the devices into authorized resellers.

The tampering cases were initially reported in Asia, with subsequent incidents identified in Europe and the United Arab Emirates.

#5062. Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi has updated its online banking terms to make customers responsible for the actions of third-party AI agents accessing their accounts, including when an agent leaks their credentials and someone else uses them to get in.

The change, reported by TechCrunch Wednesday, makes explicit how the bank intends to handle an increasingly awkward question: Who pays when software handling your money makes a mistake?

Under Citi’s new terms, actions taken using credentials supplied to an outside agent are treated as authorized by the customer, even if the tool ignores instructions, exceeds its permissions, or shares those credentials with another party.

Citi says it has no obligation to investigate whether the agent followed the customer's original prompts, subject to applicable law.

A Citi spokesperson told TechCrunch that the November revision clarifies the risks of sharing banking credentials or granting outside tools account access. The provision does not apply to AI agents offered by Citi itself.

Citi is not acting alone. JPMorgan and Bank of America announced similar policies in October, while Citizens Bank warned customers this week by email that they could be responsible for what their AI assistants do.

PNC also changed its digital agreement on Wednesday. Its rules distinguish bank-approved connections, which can provide limited access, from handing an agent unrestricted access to an account.

The move follows a banking security scare from October 22. Major banks had detected AI agents logging into customer accounts, sometimes taking steps that appeared to go beyond their users' instructions, and executives said they struggled to determine which agents had been properly authorized.

An AI agent differs from a conventional chatbot because it can act on a user's behalf, potentially navigating websites, accessing financial records, or initiating payments. That makes a mistaken instruction or compromised login more consequential than a bad answer about a bank balance.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency has since issued AI examiner guidance that addresses outside agents, payment controls, and human approval. The framework is nonbinding and does not itself create new requirements for banks.

Citi's language does not automatically eliminate consumer fraud protections. Under U.S. federal consumer protection rules, banks generally must investigate unauthorized electronic transfers and limit customers' financial liability for fraud. Those protections cannot simply be waived through a banking agreement, although their application to transactions performed by customer-authorized AI agents remains less clear.

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau says banks cannot use agreements to waive those legal protections, although the rules distinguish theft by an outsider from transfers actually authorized by a customer or someone given access.

Whether a particular AI-related loss qualifies as unauthorized depends on how the transaction occurred.

PNC's updated agreement takes a stricter operational approach: Customers may use certain connection methods approved by the bank, but cannot give third-party AI agents their passwords or one-time security codes, and agents generally cannot initiate payments unless PNC expressly permits it.

The 13F filings, analyzed by Galaxy Research, show that the reporting companies added an equivalent of 49,229 Bitcoin (BTC) in the third quarter, outpacing the 47,054 Bitcoin they shed over the last three quarters combined. This lifted the reported total holdings to 356,491 BTC, the highest on record.

“The quarter’s accumulation was the work of a few dozen large institutions,” wrote Galaxy’s head of firmwide research Alex Thorn.

Quarterly 13F filings provide a point-in-time snapshot of US-listed securities held by reporting institutional investment managers. Thorn said half of the net addition of spot Bitcoin ETF shares came from advisors and wealth platforms.

Wells Fargo posted the largest increase, adding the equivalent of 14,636 Bitcoin, making it the largest institutional holder of spot Bitcoin ETPs. Other strong gainers included Brevan Howard, Bank of America, Millennium Management and Jane Street.

Largest holders of spot Bitcoin ETP positions in Q3. Source: Galaxy Research

Bitcoin equivalent held by 13F filers. Source: Galaxy Research

#5060. CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

The UK's main crypto trade association has called on the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) to make sure that developers and governance participants in decentralized finance protocols are not treated as regulated firms by default.

CryptoUK set out its position in a response to the FCA's consultation paper CP26/36, which proposes guidance on the "perimeter" of the UK's new crypto regime: which activities fall inside it and need FCA authorization, and which don't. The group, which says it represents more than 150 crypto companies, submitted its response on Wednesday, November 18, the day the consultation closed, and published it a day later.

Yesterday, CryptoUK submitted its response to the Financial Conduct Authority’s Consultation Paper CP26/36, which proposes amendments to the Perimeter Guidance Manual (PERG) to reflect the FSMA 2000 (Cryptoassets) (Miscellaneous Amendments) Regulations 2026.

The consultation… pic.twitter.com/oOrZgAWscP

— CryptoUK (@CryptoUKAssoc) November 19, 2026

"Software development, maintenance, security work and participation in decentralised governance should not, by themselves, establish that a person carries on a regulated activity," CryptoUK wrote. That would be consistent with the FCA's "clear controlling person" approach to DeFi, under which regulation should hinge on who effectively controls a financial service, it argued.

CryptoUK said it "broadly" supports the draft guidance, which reflects further amendments to the UK's crypto legislation that the government laid in September. The FCA aims to publish its final guidance in January 2027. It welcomed new exclusions from the regime for temporary settlement and payment arrangements, assets backing UK-recognized stablecoins, proprietary trading and market making, and technical service providers.

But in several places, the draft "goes further than the legislation," the group said. It pointed to a proposed link to the UK based on where a protocol is developed or governed, requirements in the exclusions for payments and proprietary trading, and indicators that would narrow the exclusion for technical services. The FCA has acknowledged that the timing of the latest amendments created uncertainty for some firms, and said it will give applicants extra time where questions over the technical services exclusion held up their applications for authorization.

The group asked the FCA to publish a public record of stablecoins that qualify under the UK regime, to clarify how stablecoins differ from electronic money, and to include more examples of activities that typically fall outside the perimeter.

CryptoUK also urged the FCA to raise with the Treasury outcomes that put UK firms at a disadvantage, citing the treatment of UK technical providers that offer access to regulated overseas trading venues, and of payments using stablecoins issued abroad. The UK should align with the EU's MiCA framework "where appropriate," it said. The response was prepared with law firm CMS and signed by CryptoUK policy director Nick Brooks.

The consultation is part of the FCA's work to build out the UK's crypto rulebook. The regulator finalized its core rules for crypto firms in June, ahead of a regime due to take effect on October 25, 2027, and later issued guidance two weeks before its authorization window for crypto firms opened. It has also set out a shared vision for tokenization with the Bank of England.

#5059. Anthropic’s $81B IPO draws half the net retail buying of SpaceX’s

Retail investors traded $772 million in Anthropic shares on Thursday, the most Vanda Research has recorded for a market debut since 2012.

Retail investors traded $772 million in Anthropic shares on its Nasdaq debut Thursday, but their net purchases totaled just $60 million, about half the $118 million they put into SpaceX on its first day of trading in June.

Anthropic’s first-day retail trading volume topped SpaceX’s $669 million and was the highest for any market debut in Vanda Research’s data, which go back to 2012, Yahoo Finance reported.

Volume counts both purchases and sales, while net buying measures how much purchases exceeded sales.

The Claude AI developer and its existing shareholders offered 980 million shares at $83 each in the $81.34 billion initial public offering (IPO). Anthropic shares opened on Nasdaq at $111.50 on Thursday, 34.3% above the IPO price, and closed at $97.17 for a first-day gain of 17.1%.

SpaceX shares gained 19.2% in their June 12 debut, closing at $160.95 against an IPO price of $135, according to Nasdaq. Although Anthropic’s base offering exceeded SpaceX’s initial $75 billion sale, SpaceX ultimately raised $85.7 billion after underwriters exercised their option to purchase additional shares.

Before the IPO, Vanda forecast about $250 million in net retail buying, more than four times Thursday’s total. Its forecast drew on retail trading in past market debuts and the volume of Anthropic perpetual futures traded on decentralized exchange Hyperliquid.

Shortly before Nasdaq trading began, those futures traded at $110.48, just 0.9% below the stock’s opening price.

#5058. SEC charges 8 firms over fake crypto platforms tied to $37M fraud

The SEC says the operators showed investors fake certificates and pointed them to filings on the agency’s website as proof of regulatory approval.

The US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) charged eight Colorado-based entities Thursday, alleging they used fake crypto trading platforms to take $37.4 million from more than 1,500 US investors.

Four of the defendants posed as investment advisers, and each steered investors to a fake trading platform run by its partner, according to the SEC’s complaint. The four pairs operated in succession from August 2024 to May 2026.

The defendants allegedly lured investors by masquerading as investment advisers or representatives of crypto asset trading platforms, which the SEC described as fronts designed to steal investor money, the agency announced in a Thursday press release.

The SEC said the operators were likely overseas and used social media ads to lure investors into private chat groups. They first offered stock-trading advice before persuading victims to transfer money to fake crypto platforms, where victims believed they were buying genuine crypto assets.

The SEC previously warned investors that scammers used investment-related group chats to promote fraud schemes, including by impersonating financial professionals, according to a December 2025 alert.

The scheme allegedly operated through four successive adviser-platform pairs from August 2024 to May 2026, with operators switching pairs every few months.

The eight entities charged were Advisor WeChat Capital, Tech Brokerage Asset Alliance Ltd., HB Wealth Advisors, IdeaCrypto Exchange LLC, Convergent Wealth Advisors Inc., RealSimple Crypto Exchange Inc., Associated Wealth Advisors Ltd., and Essential Crypto Exchange.

“The SEC continues to seek out and expose fraudulent investment scams that prey on U.S. investors,” said Laura D’Allaird, chief of the SEC Enforcement Division’s Cyber and Emerging Technologies Unit, encouraging the public to report such schemes through its online portal.

During fiscal year 2026, the SEC filed 348 enforcement actions, down 23.7% from 456 a year earlier. The SEC filed 13 crypto-related actions in calendar year 2025, down from 33 in 2024, according to Cornerstone Research.

#5057. Coinbase Targets In-House Prediction Markets by First Half of 2027: Report

Coinbase Targets In-House Prediction Markets by First Half of 2027: Report

Coinbase is planning to launch its own prediction market contracts in the U.S. in the first half of 2027, as it looks to take more control of one of its fastest-growing businesses.

The exchange plans to offer the in-house event contracts to both retail and institutional customers, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing a person familiar with the matter.

A Coinbase spokesperson told Bloomberg the company had "nothing new to announce today," and pointed to plans it set out in October to expand its prediction markets and improve their economics by building its own clearing and other infrastructure. The spokesperson did not address the timing of an in-house launch.

Coinbase rolled out prediction markets across the U.S. through a partnership with Kalshi, after unveiling the product alongside stock trading last year. Offering its own contracts would give Coinbase more say over which markets it lists, and let it keep more of the revenue from trades.

The exchange has been laying the groundwork for its pivot. In December 2025, it agreed to buy prediction markets startup The Clearing Company, and in September 2026 it received approval from the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) for its own clearinghouse. Coinbase already runs a regulated derivatives exchange.

Prediction markets have become a bright spot for Coinbase as crypto trading slows. The business passed $200 million in annualized revenue in the third quarter, with daily traders up more than 60% from the previous quarter, and revenue growing 82% a quarter on average since launch, the company reported in October.

"Prediction markets are adding another revenue stream and are less correlated to crypto volatility," CFO Alesia Haas said at the time. On an analyst call the same day, Haas said Coinbase's new clearinghouse meant it "can launch our own contracts, and we can also then earn better economics," while continuing to offer contracts through partners such as Kalshi. Asked when, she said: "We have not shared a timeline."

The products have faced legal challenges from states that argue some event contracts amount to unlicensed gambling. Coinbase sued three states over their attempts to regulate prediction markets, and New York sued Coinbase and Gemini over their offerings in April.

Last week, a Louisiana judge temporarily barred more than 30 prediction market providers, including Coinbase's derivatives arm, from offering event contracts in the state, after Attorney General Liz Murrill sued them on November 12, Forbes reported. The CFTC has sued Louisiana in federal court to block the order, and a hearing on a longer injunction is set for November 30.

Decrypt's parent company, Dastan, operates its own prediction market, Decrypt Predict.

#5055. Bitcoin nears repeat of chart pattern that preceded 2023’s $25K retest

Bitcoin’s 21-day moving average fell below its 200-day average, and analyst Keith Alan expects a second bearish crossover by Sunday.

Bitcoin (BTC) could repeat a pair of bearish moving-average crossovers that preceded its retest of $25,000 in September 2023, according to analyst Keith Alan.

New analysis from Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, compares this week’s Bitcoin chart with a similar setup in 2023.

On Bitstamp’s BTC/USD daily chart, the 21-day simple moving average (SMA) crossed below its 200-day equivalent on Thursday. The move last occurred in early November 2025, around one month after Bitcoin’s latest all-time high of $126,200, in the early stages of the current bear market. The 50-day SMA crossed below the 200-day SMA later that month, forming a conventional death cross.

Alan expects the 50-day SMA to cross below the 100-day SMA by the end of the week.

“We’ve only seen this happen once before in Bitcoin’s history, and it should not be ignored,” he summarized in a post on X.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21-, 50-, 100- and 200-day SMAs. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The only other time in Bitcoin’s history that the two pairs of moving averages both produced a so-called “death cross” in the same period was between August and September 2023. BTC/USD retested support near $25,000 on Sept. 11.

Alan describes this double bottom as the start of a rally that took Bitcoin to its 2023 high of $44,700 in early December — an increase of 80%.

“One occurrence is by no means a large enough sample to consider a reliable pattern, or expect similar returns/drawdowns, however the timing, placement, and candle characteristics have enough similarities that we can’t ignore it either,” he said.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21-, 50-, 100- and 200-day SMAs. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Previously, Cointelegraph reported on Bitcoin losing support at the 200-week SMA for the first time since mid-August. The trend line typically functions as support during bull-market retracements, but it can act as resistance when Bitcoin rebounds from below it.

Related: Institutions pile back into US Bitcoin ETFs in Q3 after selling streak

BTC/USD fell to $61,231 on Friday, its lowest level since July 3, a day after Anthropic’s record IPO and Nasdaq debut. The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index finished Thursday’s trading session down 2.25% at 23,879 points, its lowest daily close since April 14.

Anthropic shares traded around $91.50 before Friday’s open. It closed Thursday at $97.17, 20% below the day’s $115 peak.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#5054. Bitcoin ETFs Shed $582M in a Day as BTC Drops Below $62K

Bitcoin ETFs Shed $582M in a Day as BTC Drops Below $62K

Bitcoin slid below $62,000 on Friday, extending a weeklong sell-off as investors pulled money from spot Bitcoin ETFs and braced for another interest rate hike from the Federal Reserve.

The leading cryptocurrency was trading at around $61,780, down 2.8% over the past day and 10% over the past week, after touching a 24-hour low of $61,273—its lowest level since early July—according to CoinGecko data. It has fallen more than $2,000 since struggling to hold $64,000 earlier this week, and has also formed a so-called death cross, a bearish chart pattern.

The drop triggered $727 million in liquidations across crypto markets over the past 24 hours, about 86% of which were long positions betting on higher prices, according to CoinGlass. Bitcoin accounted for just under $202 million of the total, with more than 151,000 traders liquidated.

Spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $582.2 million in net outflows on Thursday, their largest single-day outflow since October 30, according to SoSoValue data. The funds have lost more than $1.1 billion so far this week and almost $3.5 billion in November, while their total net assets have fallen to $76.6 billion.

"Within crypto, the most telling sign is this month's outflow from spot Bitcoin ETFs, which suggests investors are choosing to stay on the sidelines for now," Neil Steinhardt, head of Nexo U.S., told Decrypt.

Treasury yields above 5% and expectations that the Fed will raise rates in December have weighed on assets that don't pay interest, including Bitcoin and gold, Steinhardt said. "Because the pressure appears tied to the rate backdrop, and that backdrop has shifted before, a turn in yields or the dollar would likely be the first signal that appetite is returning," he added, pointing to the December 4 jobs report and the Fed's December 9 meeting as the next checkpoints.

Fed Governor Christopher Waller has called for a larger, half-point increase at that meeting, unless policymakers signal another rise in January. Traders are betting on a smaller move: CME FedWatch puts the odds of a quarter-point hike on December 9 at 99.2%, with the chance of a half-point increase falling to almost zero from more than 9% on Thursday morning.

The decline has left the average Bitcoin holder only just in profit. Bitcoin is still trading above its realized price, the average price at which every coin on the network last moved, but the margin is thin, onchain analytics firm Glassnode tweeted on Friday. A break below would put holders in loss for the first time since early 2023, it said.

The average Bitcoin holder is still in profit.

bitcoin:native trades above its realized price, the average cost of every coin on the network. The margin is thin: MVRV sits just above 1.

A break below would put holders in loss for the first time since early 2023. pic.twitter.com/rPOTbZS1NF

— glassnode (@glassnode) November 20, 2026

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill sees the slide as part of a longer downturn. "I see this as a continuation of the existing bear market rather than the start of another," he said in a note published on Thursday, pointing to selling by large holders since late 2025, expectations of higher rates and weakness in tech stocks.

CoinShares' base case is for Bitcoin to trade between $60,000 and $90,000 through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives the Fed room to ease, Butterfill said.

#5053. MoonPay Applies for National Trust Bank Charter to Grow Custody and Stablecoin Business

MoonPay Applies for National Trust Bank Charter to Grow Custody and Stablecoin Business

Crypto payments firm MoonPay has applied for a U.S. national bank charter, joining a growing list of crypto companies seeking a federal banking license.

The proposed MoonPay National Trust Bank would be based in Miami Beach, Florida, according to the company's November 17 application to the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC), Bloomberg reported on Thursday. The charter would help MoonPay grow its custody business and expand its stablecoin services, according to the application.

MoonPay confirmed the move on Thursday, tweeting that it was "proud to share" that it had applied for a U.S. national bank charter with the OCC.

today we are proud to share that MoonPay has applied for a US National Bank Charter with the OCC 🇺🇸 pic.twitter.com/XGHBPx84v0

Unlike a full-service bank, a national trust bank can hold and manage assets for clients, but can't take deposits or lend money. The OCC did not immediately respond to Bloomberg's request for comment.

Founded in 2019, MoonPay is best known for providing on- and off-ramps between fiat currencies and crypto. It has been expanding into new areas including AI, having launched a crypto wallet designed for AI chatbots, let users buy crypto through ChatGPT, and acquired AI trading copilot startup Dawn Labs.

It has also been courting institutions. In April, it acquired Sodot, a Tel Aviv-based crypto key management firm, to form the basis of MoonPay Institutional, a unit serving banks, asset managers and trading firms. The unit is led by former acting Commodity Futures Trading Commission chair Caroline Pham.

MoonPay is the latest crypto firm to seek a federal charter under President Donald Trump's administration. Earlier this month, Coinbase joined Circle and Ripple in receiving final OCC approval for a national trust bank, after Ripple won approval for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin. Jack Dorsey's Block has also applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin, while World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture tied to the Trump family, won conditional approval for its own trust company in August.

The approvals have drawn pushback. A banking group has sued over Block's application, accusing crypto firms of seeking "side door" access to banking, while Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's approvals of Coinbase and Ripple were illegal, a claim the crypto industry has disputed.

Warren is expected to chair the Senate Banking Committee from January, and is expected to examine World Liberty's charter as part of a wider look at Trump-linked crypto ventures.

#5052. VanEck to end Gemini ETF custody deals, keeps Coinbase

VanEck plans to end Gemini’s custody agreements for three crypto ETFs in February 2027, while Coinbase will continue serving as a custodian for the funds.

VanEck is cutting ties with Gemini as a custodian across three cryptocurrency exchange-traded funds (ETFs), ending a relationship that began with its first spot Bitcoin ETF.

VanEck notified Gemini on Monday that it would terminate custody and clearing agreements for its Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and Solana (SOL) ETFs, according to separate filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) on Thursday.

The agreements will end “on or about” Feb. 16, 2027, while Coinbase Custody, which already serves the funds, will continue providing custody services.

The move raises questions about Gemini’s position in the US spot crypto ETF market, where Coinbase serves as custodian for many of the largest funds. The filings did not disclose the reasons for the terminations.

The termination extends beyond cryptocurrency custody. VanEck’s Solana ETF (VSOL) filing also covers a Gemini staking services agreement, raising questions about how the fund will handle staking after the transition.

Staking allows tokenholders to earn rewards by helping secure a proof-of-stake (PoS) blockchain such as Solana. For ETFs that stake their holdings, the service can provide an additional source of returns for the fund.

Cointelegraph contacted VanEck and Gemini for comment on the reason for terminating the agreements and the potential impact on the Solana ETF’s staking operations, but had not received a response by publication.

Gemini’s founders, Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss, were among the earliest proponents of spot Bitcoin ETFs in the United States, filing to launch the Winklevoss Bitcoin Trust in July 2013. US regulators did not approve the first spot Bitcoin ETFs until January 2024, more than a decade after the brothers’ initial filing.

Gemini became the original Bitcoin custodian for VanEck’s spot Bitcoin ETF when the fund launched in January 2024, before VanEck added Coinbase Custody in June that year.

Related: Gemini’s quarterly retail trading volume sinks to 3-year low

VanEck’s Bitcoin ETF (HODL) is considerably smaller than the largest US spot Bitcoin funds, holding about $880 million in assets under management, compared with $47.6 billion for BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) and $10.6 billion for Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC), according to Wallet Pilot data as of Thursday.

Gemini has also provided custody services for crypto ETFs in Canada and Australia. In the US, it continues to serve as a custodian for at least one smaller fund, the 7RCC Spot Bitcoin and Carbon Credit Futures ETF (BTCK), which identified Gemini and BitGo as its Bitcoin custodians in a September 2026 regulatory filing.

#5051. Morning Minute: Anthropic IPO Pops, Sinks Markets

Morning Minute: Anthropic IPO Pops, Sinks Markets

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

The largest base IPO deal ever priced at $83 a share Wednesday night, opened Thursday morning at $111.50, and briefly pushed Anthropic’s fully diluted valuation past $2 trillion. The stock finished at $97.17, up 17% from the offer and down 13% from where it opened (it has since fallen about 15% to $88).

A 17% first-day gain on an $81.34 billion raise is a successful listing by any normal standard.

The rest of the market did not fare as well.

The Nasdaq fell 2.06%. The S&P 500 dropped 1.9%. The Dow lost 2.17% and closed below 50,000 for the first time since June. Tech had rallied before the bell on the listing and reversed by the afternoon.

MD fell 7.73%. Nebius dropped 7.8%. CoreWeave lost 5%. Nvidia slid 3.4% despite beating earnings last week. Oracle, Micron, Broadcom and Microsoft all finished lower. The biggest AI listing in history happened, and AI infrastructure was the worst place to be holding.

Joe Maher at Capital Economics put it plainly: “Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO has failed to rekindle enthusiasm for tech stocks.”

Crypto went with it. Bitcoin fell to $61,500, its lowest since early July. That’s a seventh straight weekly loss, which would be the longest run since the nine-week stretch in 2022. Eth hit the $1,750 level, down 28% in thirty days. Gold fell to a fourteen-month low. Silver dropped 3%.

When Bitcoin, equities and gold all sell off on the same session, the common factor is usually the discount rate.

That’s the Fed. Chris Waller spent this week making the case for fifty basis points in December, and the minutes showed he isn’t alone. FxPro’s Alex Kuptsikevich described the market as spooked by “the spectre of a double rate hike” and pointed out Bitcoin is now more than 7% below its 200-week moving average, the deepest it has traded below that line in three years.

So where does this leave us heading into next week?

Start with what an $81 billion IPO actually does to a market. That money came from somewhere. Add the separate $26 billion private placement from Nvidia, Google, AMD and four other suppliers, and roughly $107 billion of capital got committed to a single name in one day. Kuptsikevich raised the possibility that the listing absorbed the riskiest money in the system, and that if so, some of it rotates back out once the allocation settles.

That’s the bull case for crypto over the next few weeks. It requires the Fed to cooperate.

For the bear case, one can simply look at SpaceX.

SpaceX priced at $135 in June, opened at $150, and closed its first day up 19%. Then it kept running, hitting $225.64 four days later, up 67% from the offer. The unwind started the following session. By June 22 it had fallen 16% in a single day and sat 31% off the high. By mid-July it traded below its IPO price. By late July it was down roughly 50% from the peak.

That would project out to a market low around Christmas time, which is actually how the 2018 midterm cycle played out. Will history repeat itself? We will find out soon enough. In the interim, expect volatility.

#5050. Binance hacker moves nearly $600M in ETH, routes funds through THORChain, Chainflip

OnChain Lens tracked nearly $600 million in ETH transfers over two days as the Binance hackers moved the stolen funds through fresh wallets and cross-chain protocols to obscure their trail.

Wallets linked to the Binance hack moved nearly $600 million in Ether over two days, with some of the stolen funds routed through cross-chain swap protocols, according to blockchain analysts.

The attackers moved about $594 million in Ether (ETH) to fresh wallets, $248 million on Thursday and another 197,460 ETH, worth approximately $346 million, on Friday. Attackers routed part of the funds through cross-chain protocols THORChain and Chainflip, according to blockchain data provider Onchain Lens.

About $5 million was routed through Chainflip, while THORChain accounted for most of the cross-chain activity tracked by blockchain analyst SomaXBT, according to estimates he shared in a Friday X response. Onchain analyst Specter also estimated that only a “small portion” of the funds moved through Chainflip, which has said it screens deposits for illicit funds, although it previously acknowledged gaps in those checks.

North Korean hackers have previously used THORChain to launder funds stolen from some of the largest crypto hacks, including the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in February 2025 and the $388 million Bitget hack in September 2026. TRM Labs and Chainalysis have linked the $6.3 billion Binance hack to North Korea-linked hacking group TraderTraitor.

Following the latest transfers, the Binance hackers’ publicly tracked addresses held approximately 224,722 ETH, worth about $394 million, according to blockchain data platform Arkham.

THORChain’s 24-hour swap volume soared to $239 million on Friday, pushing the protocol’s earnings to $243,000, according to THORChain’s dashboard.

#5049. Lagarde warns Europe could miss chance to unify tokenized markets

The ECB president said Europe’s market for tokenized securities could split into platforms that cannot settle trades with one another.

European Central Bank (ECB) President Christine Lagarde warned Friday that Europe needs common rules for tokenized securities before incompatible standards take hold across trading platforms.

Speaking at the Frankfurt European Banking Congress, Lagarde said blockchain technology lets firms issue, trade and settle securities on a single platform. The global markets for tokenized assets was worth about 23 billion euros in March, five times its size a year earlier, she said.

Europe could build a single market for those assets, Lagarde said, but if platforms adopt different rules as they grow, they may be unable to settle trades with one another. She said lawmakers have a role alongside the ECB.

“Common rules will be needed too, and those lie with legislators,” she said.

The Eurosystem launched Pontes in September, giving financial institutions a way to settle tokenized securities trades in central bank money. Earlier in November, the European Stability Mechanism used Pontes to settle a 1 billion euro (about $1.1 million) digital bond.

Through a second project, Appia, the Eurosystem is working with market participants on common standards for tokenized securities. It plans to publish a blueprint in 2028.

#5048. Senate Passes Bill Banning Prediction Market Bets on War, Terrorism and Death

Senate Passes Bill Banning Prediction Market Bets on War, Terrorism and Death

The Senate has passed a bill that would ban prediction markets from offering bets on war, terrorism, assassinations and deaths, sending the measure to the House of Representatives.

The Discouraging Exploitative Assassination, Tragedy, and Harm Betting in Event Trading Systems Act, or DEATH BETS Act, passed the Senate by voice vote on Wednesday, November 19, with an amendment, according to Congress.gov. The bill was introduced by Sen. Adam Schiff (D-CA) in March, and is cosponsored by Sen. John Curtis (R-UT).

"With this bill, Congress is making it clear that these bets should be illegal," Schiff tweeted after the vote.

BREAKING: The Senate just passed my bill with @SenJohnCurtis to prohibit prediction markets from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war, military operations, or an individual’s death.

With this bill, Congress is making it clear that these bets should be… pic.twitter.com/Mzu5PWmGsL

— Senator Adam Schiff (@SenAdamSchiff) November 20, 2026

The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act to bar exchanges and clearinghouses registered with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) from listing or clearing event contracts that expressly reference terrorism, assassination, war or U.S. military operations.

It would also ban contracts that, at the time of listing, are "reasonably expected to resolve principally based on a death of an individual." The CFTC would be able to extend the ban to "any similar activity" by rule.

The bill would not affect the status of other types of contracts covered by existing CFTC rules on event contracts, such as those involving gaming, according to its text.

Prediction markets such as Kalshi and Polymarket let users trade on the outcome of real-world events, from elections and sports to geopolitics. Markets on wars and the fates of world leaders have drawn criticism from lawmakers, who have argued they let people profit from tragedy, and could reward insiders with knowledge of military plans. Polymarket users have been accused of insider trading after making $2.4 million on Iran-related predictions. Polymarket pulled a market on whether a nuclear weapon would be detonated after a public backlash.

Senate Democrats have raised concerns that people close to President Donald Trump could profit from war-related markets, and one senator has alleged White House corruption over Iran war predictions. Kalshi was also sued after refusing to pay out on a market tied to the death of Iran's then-Supreme Leader, Ali Khamenei.

The CFTC has also moved to curb such markets. In June, it proposed rules that would ban markets on outcomes that could come about through war or assassination, such as vaguely worded bets on when U.S. adversaries would be "out of office," which Kalshi and Polymarket had used to sidestep existing bans on war-related contracts.

War-related markets have also appeared elsewhere, including on Myriad, the prediction market operated by Decrypt's parent company DASTAN, where, where users traded on whether U.S. troops would enter Iran.

The bill's passage comes as prediction markets face growing legal pressure from states, which argue that some event contracts amount to unlicensed gambling. New York, for example, has sued Coinbase and Gemini over their prediction markets. The CFTC, which first sued three states in April to defend its authority over the markets, has now taken 12 states to court, most recently Louisiana.

The indicator tracks crypto market sentiment on a scale from zero, or maximum fear, to 100, or extreme greed, using market data rather than individual investor surveys. Its 2026 low was 5 on February 5, compared with a yearly high of 82 on August 26.

The selling went well beyond Bitcoin. Ethereum fell 4.24%, Solana lost 6.36%, BNB slid 4.38% and XRP declined 5.53% over the same period.

Spot fund withdrawals did not begin with Anthropic’s IPO. By Thursday, roughly $2.9 billion in U.S. Bitcoin ETF outflows for November, alongside higher Treasury yields and expectations of tighter Federal Reserve policy.

Anthropic made its Nasdaq debut Thursday at $111.50, up 34% from the $83 IPO price, before closing at $97.17. Friday brought another decline for the Claude developer, with the stock almost losing all the prime it gained after its IPO.

Friday’s market heatmap shows tech stock suffering in the middle of mostly green pastures. Nvidia down 3.78%, Broadcom is down 5.65%, AMD declining 5.06% and memory-chip maker Micron dropping 6.51%. Meta fell 2.21%, adding to pressure on companies spending heavily on AI infrastructure.

Other major stocks were doing better. Alphabet rose 2.81%, Microsoft gained 0.50% and Apple edged up 0.25%, while healthcare stocks Eli Lilly and Johnson & Johnson rose 1.21% and 1.78%, respectively. Walmart added 2.91% and Visa climbed 1.34%.

But it was not a uniform crash across the S&P 500. The tech-heavy Nasdaq was falling more sharply than the broader index, while pockets of healthcare, consumer, and financial shares remained green.

Bitcoin’s daily chart turned more decisively bearish after the break below $60,000. Its 50-day exponential moving average, or EMA, was below the 200-day EMA, a death-cross configuration, and spot prices were trading beneath both lines. That confirms sustained downside momentum rather than a brief intraday dip.

The relative strength index, or RSI, had fallen into the teens, far below the 30 threshold usually associated with oversold conditions. The average directional index, or ADX, climbed to 43.7, signaling a strong trend, with the chart’s directional readings favoring sellers. Oversold does not mean a bounce is guaranteed.

The longer-range chart puts the $60,000 breakdown in context. Bitcoin traded around this area several times in 2024 and returned to it during the mid-2026 retreat, making it a visible historical price zone as well as a round-number threshold.

With $60,000 giving way, the roughly $58,000–$59,000 support area near the mid-2026 lows comes into focus. If that zone fails, the next notable historical demand area is around $55,000–$56,000, followed by the $50,000 region marked by the deeper 2024 selloff.

For bulls, $60,000 is now the first level to hold. The next hurdle is $62,000–$62,500, followed by $65,000 and heavier resistance near $68,000–$70,000. A brief bounce back above $60,000 would not, by itself, reverse the bearish trend.

As of right now, Bitcoin had touched $59,300.13, confirming that sellers had pushed below the $60,000 support zone. The next test is whether that level becomes resistance on a rebound.

#5046. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, ECB President Christine Lagarde warned that differing rules could prevent Europe’s tokenized securities platforms from settling trades with one another, institutional investors’ holdings of US Bitcoin ETFs reached a record high in the third quarter and Anthropic shares surged on their Nasdaq debut before giving back much of their gains.

European Central Bank (ECB) President Christine Lagarde warned Friday that Europe needs common rules for tokenized securities before incompatible standards take hold across trading platforms.

Speaking at the Frankfurt European Banking Congress, Lagarde said blockchain technology lets firms issue, trade and settle securities on a single platform. The global markets for tokenized assets was worth about 23 billion euros in March, five times its size a year earlier, she said.

Europe could build a single market for those assets, Lagarde said, but if platforms adopt different rules as they grow, they may be unable to settle trades with one another. She said lawmakers have a role alongside the ECB.

“Common rules will be needed too, and those lie with legislators,” she said.

The Eurosystem launched Pontes in September, giving financial institutions a way to settle tokenized securities trades in central bank money. Earlier in November, the European Stability Mechanism used Pontes to settle a 1 billion euro (about $1.1 million) digital bond.

Through a second project, Appia, the Eurosystem is working with market participants on common standards for tokenized securities. It plans to publish a blueprint in 2028.

Institutional investment managers’ holdings of US Bitcoin exchange-traded funds climbed to a record high in the third quarter, as they reversed three straight quarters of net selling, according to regulatory filings.

The 13F filings, analyzed by Galaxy Research, show that the reporting companies added an equivalent of 49,229 Bitcoin (BTC) in the third quarter, outpacing the 47,054 Bitcoin they shed over the last three quarters combined. This lifted the reported total holdings to 356,491 BTC, the highest on record.

“The quarter’s accumulation was the work of a few dozen large institutions,” wrote Galaxy’s head of firmwide research Alex Thorn.

Quarterly 13F filings provide a point-in-time snapshot of US-listed securities held by reporting institutional investment managers. Thorn said half of the net addition of spot Bitcoin ETF shares came from advisors and wealth platforms.

Anthropic shares opened trading under the ticker ANT on the Nasdaq at $111.50 Thursday, about 34.3% above their initial public offering (IPO) price.

The Wall Street Journal had reported an expected opening of $110 minutes before trading began as fresh headlines poured in by the minute with ever-rising opening price predictions. The opening price values Anthropic at around $2 trillion on a fully diluted basis.

Anthropic had priced 980 million shares at $83 on Wednesday, above its initial $70-$78 range. The $81.3 billion IPO includes stock from existing shareholders. A separate $26 billion private placement brings the combined deal to $107.3 billion.

Anthropic’s IPO has blasted past SpaceX’s record $75 billion IPO in June, showing that the scale of investor demand for AI companies appears undeterred. SpaceX shares opened at $150, above their $135 offer price.

By the end of Thursday’s trading session, however, Anthropic shares had pared most of their early gains, closing at $97.17, up 17.07% from their IPO price, according to Yahoo Finance data.

#5044. DBS bets it can lose the deposit and keep the client

The Singapore bank earned a record $208 million in transaction services fees in the third quarter — now it’s helping companies turn deposits into stablecoins they can spend without it.

If a supplier wants USDC on a Sunday, an eligible company can now get Circle’s stablecoin directly from DBS.

That gives the bank a conversion option its client might otherwise take to a crypto exchange. If the treasurer needs to move dollars between the company’s Singapore and US accounts that weekend, DBS can also handle that through its new tokenized deposit link with JPMorgan.

Once the company holds USDC, its treasurer can pay the next invoice without involving DBS. For shareholders, the payoff depends on how often that treasurer comes back, what DBS earns when the money moves and how much operating cash the company leaves on deposit.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore issued 100 million Singapore dollars ($77.6 million) of four-week bills on Nov. 6, and the dealers paid in wholesale Singapore dollar central bank digital currency. Here DBS participated as a securities dealer. MAS plans to expand trials in 2027 to explore how institutions could use the bills as collateral.

DBS’s quarterly results put a figure on the business it has to defend. Transaction services generated a record 268 million Singapore dollars in fees in the third quarter, up 8% from a year earlier, before either November deal.

#5043. Google Gives Gemini Control of Mac Apps, But Its New AI Agent Comes With Risks

Google Gives Gemini Control of Mac Apps, But Its New AI Agent Comes With Risks

Google wants Gemini to do more than answer questions on your Mac.

The company launched a computer-use feature Thursday that lets its AI assistant open applications, navigate menus, fill out forms, and move information between local files and websites on a user's behalf.

“Starting today, computer use in the Gemini app for Mac helps you get multi-step tasks done across your local files, desktop apps, and the web,” Google announced. “Just describe what you need, and Gemini handles the clicking, typing, menus, and forms for you.”

The new capability runs in what Google calls an isolated background window, leaving the computer's main display and cursor available for the owner. Users can watch the agent's actions through a live preview inside the Gemini chat and interrupt a task if it goes off course.

Google's examples include extracting shipping details from PDF documents and entering them into online forms, editing batches of photos, and building presentations from dashboard data. The company also suggests researching prices and preparing resale listings based on pictures stored on a Mac.

Those jobs involve a different kind of AI interaction than asking a chatbot to write instructions. Gemini is supposed to operate the software itself, carrying out the clicks and keystrokes that would otherwise fall to the user.

The feature is rolling out in English in the United States to adult users with personal paid Google accounts. It requires an Apple Silicon Mac running macOS Sequoia 15.0 or later; work and school accounts are not supported at launch.

To activate it, users enable computer use in the Mac app, open Task mode, and prompt Gemini with @Computer use. According to Google's support documentation, the assistant asks for permission before controlling a new app, with options to deny access, allow it once, or allow it on an ongoing basis.

Users can create lists of allowed and blocked apps, monitor the virtual screen, and stop a task. Google says sensitive actions require confirmation before the agent proceeds, though an app marked "always allow" can be accessed again without another app-level prompt.

Computer use adds a local dimension to Google's agent strategy. Decrypt previously covered Gemini Spark, the company's cloud-based task agent, while competitors have developed desktop-control tools including OpenAI's Codex, Anthropic's Claude, and Kimi Work.

The separate window is also a practical distinction: an agent that commandeers the visible cursor can make a computer difficult to use during a long task. Google's approach lets the person keep working while Gemini handles another workflow.

A background window should not be confused with a guarantee that files or accounts are isolated from risk. Google's own documentation warns that the agent can reach active sessions in desktop applications, encounter authentication codes displayed on screen, and take actions that some apps cannot reverse.

In that case, any error is the user’s fault, not Google’s.

It also warns about prompt injection, an attack in which malicious instructions hidden in a document or website persuade an AI system to do something its user never requested. With permission to operate multiple apps, Gemini could encounter hostile content in one program and act on it in another.

Google offers optional on-demand file backups but says they cannot always be created. Where they work, the temporary copies remain available for 24 hours or until the next prompt, and some applications may prevent their content from being backed up at all.

The launch also comes three days after Apple introduced tighter access controls in the macOS 27.3 beta. Those changes give users more ways to restrict powerful permissions to specific apps or folders and to require fresh approval each time access is requested.

#5040. US Senate advances prediction market restrictions with DEATH BETS Act

The Senate-approved bill would ban prediction market contracts tied to war, assassination and death amid concerns over potential insider trading on military events.

The US Senate passed legislation Thursday that would prohibit federally regulated prediction markets from offering contracts tied to war, terrorism, assassination and events primarily determined by a person’s death, sending the bill to the House for consideration.

The DEATH BETS Act, introduced by Democratic Sen. Adam Schiff in March, passed by voice vote on Thursday after lawmakers adopted an amendment by unanimous consent. The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act to prohibit CFTC-registered exchanges from listing or clearing contracts tied to the specified events.

The legislation also covers contracts referencing US military operations and gives the Commodity Futures Trading Commission authority to identify similar activities that would fall under the ban. Contracts would also be prohibited if, at the time of listing, they are reasonably expected to be settled primarily based on an individual’s death.

The bill was first introduced in March amid insider-trading concerns after six Polymarket traders reportedly earned $1 million betting on the timing of US strikes against Iran. Several wagers were placed just hours before the first reported explosions in Tehran, raising suspicions that traders may have had access to nonpublic information.

When introducing the bill, Schiff warned that allowing traders to profit from violent events could encourage the misuse of classified information, threaten national security and create incentives for violence.

The bill now moves to the House of Representatives, where it must pass before being sent to President Donald Trump for his signature.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That money never belonged personally to Bankman-Fried. The bankruptcy estate sold the shares to repay creditors after FTX’s 2022 collapse.

The former CEO is serving a 25-year sentence for fraud after using FTX customer funds to make investments and cover losses, plus got hit with $11 billion in forfeiture—a fraction of what his single Anthropic investment would have returned today.

A company linked to Alameda, FTX’s sister company, committed $500 million to Anthropic in October 2021. The money converted into roughly 44.54 million preferred shares during the AI startup’s 2022 Series B, about 13.6% of the company.

Bankruptcy administrators sold the position in two batches in 2024 for approximately $1.34 billion. Buyers included an Abu Dhabi-linked fund and Jane Street, Bankman-Fried’s former employer, as filings showed.

At Thursday’s closing price that investment would have produced a total of nearly $43.28 billion—roughly 32 times what creditors’ administrators actually collected.

Sadly, that fortune is hypothetical. Later financing diluted FTX’s percentage ownership, the shares carried restrictions, and the estate needed liquidity to compensate customers who lost money when FTX went belly up. A judge approved the sale in February 2024.

Alameda’s $1.15 billion Genesis Digital investment would imply around $290 million at a 2026 transaction valuation. By contrast, $40 million in drone maker BRINC could mark near $109 million at a funding estimate, and $20 million in Anchorage Digital about $31 million at its 2026 valuation.

A 461-position reconstruction puts $5.32 billion in total investments at roughly $59.5 billion on today’s valuation for those companies and assets—about $54.2 billion in hypothetical gains. The model assumes all investments were held and imputes values for illiquid positions.

That’s good for a 1,018% return, and proof SBF knew how to pick his bets. But even the best investments can't justify gambling with other people's money without their consent. He made that bet, too—and lost.

#5037. Bitcoin falls below $60K for first time since July amid broader market selloff

Bitcoin fell nearly 6% as US stocks declined, with the Nasdaq dropping more than 2% and Anthropic shares losing almost 10%.

Bitcoin fell below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since July, extending its recent losses amid a broader selloff in technology stocks and other risk assets.

Bitcoin (BTC) dropped as low as $58,811 before recovering slightly to trade around $59,503, down 5.6% over the past 24 hours as of 16:52 UTC on Friday, according to CoinGecko data. The cryptocurrency has fallen 13.8% over the past week and nearly 30% over the past month.

The decline came amid a broader selloff in US equities. The Nasdaq Composite fell 2.73%, while the S&P 500 declined 1.44% as of approximately 16:27 UTC on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

Shares of Anthropic (ANT), which made its Nasdaq debut on Thursday, fell 9.92% to $87.54 as of 16:27 UTC, amid concerns over valuations and spending in the artificial intelligence sector.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds recorded $582.3 million in net outflows on Thursday, up from $306.5 million the previous day, according to Farside Investors. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) accounted for $541.1 million of Thursday’s withdrawals.

Bitcoin’s decline appears to be driven more by the broader selloff in artificial intelligence stocks than by problems specific to the cryptocurrency market, according to James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares.

Butterfill told Cointelegraph that Bitcoin has increasingly traded alongside other liquidity-sensitive growth assets, with deteriorating sentiment toward AI stocks spilling into crypto as investors reduce risk. The prospect of interest rates remaining elevated has made the broader selloff more severe, he added.

The Federal Reserve held its benchmark interest rate at 3.75% to 4.00% in October, following a quarter-point increase in September. Traders on Polymarket were pricing in a 94% chance of another rate hike by the Fed’s next policy decision on Dec. 9, as of 17:19 UTC on Friday.

Polymarket traders price in 94% chance of another Fed rate hike in 2026. Source: Polymarket

Despite Bitcoin falling below $60,000, Butterfill said the decline has not fundamentally changed his medium-term outlook.

Butterfill said Bitcoin could face further short-term losses if the AI selloff deepens, but should benefit relatively quickly if technology stocks stabilize.

Alexander Blume, founder and CEO of digital asset investment firm Two Prime, told Cointelegraph that macroeconomic instability, interest rates, inflation and a lack of Bitcoin-specific catalysts were contributing to the decline.

However, Blume suggested that selling pressure may be nearing an end.

#5035. Sky Protocol sees $2 billion in stablecoin withdrawals, disputes illicit fund claims

Sky says its USDS stablecoin held its $1 value despite more than $2 billion in withdrawals, as it disputes a report linking the protocol to illicit activity.

Sky Protocol processed more than $2 billion in stablecoin redemptions on Thursday, the same day a CoinDesk investigation linked $518 million in transactions through the protocol to illicit activity.

The withdrawals involved users exchanging Sky’s USDS stablecoin for Circle’s USDC through its Peg Stability Module (PSM), a system that allows users to swap the tokens at a fixed rate.

Sky, which has a total value locked (TVL) of $2.3 billion, said on Friday in a statement that USDS maintained its $1 peg throughout the withdrawals. According to the protocol, the amount of USDC held in the PSM never fell below $800 million and had climbed back above $1.1 billion by Friday.

Sky also challenged the investigation, pointing to a review by Block Analitica, which notably serves as a risk adviser to the Sky ecosystem.

The withdrawals came amid concerns raised by the investigation that Circle could freeze USDC held by Sky, potentially making it harder for users to redeem their stablecoins.

CoinDesk’s Nov. 19 report looked at research by blockchain analytics firm Inca Digital, which identified $518 million in transactions involving wallets linked to hackers, sanctioned entities, and other threat actors over a span of five years.

Of that amount, around $101.9 million was linked to North Korean actors, including wallets connected to the February 2025 Bybit hack.

Meanwhile, Block Analitica disputed some parts of the investigation, saying that the $518 million represented less than 0.06% of the more than $960 billion processed through Sky’s peg stability modules.

The firm said about two-thirds of the flagged activity occurred between 2021 and mid-2024 – before Sky introduced its newer LitePSM system.

Block Analitica also questioned the reported links to North Korean threat actors, stating that some wallets were several transactions removed from known addresses and may have belonged to unrelated users.

The firm added that most of the flagged transactions passed through trading services such as 1inch, ParaSwap and MetaMask Swaps, which automatically choose where to route trades.

The large withdrawals also came amid a sharp drop in the number of USDS tokens in circulation. The supply fell nearly 40% in one day to about 3.2 billion tokens, according to DefiLlama data. However, the stablecoin maintained its $1 value despite the decline.

CoinDesk reported that a wallet linked to crypto exchange Poloniex accounted for a large share of the withdrawals, although it wasn’t clear whether the exchange itself was behind the transactions.

The combined circulating supply of Sky’s USDS and DAI stablecoins fell to $6.11 billion as of Nov. 20, down 37.8% over the past 30 days. Source: Sky Ecosystem Financial Insights

Sky said it held about $9.3 billion in assets backing its stablecoins as of Friday, compared with around $6.11 billion in USDS and DAI in circulation, according to its financial dashboard. The dashboard also showed about $93 million in reserves to help cover potential losses.

Cointelegraph reached out to the Sky Frontier Foundation for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#5033. Wrapped Monero token crashes 99.8% amid suspected $13.5M exploit

Bitquery traced $13.5 million in suspicious sales of the wrapped Monero token, as its operator blamed a security breach and promised to reimburse affected traders.

A wrapped version of Monero (XMR) plunged more than 99% on Hyperliquid on Friday after a wallet that received tokens from the deployer account sold approximately $13.5 million worth of the asset, according to blockchain analytics firm Bitquery.

The token, known as XMR1, fell from around $500 to $1 in roughly 30 minutes, a 99.8% decline, according to Bitquery. Wrapped tokens are designed to represent other cryptocurrencies on different blockchains, allowing them to be traded outside their native networks. The collapse affected XMR1 rather than Monero’s native cryptocurrency.

According to Bitquery’s onchain analysis, the wallet received 5.2 million XMR1 tokens from the deployer account before selling approximately $13.5 million worth of the asset on Friday.

The firm traced $13.28 million in USDC transferred to Arbitrum, where $13.22 million was exchanged for 7,467 Ether (ETH) through CoW Protocol. Most of the ETH was subsequently bridged to Ethereum.

Bitquery also found that proceeds from four hacks earlier in November, involving Across Protocol, Kava, Lighter and Safe, had been used to purchase approximately $6.6 million worth of XMR1, apparently as a route to native Monero. The firm said it found no onchain evidence linking those incidents to Friday’s suspected exploit.

Value of XMR1 purchases linked to funds from four November hacks. Source: Bitquery

Bitquery said its onchain investigation could not establish how the deployer account’s private key was obtained or who controlled it during the transfers, leaving the circumstances of the suspected compromise unconfirmed.

Related: Binance hacker moves nearly $600M in ETH, routes funds through THORChain, Chainflip

PerpetualCow, the pseudonymous operator of Wagyu, the service behind XMR1, attributed the collapse to an alleged breach of hosting provider 1984, claiming that customer data and internal server files had been exposed.

According to PerpetualCow, the XMR1 deployer key had been stored on a server that was subsequently deleted, but the hosting provider allegedly retained logs and files associated with it.

PerpetualCow also pledged to personally reimburse affected traders and said Wagyu would relaunch at a later date, although no timeline was provided.

Cointelegraph contacted the operator for additional details about the suspected breach and reimbursement plans but had not received a response by publication.

#5029. After Launching McGee & Co., Shea McGee Shares 8 Lessons for Opening a Store

My husband Syd and I are both lovers of retail, and have always known one day we’d have a McGee & Co. shop of our own. Over the years, we’ve spent a lot of time jotting down ideas for our own store. Our new flagship is a repository for all that knowledge that we’ve been squirreling away—but it also taught us a few lessons of its own.

Speaking to you now, on the other side of its debut, there are some things we only learned through actually doing. From the importance of developing a training manual to the do-or-die value of a soft launch, here are eight hard-won learnings from our McGee & Co. store launch.

It was important to us that the shop feel like the homes we create for our clients—layered and collected, with a variety of patterns and objects for your eye to explore. Despite the fact that the dollars-per-square-foot model is the traditional method of conceiving an in-store assortment, it was more important for us to remain true to ourselves with this first location: we’re a designer-led brand, not a merchant-led one.

“I’m getting to see our products through new eyes—and I was the one that concepted them!” says Shea. “When you see them in a store setting, pieces can have a lot more detail than you can see online.”

We started by coming up with the sections we envisioned, like a kids’ area, a kitchen zone, and so on. Then we created planograms to see how much we could actually fit, and reconciled with our merchandising team to see what made business sense. Sometimes, that meant pivoting from the early vision. A lighting gallery we had planned, for instance, ended up shifting: We loved the idea, but when we looked at the percentage of lighting to the rest of our sales, we could see clearly that it was a smarter business decision to use this space to showcase more furniture. Instead of the sprawling lighting gallery we had imagined, we created a moody lounge area that can flex to showcase different pieces.

We don’t think small: Our aspirations are to grow nationwide. But however much we scale, it’s critical that we not lose the warm, personal feeling that comes from a standalone store. As McGee & Co. grows its brick-and-mortar operation, that’s one of my biggest goals. And there’s another layer here, too: Being a personality-led business, the team members in our store aren’t just representing our brand—they’re representing me, for better or worse. The design world can sometimes feel closed-off and unapproachable, and it’s always been the McGee mantra—in our content, in our personality on social media, in how we work with clients—to be warm and accessible. Now that attitude needs to trickle down to our store associates, too.

Before we opened, I spent months developing a comprehensive training guide for our associates. It’s essentially Design 101, McGee-style, covering everything from how many inches I place a rug away from a wall, to how to pair and arrange accent tables, to how to layer bedding. We cover every level of the decorating process. Thank goodness for AI—it’s been a lifesaver: I’ll narrate as we go on our walk-throughs, and use those notes as the basis for the training manual. And it’s not a one-and-done deal. We’ll continue to add to this document as we evolve and grow.

Every issue, every era. Explore 100+ years of the Architectural Digest Archive, an unparalleled digital resource for research and inspiration. Start Searching

Having that Design 101 established is a first step, but how do you ensure that the team is really internalizing the lessons? For us, the answer was checkpoints. As our design associates go through training and familiarize themselves with our ways of doing things, we hold check-ins with them to make sure that they’re absorbing our ways of operating. It’s the exact same as we do for the junior interior designers at Studio McGee: The checkpoints allow us to build up mutual confidence, with the aim for them to eventually fly on their own.

Shea plans to have the studio’s senior-level designers hosting regular check-ins with the shop’s design associates.

Personally dropping in myself can serve as another kind of checkpoint, too. With the flagship local, I’m able to stop by regularly in these early days to make sure that our standards are upheld, that our visuals are tight, and the operation is humming. That won’t work for us in every location, but it’s certainly a perk of opening up in our own hometown.

As much as I love a good plan, there’s nothing like real-time feedback to show you what’s working and what’s not. Which is why we kicked off our flagship back in September with a whisper and not a bang. We did a very soft launch with no announcement, no social post. We just opened the doors. As folks wandered in, we learned, and we tweaked. And two weeks later, we opened officially. That soft-launch period was vital for us in getting the kinks out.

One benefit of having had an ecommerce business for so long is that we have rich data on exactly where our customers are. That means that from day one, we already know where we need to open next. While launching in Salt Lake City was a logical place for our flagship, we’re already thinking ahead to how we’ll expand to some of our other biggest markets, like Texas and California.

Located in Salt Lake City’s Trolley Square, the “shop,” as Shea calls it, is 7,500 square feet.

We want to capitalize on the fact that the retail-ecommerce loop is an advantageous cycle: Where you have a retail store, your ecommerce sales lift. People who are aware of your product can go see it in person, give it a sit test, think about it, and then finish the sale online. In store, we can educate them, give them styling suggestions, and show off the quality of the product in ways we can’t online. Even for us, we’re discovering new things about our assortment by merchandising it in the store—we’re seeing it with fresh eyes, and we know our customers will too.

Aside from the obvious metric of profitability, to me, success in these early days means nailing down our systems and processes. It’s not the most glamorous side of this business, but it’s a must. I want to be able to say that we know what it takes to run a store and to make the shopper experience in it as smooth as possible. For instance, I want customers to know how to get to our free design services, and to have a great experience using them. If we can do that, we know we’re on our way to some of our loftier goals, too.

I’m not under any illusions here: This first store is a baptism by fire. Not only are we opening a flagship and planting seeds for our future locations—we’re also doing it in Q4, right before the holidays. But my hope is that at the end of it, we can catch our breath and take a moment to reflect on what worked, what didn’t, and then get to work fixing it. In this business, honesty with yourself is the only way up.

“The kitchen is a working kitchen,” says Shea, who envisions cooking demos and community events enlivening the space.

#5026. How to Open a Retail Store, Win Customers, and Build a Business That Lasts

Even in the age of one-click shopping, opening an actual brick-and-mortar retail store is a dream for many designers. There’s something almost romantic about creating an entire environment where customers can sit on the sofas, feel the fabrics, or pick up a last-minute gift on their way to a party. It’s an opportunity to bring your brand to life and connect with customers in a way a website never can.

But opening a store requires a big leap of faith. Even if you have a loyal customer base, there’s something different about building a store. You have to think about everything from foot traffic to merchandising to hiring staff, and much more.

These are questions I’ve been wrestling with myself. Almost two months ago, after years of nurturing a thriving e-commerce business, my husband Syd and I unveiled our McGee & Co. flagship in our homebase of Salt Lake City. This project has been a goal of ours for a long time. We’ve been collecting ideas for years to ensure that whenever it opened, our physical retail operation would hold up to the high standards of service and design that we expect from every other part of our business. This project has had me thinking about retail at every hour of the day, mulling over some big questions... How do we translate a digital brand into a physical space for selling? How do we nurture customers and ensure they return? And how do we map our knowledge so that we can repeat our successes as we scale?

This guide, produced with our friends at AD PRO, distills some of the wisdom from our own experiences, as well as from a few insiders elsewhere in the industry. I’ll share some of the thinking and practical decisions behind our shop, from our merchandising strategy to how we recruited and trained our employees.

Next, AD PRO contributor Lila Allen speaks to me and my team, as well as professionals from retailers like Lawson-Fenning, Il Buco Vita, Tailor-Made Textiles, and ABC Carpet & Home, about how they cultivate customer loyalty over the long term. When access to product and knowledge is seemingly unlimited across the internet, we all agree: Earning trust and becoming a destination for discovery takes a lot of care. But it pays off. Relationships might be the most precious resource we have.

Then, contributor Margaret Borden catches up with the creatives behind some of this year’s buzziest shops—from a jewel-box fashion boutique in Paris to a bright, inviting kitchen and home goods shop in New York City—to glean their merchandising tips and tricks.

Whether you’re a designer thinking about hanging your own retail shingle, or you’re simply looking for a fresh perspective on the landscape of selling in 2026 and beyond, I hope this report will give you plenty of inspiration for the year ahead.

Our Salt Lake City flagship is a baptism by fire—but we wouldn’t have it any other way. Here’s how we planned our first retail location for profitability, growth, and an unmistakably “McGee” experience —As Told to Lila Allen

In the age of instant gratification and easy comparison shopping, relationships matter more than ever. Here’s how retail pros across the country are building them—and keeping them —By Lila Allen

How do you design a store enticing enough to lure people off their sofas and through the door? The creatives behind four of 2026’s buzziest shops reveal their strategies —By Margaret Borden
